Majid Pakdel received his Master’s degree in Electrical Power Engineering from Isfahan University 
of Technology, Iran, in 2007, his PhD in Electrical Power Engineering from the University of 
Zanjan in 2018, and his second Master’s degree in Computer Engineering – Artificial Intelligence 
and Robotics from Malek Ashtar University of Technology, Tehran, in 2023. He has published over 
20 papers and books in the fields of electrical engineering and computer science.
About the Author
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. --- Page 13 ---
As technology continues to evolve at an unprecedented pace, the ability to effectively design and 
implement digital systems has become increasingly essential. Field-programmable gate arrays 
(FPGAs) represent a pivotal advancement in this domain, providing unparalleled flexibility and 
performance for a wide variety of applications – ranging from embedded systems to high-speed 
communications and beyond. The advent of sophisticated design environments, such as Vivado 
from Xilinx, has opened new avenues for educators, engineers, and hobbyists alike to harness the 
power of these versatile devices. This book, “ Fundamentals of VHDL for FPGA Programming Using 
Vivado,” serves as a comprehensive introduction to the fundamental concepts and practical skills 
necessary for designing digital circuits with VHDL (VHSIC hardware description language) and 
implementing them on FPGA platforms. My goal is to equip readers with the knowledge required 
to navigate the complexities of VHDL programming and Vivado design flows, enabling them to 
bring their innovative ideas to life. Geared toward both beginners and those with some experi-
ence in digital design, this text begins with an overview of VHDL’s syntax and semantics, empha-
sizing practical applications and real-world examples. Subsequent chapters delve deeper into 
the core principles of digital design, exploring topics such as simulation, synthesis, testbenches, 
and optimization techniques. The hands-on tutorials will guide readers step-by-step through the 
design process using Vivado, providing them with the confidence and competence to tackle their 
own projects. Throughout the book, I aim to balance theoretical knowledge with practical appli-
cation. The provided examples, projects, and exercises are designed to reinforce learning, foster 
critical thinking, and encourage creativity in design. Furthermore, I address common challenges 
faced by designers and offer strategies and solutions drawn from my own experiences in the field.  
I believe that mastering VHDL and FPGA design is not just about understanding concepts and 
syntax, but also about cultivating the ability to solve problems and innovate. Whether you are 
a student, an aspiring engineer, or a seasoned professional looking to refresh your skills, I hope 
that this book will serve as a valuable resource on your journey into the exciting world of digital 
design. In conclusion, I would like to extend my gratitude to my family, colleagues, and fellow 
educators for their unwavering support and encouragement throughout the writing process. I also 
want to acknowledge the vibrant community of learners and practitioners who continue to inspire 
me with their passion for technology and creativity. Welcome to the world of VHDL and FPGA 
programming. Let the adventure begin!
Please note that all the figures in the book are printed in grayscale; however, they are reproduced 
in color on the book’s website – please visit www.wiley.com/go/pakdelVHDL.

The website includes:
	 •	 MATLAB 	and 	Simulink 	Models:  This directory contains all the project files and models 
referenced in the book, particularly for chapters covering HDL Coder and Model-Based 
Design. These files allow readers to simulate systems in Simulink and generate the VHDL 
code discussed in the text.
	 •	 Supplemental 	Chapters 	19, 	20, 	and 	21:  These three advanced chapters are provided in their 
entirety as downloadable PDFs. They extend the book's coverage into critical areas such as 
deploying controller design on FPGA, combinational and sequential logic design, and the 
complete VHDL implementation of an 8-bit CPU, offering readers significant additional 
technical depth.
	 •	 Vivado 	Codes:  This is a complete collection of all VHDL source files, test benches, and XDC 
constraint files for every project in the book. Readers can use these codes to run their own 
simulations, syntheses, and implement the designs on an FPGA board without needing to 
type the code manually.

1.1   Introduction
In this book, we are going to use the Vivado tool from Xilinx, which is their newest tool that we 
will be using to develop our projects on the A7 development board. What is this book about? 
Well, this book focuses on two main topics: VHDL and FPGA development. There is a lot to 
the VHDL language, so what we’re going to do is go through and explain specific parts of the 
VHDL language, specifically how it relates to FPGA development. You can use VHDL for a 
variety of things, but in this book, we’re going to focus on the part of the shell that’s used to 
implement a design on an FPGA. This includes different types of digital logic or anything else 
you can think of that can be done digitally – we’re going to put it on the FPGA. We’re going to 
show you how to do just that. Thus, in this book, we’re going to explain how to use Vivado, the 
software development kit for Xilinx, and how to develop on the A7 development board, which 
is a Xilinx development board. Now, the layout of this book is such that we are going to cover 
the basics of Vivado, and then work on a project. This means you will get some theory and an 
understanding of how different parts of the Vivado work. After that, we’ll go ahead and do a 
project and then we’ll cover more theory in another project. So, the idea is that once you come 
to a project, at that point, you should have learned everything you need to know to complete it 
successfully. As we go through the different theories and explain them, we will walk through 
and show how they relate specifically to the next project in the book as they step down. So, all 
of the different projects that we’re working with include the following: we have a buttons and 
LEDs project, where we are going to press a button on our FPGA board, which will light up an 
LED. The whole idea behind this is to show you how to do the basics – how to take inputs and 
generate outputs. You’re going to have to use the buttons as input and display the output on 
LEDs. Then we have a blinking LED project, where we’re going to show you how to create a 
timer, specifically a counter, which control how LED blinks on and off at a specified interval. 
Then we have the brightness project, where we are going to vary the brightness of the LEDs. If 
you have the Arty A7 board, you will have both standard LEDs and an RGB (red–green–blue) 
LED. In this project, we are going to vary the brightness using pulse width modulation. We’re 
going to explain how pulse width modulation works, how to implement it in VHDL, and then 
deploy it on the development board to vary the brightness of the LEDs on the board.
We then want to show you the UART demonstration project, which is a very basic introduction to 
using UART on your development board. Both boards have a UART port, and this is going to show 
you how we can take information from the actual developer board and display it on a computer. Well, 
you can use UART to take that information displayed on the computer. So, we’re going to show you

how to do that. Then, we have the other UART IO project, where we will take information just like we 
did in UART demonstration project. But take it a step further, where we are going to take inputs in, 
so we’ll type in commands on our computer to have certain actions occur on the development board, 
such as checking the input to tell us which buttons are pushed or which switches are high, and also 
sending a command to tell it to light up certain LEDs. We have an ADC processing project, where 
we are going to interface the board with a temperature sensor to demonstrate the analog-to-digital 
conversion. We’re going to vary the temperature of the sensor to show how it responds – when the 
sensor gets colder, you’ll see the temperature decrease, and when it gets hotter, the temperature will 
increase. We’re going to take that temperature, convert it into both Celsius and Fahrenheit, and then 
display the values on the computer screen so that you can see the actual ambient temperature or the 
temperature that a sensor is indicating. We have an SPI interface project. This project is going to be 
working only with the Arty A7 development board, and what we’ll do is we are going to take and store 
information on the onboard SPI flash. This is because these are volatile memories, which means that 
when you program the package and then power it off, the configurations are lost when it is powered 
back on. So, we’re going to show you how to take information and store it on the external flash so that 
when you power it back on and reprogram it, you can pull that information from the external flash. 
So, we’re going to work on it and show you how to do the interface between an FPGA and external 
flash using the SPI interface. Who’s this course for? This is for anyone who has no previous FPGA or 
VHDL design experience and wants to understand how it’s used as well as a design from scratch on 
FPGA. So, whether you’re an engineer, student, or hobbyist, this course is specifically designed for you 
to show you how to do this type of project and use FPGA. Now that you know what to expect from this 
book, let’s get started.
1.2   Introduction to VHDL
In this section, we’re going to introduce and briefly explain what VHDL is. Well, it’s a hardware 
descriptive language, and it’s similar to Verilog. If you have ever heard of Verilog, they are not 
one and the same. They’re two different languages, but they’re doing the same thing. We’re 
describing hardware. It’s a hardware descriptive language, hence the name VHDL, and what 
that means is we’re using the VHDL to describe a hardware circuit. We’re not actually writing 
software, so you kind of have to wrap your mind around the idea. When you’re working with 
the VHDL, you’re not writing software. It’s not like C, Java, or MATLAB, where everything you 
write happens sequentially. It’s not that at all. What we’re doing is describing how we want a 
digital hardware circuit to operate, so we can think of it as architecting a digital schematic. 
So, you’ll say, OK, we want the specific AND gate or we want a comparator and we want to 
drive some sort of digital; whatever type of digital thing you’re wanting to do, you implement 
it and describe it in detail, and that’s the whole idea behind it. The big advantage of using 
VHDL is that it’s very portable. So, if we have VHDL that we are running on Xilinx or Altera 
device, we can take that same VHDL out, as long as we’re not using specific components on 
the actual device, such as memory, which means we have to change a little bit to use memory 
on the other device. We can take this one feature design and move it around a different logic 
device on FPGA, a couple or any other type of logic device you might be using. So, that’s one of 
the big advantages of using the VHDL – you can build up a library so that if you’re creating a 
project with an Altera device, you can use those same designs. When you create a project on a 
Xilinx device, you can use almost all of those same designs when designing a device. That’s the 
whole idea – it’s very portable. The whole reason that VHDL was developed is that designing 
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. 1.2 Introduction to VHDL
 3
digital hardware used to take a long time. If you wanted to design something digital, you had 
to place new chips on a board, which requires a new board layout, a new schematic, and long 
turnaround times. If you can just place one chip in the middle that’s implementing all of this 
functionality and you want to change it, such as your algorithm changes or you find a small bug 
with an FPGA and VDL, you can then modify your VHDL circuitry and reload it on the FPGA 
and now your design change has taken place without an actual board layout change. So, that’s 
one of the major advantages of using VHDL, or as you call it, configurable hardware, and the 
important thing we understand is it’s not software. You’re not writing software.
Some of the constructs, syntax, and structures are very similar, and they operate in the same 
manner. But you must understand that you’re describing hardware and that’s the overall concept. 
What you’re doing when you’re working with the VHDL is you’re not actually writing a software 
program; you’re describing a hardware circuit. Where is VHDL actually used? So, it’s used in sev -
eral different ways, and probably the most common is that it’s used to configure – or we’ll call it 
program, but you’re not necessarily programming – you’re configuring the circuit. CPLDs and 
FPGAs are types of configurable hardware devices, and CPLDs and FPGAs are similar in a lot 
of aspects. But typically, your FPGAs are larger devices, and they have SRAM-like base, whereas 
your CPLDs might be like EPROM or flash-based. So, the FPGA doesn’t contain its configuration. 
When you power it off, you’ll typically have to store that configuration on a memory chip offboard 
the FPGA. So, any power on it that sucks in that configuration or as in this book here, we’re going 
to just program the JTAG. So, once your FPGA is powered up, you program and/or configure it. 
Then you’ll notice that when you remove the power and power it back on, it loses its configura -
tion. That’s because we just loaded it directly onto the FPGA, which is just RAM memory. So, as 
soon as your power is down, it loses all the configurations. Another reason you can use VHDL is 
to simulate digital circuits. So, if you had a board, sort of a board layout you’re working on, and 
you’ve got a section with several components where everything they’re doing is digital, you can 
use VHDL to describe each one of those chips and how they interact with each other so that you 
can simulate it on your computer. Especially if you have an analog and digital interaction, you can 
say, “OK, we’re going to stimulate my digital FPGA and we’ll use PSpice to simulate my analog 
and verify that the interaction is occurring correctly.” Another thing you can use VHDL for is to 
create an ASIC (application-specific integrated circuit). For example, if you’re working with a 
microprocessor or an ASIC that handles Ethernet communications, a transceiver, or any similar 
type of ICs (integrated circuits), VHDL can be used to describe its hardware. If you work with 
circuit boards or develop any type of circuits in electrical engineering, you’ll work with ASICs. 
When they’re developing these, they’ll use VHDL to describe the hardware that goes inside these 
ASICs, and we can use that to compile it and internally understand where they need to place all 
the gates for the actual chip to do the functionality you’re describing. Finally, we’re going to under -
stand how we are going to use VHDL. Therefore, in this book, we are going to use the VHDL to 
configure programs on FPGA. Thus, we have these development boards and they’ve got different 
peripherals on them, including buttons, switches, LEDs, and communication ports, and we are 
going to use VHDL to interface these different ports to each other and just get an understanding 
of how VHDL works and how we can use it on these FPGAs. So, we’ll be using these development 
boards. However, the overall idea is to use VHDL to describe a circuit inside the FPGA and we’ll 
explain how these peripherals interact with each other. Therefore, this is just a brief introduction 
and explanation of the VHDL.

2.1   Introduction
Vivado is a design suite developed by Xilinx, primarily used for the synthesis and analysis of 
hardware description language (HDL) designs. It is a comprehensive toolset designed for the 
development of Xilinx’s field-programmable gate array (FPGA) and system-on-chip (SoC) devices. 
Here are some key features and components of Vivado:
	 1.	Design Entry: Supports VHDL, Verilog, and SystemVerilog for writing and managing design 
code. It also offers block diagram entry and high-level synthesis (HLS) from C, C++, and 
SystemC.
	 2.	Synthesis: Converts high-level design descriptions into a gate-level representation optimized 
for Xilinx FPGAs.
	 3.	Implementation: Includes processes like translation, mapping, placing, and routing of the 
design onto the FPGA.
	 4.	Simulation: Provides integrated simulation capabilities for verifying the functional correctness 
of designs.
	 5.	Analysis and Debugging: Offers various tools for analyzing and debugging the design, 
including timing analysis, power analysis, and on-chip debugging tools like the integrated 
logic analyzer.
	 6.	Intellectual Property (IP) Integrator: Facilitates the integration and configuration of IP cores.
	 7.	Design Environment: Provides a graphical user interface (GUI)-based environment as well as 
scripting capabilities for automation using Tcl.
	 8.	Device Support: Continuously updated to support the latest Xilinx devices, including the latest 
UltraScale, UltraScale+, and Versal families.
Vivado replaces the older ISE Design Suite and offers enhanced performance, capabilities, and 
support for larger and more complex designs.

3.1   Introduction
VHSIC Hardware Description Language (VHDL) is a hardware description language used in 
electronic design automation to describe the behavior and structure of electronic systems. The 
design structure in VHDL is hierarchical and modular, allowing designers to build complex sys -
tems by combining smaller, reusable components. The key elements of VHDL design structure are 
entities, architectures, configurations, and libraries/packages.
3.2   The VHDL Keywords
In this section, we will discuss the VHDL keywords and syntax. Therefore, we’re going to explain the 
commonly used VHDL syntax and keywords. In the VHDL language, there are many different key -
words and syntax that you need to perform certain actions. However, we’re only going to cover the 
ones that you’ll need to complete the projects in this book. So, the first keyword we’re going to cover 
is “comment.” A comment is similarly used as any other type of programming language. You’ll put 
this in your actual code to kind of guide you to explain what’s happening. Often, at the beginning of 
your VHDL code, you’ll have a bunch of comments that explain who wrote the code, its version, or 
any other type of notes. A comment is denoted by a dash-dash (--) in the VHDL. The next keyword is 
“library,” which is used to designate the specific library you’re going to use in VHDL. Different libraries 
have different packages, and these packages provide different functionalities, such as functions, data 
types, and different operations, whether it’s logical or math operations. If you’re a C programmer or an 
embedded type of programmer, this is akin to a header file or a .h type of file. We also use a keyword, 
which is spelled out like it sounds “use,” which makes a package available. So, we want to define a 
library and the packages we want to use in that library, for example, the following codes.
--This is a comment
library IEEE;
use IEEE.STD_LOGIC_1164.all;
The next keyword is “entity.” In VHDL, an entity defines the inputs and outputs of your design. So, if 
you think of VHDL as a shell, the outside represents the entity, while the internal structure is defined 
by another keyword we are going to cover, “architecture.” So, you have your entity that defines the 
inputs and outputs, whereas the architecture defines what’s going on inside your design. The keyword 
is defined by spelling out “architecture,” for example, consider the following codes.
3
The VHDL Design Structure
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

entity example is
end example
architecture test of example
The next one is “generic,” which allows you to pass certain parameters into your design without 
actually having to define them as an input or output – it’s a generic parameter. For example, if you 
want to have an 8-bit, 16-bit, or 32-bit adder, you could have a generic parameter that you call the 
size of the adder and give it a number, and you don’t have to define it as an input or output; for 
example, consider the following code.
entity example is
generic(
);
end example
The next keyword is “port,” which defines the interface. Inside the entity, you have a port that 
defines whether it’s an input (in), an output (out), or an input/output (inout), which means that it 
can be an input or an output, allowing data to be sent and received on that same line. The next key -
word is “downto,” which is used to define a range. If you have a specific data type, which we will 
cover later, you can use “downto” to say, OK, we have a data type that goes from 31 down to 0 and 
that just fills all the gaps in between. So, that means you have 31, 30, 29, 28, 27 all the way down to 0; 
for example, the following code.
entity example is
Port (
     My_input : in std_logic;
     My_Output : out std_logic_vector(31 downto 0);
);
end example
Just as it sounds, if you want to go from 7 down to 0, use the “downto” keyword. The key -
words “begin” and “end” are used as a begin–end pair, which are used kind of all over the 
VHDL language, when defining your architecture, you use a begin–end pair to specify the 
beginning and end of the architecture’s logic. Similarly, if you’re using an if statement, a loop, 
or any other type of statement, you’ll say, OK, if this happens again and then it’ll look for the 
end. So, if you have a begin–end pair as opposed to other languages, and if you’re commonly 
working with C or any other type of language, you’ll have curly braces ({}). Instead of that, we 
have a begin–end pair in VHDL. The next keyword is “end,” and like we said before, you have 
“begin,” and you have “end,” and this just specifies the end of your “begin–end” pair, which are 
used in various ways. The next keyword here is “map,” which is used to map parameters. So, if 
you have multiple designs inside one VHDL, you might have various files, and we’ll do what 
we call a component instantiation. This is where you’ll map certain parameters. You’ll say, OK, 
the inputs from a top-level entity are mapped to different inputs on my components, which we 
have instantiated inside our design. We’ll cover this much later in much greater detail, showing 
you how to do a component instantiation, how to map the certain inputs to the other inputs of 
your other design, and much more. Another important keyword is signal assignment, which 
is represented by the arrow equals, and this is assigned as a signal assignment. So, if we have 
signal A and signal B, and we’re assigning about A equal to the value of B, then you can think of 
it as similar to working with a circuit, it’s like taking a wire and going from A to B, and B is now 
equal to A. It’s like connecting two points with a wire. Therefore, we’re assigning the signal B, 
the value of A, as shown in the following codes.

3.3   The VHDL Libraries
This section is about the VHDL packages and libraries. In this section, we’re going to tell you 
about certain packages or the most commonly used ones in the IEEE library. IEEE stands for 
the Institute of Electronics and Electrical Engineers, and they’re the ones who manage the pack -
ages we’ll be using in our project, individual language, or in the IEEE library. There are quite a 
few different packages, but we’re going to cover the most common ones, specifically the ones 
that you’ll need to complete all the projects in this book. These libraries or packages include the 
IEEE standard logic 1164, the IEEE math real, and the IEEE numeric standard packages. So, 
for the first one, the IEEE 1164, it has some key features. So, we’ll review some of the key fea -
tures for each library, and then we’ll show you where to go to actually see all of the definitions 
and the actual code behind the library. The logical operations that are supported in the standard 
logic 1164 library include AND, OR, NAND, NOR, and all the basic logic gate operations. So, by 
using this package, it allows us to do all of these basic Boolean algebra and different logic opera -
tions. The data types it supports are standard logic and standard logic vector. These are the most 
common and basic data types used in VHDL, which we’ll cover later in the data type section. The 
third most important feature of the standard logic 1164 library is the functions it supports, such as 
rising edge and falling edge. These functions work when you have a process. You’ll use the rising 
edge function to trigger that process on the rising edge of a signal, and conversely, with the falling 
edge function, when you have a process and you call the falling edge, you’ll trigger that process on 
the falling edge of that signal. The next library we’re going to talk about a little bit is the IEEE math 
real library. Some key library features are that it basically supports some mathematical constants 
and operations. We won’t use a whole lot of the math real library, but the biggest functions that 
we’re going to use from here are the ceiling function and the floor function. So, with the ceiling 
function, you give it a possible number, and it takes you to the nearest integer that represents that 
number. The floor function does the opposite: you give it a number, and it’ll give you the lowest 
integer used to represent that number. Now, in the math real library, the two different types of 
supports are integers and reals, where reals are floating-point numbers. However, if you’re using 
this math real library, you can’t use real data types when you want to synthesize your design on 
your FPGA. So, when we’re using this, you can use it for simulation purposes. For example, if you 
want to simulate and have a value of π, it’ll represent the value of π given a constant. But you can’t 
actually synthesize that in your design. So, in all the designs we use, the only functions we will 
actually use from this library are the ceiling and floor. The next library or package we have is the 
numeric standard. Some key features of this library are that it supports logical operations such 
as greater than, less than, and greater than or equal to. The data types are unsigned and signed, 
which is key. The unsigned data type is very similar to a standard logic vector, except it gives you

3.4   The VHDL Entities and Architecture
This section is about the VHDL entities and architecture. Here, we’re going to explain what VHDL 
entity and architecture are. Every design of VHDL requires one entity and one architecture. At a 
high level, the entity defines the overall structure of your design, including its inputs and outputs. 
So, when you have a design, you can think of what the inputs and outputs are?
The entity defines these inputs and outputs. Inside the entity, you define if it is an input, output, 
or bidirectional. These are the three types of modes. You can have either an input/output or an 
“inout,” which is a bidirectional type of signal for your entity. When you define the entity, it’s 
structured as follows: you have the name, the direction (which can be “in,” “out,” or “inout”), the 
data type, and optionally, you can define the initial value. For example, if you have an output, you 
would define its name as C, set it as an output, specify the data type (which we’ll call standard 
logic), and give it an initial value, like zero (logical low or false). Now, let’s go ahead and take a 
look at the entity example. We’re going to start with a very basic example of an “AND” gate. So, if 
we have an “AND” gate, we have two inputs, A and B, and one output, which we will call C. This 
simple “AND” gate can be represented in VHDL, as shown in Figure 3.16.
Our entity is our inputs and outputs. So, we know that we’ve got our inputs, which are A and B,  
and our output, which is a C, defined in our entity. This is what we want to define inside our  
entity and how do we do that? Well, we would use the “entity” keyword as shown in the following 
codes.
Figure 3.15   The numeric standard data types.
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

entity AND_GATE is
Port(
     A : in std_logic;
     B : in std_logic;
     C : out std_logic);
end AND_GATE;
So, this entity is defining the inputs and outputs of our design. For inputs, we know we have 
A and B; for the output, we have C. The data type tells us the bit, so we know we have two bits 
going into A and B and one bit coming out as C. The next part we want to talk about is the 
architecture of a design. In VHDL, architecture defines how your design operates. So, while the 
entity defines the inputs and outputs, the architecture is going to define and explain how this 
actually works. In the case of AND gate, which we used, we know we have two inputs A and B, 
and our architecture is going to tell us to AND those inputs logically to give us our output C. For 
example, in the Button_LED project, our architecture will define how pressing a button, which 
is our input, controls the LEDs, which is our output, where our architecture needs to define how 
we are going to process the inputs to determine the outputs. Because we also have a switch that 
either enables or disables the LEDs when the button is pressed, that’s the type of stuff that will go 
on inside your architecture. So, you have a very complex VHDL design. Almost all of your VHDL 
is going to be inside that architecture. This is where the core logic of the design resides. Now, let’s 
take a look back to the example of our AND gate and define its architecture. To do this, we use 
the keyword “architecture,” as shown in the following codes.
Architecture behave of AND_GATE is
begin
    C <= A AND B;
end behave;
So, here we have effectively our entirety, our entity and the architecture of our AND gate design. 
We have our AND gate, which we define through the entity, telling us that A and B are inputs, and 
C is an output. We have our architecture, which defines the actual AND gate itself. So, our entity 
defines the inputs and outputs, and our architecture is inside the design, describing what’s actually 
happening. So next, let’s go ahead and open up the Button_LED project on your computer. We will 
take a look and see that we have the entity for our design, which is a defined entity, and we’ve named 
it BUTTON_LED. What we want to do is uncomment the port code, as we need to define the ports for 
our design. We are going to have what we call Led_Out, so we’ll use the keyword “out” and specify that 
it is an output. Then, we’ll have to give it a data type, and once we get to the data type section, we’ll 
decide what data type makes the most sense. The next keyword or entity we want to describe is our 
input. We know we have a button, so Button_In will be the input, and this is going to be a bit of a type 
“in.” This is going to be an input. We know we have one output, which is Led_Out, and we have two 
inputs: Button_In and Enable. This is what’s going to enable or disable the output of the LEDs, and 
this is going to be input as well. Right now, we have some squiggly lines telling us that our entity is not 
properly defined, and that’s because we have not defined the data type yet. We will get to that when

3.5   The VHDL Generics
This section is about VHDL generics, and we’re going to explain what a VHDL generic is and how 
we can use them in our designs. The VHDL generic provides a way of passing information into 
an entity externally. Within an entity, we have our inputs and outputs. However, with a generic, 
you don’t have to define it as an input or an output; you can think of a generic as a constant. You 
can apply it externally, which is a huge benefit in that it allows you to customize your designs in a 
way that you can modify one line of code or essentially one value in your generic to change your 
design. We’ll be using generics throughout our designs, and in any future designs that you work 
on, you’ll most likely use them. They are a very powerful tool in a way, which lets you modify your 
design or defiant in a way that is very easy to customize. For example, let’s take a look at what’s 
taken and generate a generic. If we want a design that implements an AND gate, but with three 
inputs on each side, so a total of six inputs, we’ll define two signals for A and B, each having three 
inputs. We then want to combine all of these into the AND gate, which will give us a single output, 
as illustrated in Figure 3.18.
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 43 ---
28 3 The VHDL Design Structure
So, we’ll take the top three as our A signal, and the bottom three as our B signal, with C being 
our output. So, in order to define this, we first need to make sure that we define our generic and 
the entity, as well as the architecture, as shown in the following codes.
entity Example is
Generic(
    Bits : integer := 3);
Port(
     A : in std_logic_vector(Bits-1 downto 0);
     B : in std_logic_vector(Bits-1 downto 0);
     C : out std_logic);
end Example;
architecture behave Example is
begin
   C <= A AND B;
end behave;
Now, this code defines the following AND gate, where we have a total of six inputs: A and 
B. A has three inputs and B has three inputs. Now, if we wanted to make this a four-input or 
a five-input AND gate, instead of having to modify any other piece of code, all we have to do 
is change this generic value. We can reference the Bits throughout our design – example here 
being in our port – to define the number of inputs we have, which is very powerful because, 
otherwise, if we wanted to modify this design, we would then have to go and come in the actual 
port and modify here. What’s even more powerful is that when we do things like component 
instantiations, we can pass this generic into our design. The Bits is from a higher-level design 
and trickles down, so you don’t even have to make any modifications to this specific design. 
This is an example of using a generic, and the key thing to note is that by changing just this one 
line, this generic value of 3, you can change the input width of your A and B signals. This makes 
it a lot easier. Now, in this case, it’s a very simple design, but when you’re working with com -
plex designs that have lots of things going on, you might have more than just one generic – you 
could have two, three, four, or five. Of the available generics, you just change those three, four, 
or five values and it changes the way your design operates. A lot of times, the most common 
things you’ll see in generics are, for example, like Bits. If you’re working with different designs, 
such as the bus width (the number of data bits being transferred), the number of address bits, 
the clock rate, or the intended output clock rate. So, there are many things you can define as a 
generic, but it’s a very powerful tool and something that you’ll definitely be using a lot as you 
work through different designs in VHDL. So now, what we want to do is open up your Button_
LED design, and here, we want to have a generic that we will be using. The generic we’re going 
to have will define the number of buttons we have, because for every button, we want to have an 
LED to correspond to that button. So, as opposed to physically manually changing that, we’re 
going to give it a generic. So, in order to do that, we have to define the generic before we define 
our port, as shown in Figure 3.19.

4.1   Introduction
VHSIC Hardware Description Language (VHDL) is used for describing digital and mixed-signal 
systems such as field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs) and ASICs. Here’s a brief overview of its 
statements and data types:
The VHDL statements include the following items.
	 1.	Entity Declaration: Defines the name of the module and its interface (inputs and outputs).
      entity ModuleName is
          port (
              input1: in std_logic;
              output1: out std_logic
          );
      end ModuleName;
	 2.	Architecture Declaration: Describes the internal implementation of the entity.
      architecture Behavioral of ModuleName is
          begin
              -- Implementation
      end Behavioral;
	 3.	Process Statement: Describes sequential behavior. It can include signals, variables, and state -
ments like if-else, case, loops, etc.
      process (clk)
          begin
              if rising_edge(clk) then
                -- Sequential logic
              end if;
      end process;
	 4.	Concurrent Statements: These execute simultaneously and include component instantiations, 
signal assignments, and concurrent procedure calls.
      output1 <= input1 and input2;
4
The VHDL Statements and Data T ypes
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 47 ---
32 4 The VHDL Statements and Data Types
	 5.	Component Instantiation: Includes instances of other modules within an architecture.
      U1: ModuleName port map (
          input1 => signal1,
          output1 => signal2
      );
The VHDL data types include the following items.
	 1.	Scalar Types:
	 ▪	 bit: Represents binary values (“0” , “1”).
	 ▪	 boolean: Represents logical values (‘true’ , ‘false’).
	 ▪	 integer: Represents whole numbers.
	 ▪	 real: Represents floating-point numbers.
	 2.	Composite Types:
	 ▪	 array: Represents collections of elements of the same type.
type bit_vector is array (0 to 7) of bit;
	 ▪	 record: Represents collections of elements of different types.
        type Employee is record
             ID: integer;
             Name: string;
             Salary: real;
        end record;
	 3.	Access Types: Pointers to dynamically allocated objects.
	 4.	File Types: Handles for file input and output operations.
The commonly used standard data types include the following items.
	 ▪	 std_logic: Represents a multi-valued logic type with values such as “0” , “1” , “Z” (high imped-
ance), and others for unknown and uninitialized states.
	 ▪	 std_logic_vector: Represents an array of “std_logic” values, used for buses and multi-bit 
signals.
These elements together form the foundation for designing and simulating digital systems 
in VHDL.
4.2   The VHDL Data T ypes
This section is about the VHDL data types. In this section, we’re going to cover some of the 
commonly used types, specifically the ones we will be using for the projects in this book. These 
include the standard logic, standard logic vectors, unsigned versus signed, and integer data 
types. The first one to cover is the standard logic data type. The standard logic data type repre -
sents 1 bit of data. It can represent several values: X (unknown), 0 (zero or ground), 1 (high or 
logic one in digital systems), and Z (high impedance or tri-state). The Z state is typically used 
only in cases involving “inout” signals or shared buses where multiple drivers might access 
the bus, causing it to enter a high-impedance state. A standard logic is represented with the 
keyword “std_logic.” When assigning values to a standard logic data type, you want to use a 
single quote. For example, if you have a standard logic of data type A and you want to assign 
the value of 1 (high), it would be a single quote, one, and a single quote (‘1’). The next data type 
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 48 ---
4.2 The VHDL Data Types
 33
to represent is the standard logic vector, which is effectively an array of standard logic types. 
So, if you have 10 standard logic data types, you can create a standard logic vector, which is 
just an array of those. The same logic vector can be of any size. You specify it when you define 
the data type; For example, standard logic vector A is defined from 27 down to 0, giving you 
bits 27 to 0 – 28 individual standard logic data types. The keyword for a standard logic vector is 
std_logic_vector. Inside the parentheses, you define the size of the standard logic vector, from 
starting bit and then use the keyword “downto” with the ending bit to give you the size of that 
actual vector. When you’re assigning values to a standard logic vector, you use double quotes. 
So, if you have a standard logic vector with a width of 3 and want to assign it all zeros, you 
would use a single assignment with a double quote, zero, zero, zero, and double quote, and that 
would define all three bits in that standard logic vector as all zeros. Just like the standard logic, 
the standard logic vector can represent the same values: X (unknown), 0 (low), 1 (high), and 
Z (high impedance or the tri-state buffer). So now that we understand the standard logic and 
standard logic vector data types, let’s take a look at some of the following examples.
-- VHDL Data Type Examples
-- Standard Logic
signal sig_A : std_logic := ‘0’;
signal sig_B : std_logic := ‘1’;
So now we have signal A, which is a standard logic data type with a value of zero. We also have 
signal B, which is a standard logic data type with a value of 1. Now, we can start assigning values 
with the following codes.
sig_A <= ‘1’;
sig_A <= ‘0’;
sig_A <= sig_B;
So, these are the different ways in which you can represent your standard logic. You can assign 
it a 1 or 0, you can assign it the value of another signal, which has to be a standard logic, or you 
can also assign it an element from a standard logic vector. It is just the width has to match. For 
example, if you’re working with a width of 1, it has to match the value you’re actually assigning. 
The next data type we’re going to look at is the standard logic vector. These, along with standard 
logic, are probably the most commonly used data types in VHDL. Almost every design you work 
with will have a standard logic or a standard logic vector. So, for a standard logic vector, we can 
write the following code.
signal sig_C : std_logic_vector(5 downto 0) := “000000”;
We can also use another keyword called “others.” So, we define another standard logic vector, 
and we’ll make it from 5 down to 0, giving it the initial value. We want all the values to be one, as 
shown in the following code.
signal sig_D : std_logic_vector(5 downto 0) := “111111”;
However, instead, what we can do is use the “others” keyword, as shown in the following code.
signal sig_D : std_logic_vector(5 downto 0) := (others => ‘1’);
So, that will make every one of the six bits in sig_D to logic high, or equal to the value 1.
Next, we can go ahead and make sig_C equal to the value of sig_D because both sig_C and sig_D 
are 6 bits wide, so a width of 6. Thus, the following statement is valid.
sig_C <= sig_D;

We can also access individual bits. For example, bit 3 of sig_C can be equal to sig_B, as shown 
in the following code.
sig_C(3) <= sig_B;
Because sig_B has a width of 1 bit, or we can make it equal to bit 1 of sig_D, as shown in the 
following code.
sig_C(3) <= sig_D(1);
The next part we’re going to look at here is the unsigned data types. An unsigned data type uses 
the syntax unsigned. They’re very similar to a standard logic vector in that you can access the bits 
individually, as we’ve seen. When you initialize values or give them a value, you need to use double 
quotes, which is very similar to the standard logic vector. However, the difference with an unsigned 
data type is that you can perform addition/subtraction, so you can do an unsigned data type and say 
plus/minus one to two, or you can take two unsigned data types and add/subtract them together to 
get a third unsigned data type. With standard logic vectors, you don’t have that freedom; you can’t 
do simple additions like that, and it doesn’t allow you to do that. So that’s an advantage of using the 
unsigned data type. Additionally, these can only be used to represent positive numbers. If you want 
to represent negative numbers, you have to use the signed data type. The signed data type allows 
you to use or represent negative numbers. However, when representing negative numbers in the 
signed data type, you represent them in the two’s complement form. So, it’s not as simple as the bits 
directly representing the number; so, you’ll take a number in binary and do the two’s complement 
to get the negative number. We’ll show you an example of how to do that. Just like the unsigned 
data type, the signed data type allows you the ability to use the addition/subtraction, as well as the 
greater than or equal to. For example, you can use these in an if statement for comparisons, which 
makes it very useful. So, the signed data type has advantages over the standard logic vector data 
type. Let’s go ahead and take a look at some examples. First, let’s look at the unsigned data type, as 
shown in the following codes.
-- Unsigned
signal sig_E : unsigned(3 downto 0) := (others => ‘0’);
signal sig_F : unsigned(3 downto 0) := “0010”;  -- 2
sig_E <= (others => ‘0’);
sig_E <= sig_F + 1;  -- 3
The next data type will be the signed data type that uses the keyword “signed,” as shown in the 
following codes.
-- Signed
signal sig_G : signed(3 downto 0) := (others => ‘0’); -- 0
signal sig_H : signed(3 downto 0) := “1011”;  -- -5
sig_G <= (others => ‘0’);
sig_H <= sig_G - 1;  -- -1 = “1111”
So that kind of shows you how to use this standard logic, as well as the unsigned and signed data 
types in VHDL. The next thing we’re going to do is figure out how to represent these in our Vivado 
project. Now that we know what the standard logic, standard logic vector, and unsigned and signed 
data types are, let’s see how to assign them in Vivado. Now that we have the project open, let’s 
assign the data types to our project in Vivado. We know that we have the standard logic, standard 
logic vector, and unsigned and signed data types. So if we look at our project, we have the number 
of buttons, which is defined as an integer. We also have Led_Out, which we need to define and

4.3   The VHDL Integers
This section is about the VHDL integer data types. In this section, we’re going to explain the integer 
data type and how it’s used in VHDL. In VHDL, the syntax for the integer is simply “integer,” and 
it is 32 bits by default, if you don’t define the range. We’ll show you exactly how to define the range 
for your integer, and integers can represent both positive and negative numbers. By default, the 
integer is 32 bits, but when you define the range, you can have an 8-bit, 9-bit, or any other size. 
The number of bits required to represent that range is how you will define your integer. When you 
assign a value to an integer, no quotes are needed. However, you still need to use signal assign-
ment, and then upon doing that, you assign a value without any quotes. With integers, you can 
perform operations like addition, subtraction, and comparisons (greater than, less than), similar to 
the signed and unsigned data types. Now, let’s take a look and see how the integers actually work. 
So with the integer, we are going to add to our data type examples. Here’s the code for the integer 
data type:
-- Integers
signal sig_I : integer := 0; 
So, if we were to assign a value of zero, it would be a 32-bit integer with the value of zero. Now, 
we can also define the range. So we will say integer range and then we give it the low range; we’re 
going to define the range from 0 to 255, which is equal to a value of zero.
signal sig_I : integer range 0 to 255 := 0; 
Now, we have an integer that would be 8 bits. Why? Because the range from 0 to 255 requires  
8 bits to represent that. Next, we define sig_J as follows.
signal sig_J : integer range 0 to 255 := 5; 
This is also an integer, with the same range from 0 to 255, except we’re going to give it an initial 
value of 5. So now we have two integers: sig_I, whose initial value is 0, and sig_J, whose initial value 
is 5. Now, we are going to assign them a value, as shown in the following codes.

4.4   The When-Else Statement
This section is about the VHDL when-else statement. In this section, we’re going to explain how to 
use the when-else statement in VHDL. First off, what is the when-else statement? The when-else 
statement is used to form a conditional signal assignment. What this means is that we are going 
to form a signal assignment, but based on a specific condition. For example, in the Button_LED 
project, we are going to form a signal assignment for the LEDs based on a condition, and that 
condition is going to be the Enable switch. If the Enable switch is ON (enabled), we want the 
output of the LEDs to be the state of the buttons. If the Enable switch is OFF (disabled), we want 
the output to be a zero or no LEDs. So, the whole idea behind the when-else statement is to form 
a conditional signal assignment. Let’s take a look at the actual syntax of the when-else statement, 
as shown in the following code.
signal_name <= expression1 when condition1 else expression2
For example, in the Button_LED project, our signal_name would be Led_Out, and this will be 
equal to expression1, which is equal to Button_In. When Enable is true (enabled), we want the 
output to be equal to the buttons; otherwise, we want it to be equal to a zero. Now, expanding on 
the when-else statement, you also can handle multiple conditions. In the first case, we only have 
a single condition, but we can also use multiple conditions. If we have two conditions, the syntax 
would be as follows.
signal_name <= expression1 when condition1 else
               expression2 when condition2 else
                expression3;
You have signal_name equal to expression1 when condition1 is true, or to expression2 when 
condition2 is true, or else to expression3. So, the signal_name can take one of the possibilities: 
expression1, expression2, or expression3, based on whether condition1 or condition2 is true. If 
condition1 or condition2 is true, you’ll get expression1 or expression2; otherwise, expression3 will 
be assigned to signal_name.
Now, with the way you have this one statement, you could perform this type of operation. For 
example, in our Button_LED project, where we want to assign the output of the LEDs (Led_Out) 
based on a button (Button_In), you could use a when-else statement like we have here, or you could 
use an if statement or various other types of statements to do this. You could also do it just with logic 
using AND gates and OR gates. However, this is one of the ways you can do it in a single line. So, the 
next thing we want to do is open up our Button_LED project, and we’re going to assign the when-else 
statement to define our LED output (Led_Out). We already have our library set up, our entity defined, 
and our inputs and outputs ready. The architecture is in place, and now we just need to begin the 
when-else statement for it. So, let’s assign our LED output (Led_Out) as the following code.
architecture Behavioral of BUTTON_LED is
begin
    -- Set the output of LEDs
    Led_Out <= Button_In when Enable = ‘0’ else (others => ‘0’);
end Behavioral;

So, this statement is saying that when Enable is zero, our Led_Out will be equal to Button_In. If 
we press any of the four buttons, they will be high, and our LEDs will be high as well. Each button 
will be mapped to its corresponding LED. Now, if Enable is high (or one), the LEDs will automat -
ically be reset to zero (turned off), regardless of which button is pressed. So, we were able to take 
the Button_In and Enable signals and map them to Led_Out in one single statement using the 
when-else statement. So, that’s an example of how you would use the when-else statement in the 
Button_LED project.
4.5   Concurrent Versus Sequential Statements
This section is about the concurrent versus sequential statements. In this section, we’re going 
to explain the difference between a concurrent statement and a sequential statement in VHDL. 
When we’re working with VHDL, we know that we’re describing a physical hardware circuit, 
and the advantage of using VHDL or something like an FPGA is that you can do multiple things 
in parallel, hence the concurrent statements. So, within concurrent statements, these are things 
that obviously happen at the same time. For example, you can do things such as signal assign -
ments, and these can be inside a process, a case statement, or they can happen outside of a 
process. Now, a process statement allows you to rearrange process statements. However, inside 
a process, you can have various things that occur. You can put a sequential statement inside a 
process, or that these sequential statements have to go inside a process. These include an if state -
ment, which is the infer priority, and a case statement, which is equal priority. These must go 
inside a process. With processes, you can rearrange them, and it doesn’t matter because they all 
have the same signals in the sensitivity list. They’re all going to happen at the same exact time, 
as shown in Figure 4.1.
So let’s take a look and see some examples. You will see an example of a signal assignment and 
some processes, and just take a look at some VHDL code. Now, take note that when you’re looking 
at this code, we’re not expecting you to understand everything that’s going on inside of it. We just 
want to show you some of the constructs so you can get an idea of what it actually looks like. So, 
if we take a look, let’s see what a signal assignment looks like? Well, in the case of this design, we 
have a signal, and we have this start_proc signal, and we’re giving it a signal assignment, as shown 
in the following code.
start_proc <= start_reg(1) and start_reg(0);
So, that is a signal assignment and that happens concurrently. If we look down and see data_out 
as follows, data_out is occurring at the exact same time as start_proc, even though start_proc is at 
line 103 and data_out is at line 175.
data_out <= data_reg(5 downto 1);

They’re happening at precisely the exact same time because they’re concurrent; they’re hap -
pening in concurrency, so these are occurring at the same time. Now, this also applies to process 
statements. For example, we have number_bits_proc and done_reading_proc, both of which are 
processes occurring at the same time, as shown in the following codes.
number_bits_proc : process(clk)
begin
………….
end process number_bits_proc;
done_reading_proc : process(number_bits)
begin
………….
end process done_reading_proc;
However, since the process is sensitive to a number of bits (number_bits) rather than the clock 
(clk), swapping the order of the processes – starting done_reading_proc at line 134 and moving 
number_bits_proc to line 160 – would not affect the design; the functionality would remain the 
same. So, the way in which we order these process statements doesn’t matter; the order makes no 
difference. We can move them around in our design, and it will remain exactly the same. Now, 
the time when it does make a difference is when you’re inside your process. For example, if we 
look at this start_bit_proc process, this occurs every time we have a clock (clk) signal, and we 
check the if statement on the rising edge. Then we have an if, else-if, else statement. So, first, 
we’re checking our statement, which is in first priority; if our reset is equal to zero or start_bit_
counter is equal to the max_start_bit_count minus one, then start_bit_counter becomes zero 
and is assigned the value of zero; otherwise, if read_start is equal to 1, then that condition takes 
priority. The reason that reset has first priority is because, for example, if read_start is equal to 
one and reset is equal to zero, we’re looking at a reset, and if that’s the case, start_bit_counter 
is set to zero, and we exit the statement. So, as soon as one of the conditions in the statement 
evaluates to true, you right away jump out. That’s how priority works: the item we have here at 
our top level is if the very first thing we have in the statement is the highest priority. So, when 
we say that for a priority, that’s what we mean by that, meaning if you have a reset that takes the 
highest priority, you’re going to set it to zero and just jump out of that if statement, as shown in 
the following codes.
start_bit_proc : process(clk)
begin
   if(rising_edge(clk)) then
      if((reset = ‘0’) or (start_bit_counter = max_start_bit_counter-1)) then
           start_bit_counter <= 0;
      elseif(read_start = ‘1’) then
           start_bit_counter <= start_bit_counter + 1;
      else
            start_bit_counter <= 0;
      end if;
   end if;
end process start_bit_proc;
If we look at, for example, a case statement, please note that it does not infer priority. Another 
reason for this is that when you have a case statement, you have to make sure that each case is 
unique and cannot overlap; you can’t have the same condition happening twice. For example, we 
have this case statement where our state is init_state. We cannot be in any of these other states. So, 
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

4.5 Concurrent Versus Sequential Statements
 39
if our state is read_start_bit_state, then we can’t be any of these other states, as illustrated in the 
following codes.
nxt_state_proc : process (state, start_proc, read_bit, done_reading)
begin
      nxt_state <= state;        read_start <= ‘0’;
      reading <= 0;        data_valid <= ‘0’;
      case state is
            when init_state =>
                 if start_proc = ‘1’ then
                       nxt_state <= read_start_bit_state;
                 else
                       nxt_state <= init_state:
                end if;
           when read_start_bit_state => 
                read_start <= '1';
                if (read_bit = ‘1’) then
                       nxt_state <= read_bits_state;
                else
                       nxt_state <= read_start_bit_state:
               end if;
          when read_bits_state =>
               reading <= ‘1’;
               if (done reading = ‘1’) then 
                      nxt_state <= done_state;
              else
                     nxt_state <= read_bits_state;
              end if;
         when done_state =>
              data_valid <= '1';
              if (start_proc = '1') then
                    nxt_state <= read_start_bit_state;
             else
                   nxt state <= init_state; 
            end if;
         when others =>
                  nxt_state <= init_state;
     end case;
end process nxt_state_proc;
Therefore, the way in which you order these doesn’t matter. We could have the read_start_
bit_state at the top and place init_state at the very bottom. So, the order of your case statements 
doesn’t matter; it’s not going to change the way in which your design operates. However, with if 
statements, the order does matter and will make a difference. For example, if we would switch 
this line 120, this reset equals zero or start_bit_counter equals max_bit_counter minus one. 
Now, we would swap that with read_start equals one inside the start_bit_proc process. Well, if 
read_start is one, it doesn’t matter whether we reset or not, because as soon as that condition 
evaluates to true, it will make our start_bit_counter equal to start_bit_counter plus one and 
won’t look at that first term. So, when you’re working with if statements, it does matter. When 
you have multiple conditions, you’re checking against the order in which you set each of those 
signals. That’s just a quick overview of concurrent versus sequential statements in VHDL. This 
explanation gives you an understanding of what the design looks like, as well as how you can

5.1   Introduction
This chapter is about simulation. In this section, we’re going to introduce simulation in Vivado. So 
first off, what is this section even about? Well, we’re going to explain why simulation is necessary 
and use industry-standard tools to simulate our VHSIC Hardware Description Language (VHDL) 
designs, specifically, the Vivado simulator tool from Xilinx, which is included within the Vivado 
package. With this action, we’re going to help you understand how to run these simulations, which 
is going to help as we go through different projects, and how to verify whether your project is 
working correctly. So, when you use these, we’re going to show you the steps required. You’ll make 
your design, your test bench, and then you’ll put them in a directory. We’ll show you the exact but -
tons to click and where to go to actually get the waveforms showing your design being simulated. 
So, we’ll go through all that within this section on VHDL simulation. Something that’s very impor -
tant – the reason it’s very important – is because this is used to verify your designs are behaving 
properly with conventional code. If you’re working with Visual Studios or any other type of tool, 
you can insert what they call breakpoints. This means that as your code runs, it stops, and you 
can see how the different variables are behaving internally because you can actually look at the 
values. Now, with VHDL, it’s very hard to do that – or really, it’s virtually impossible. You take the 
design, compile it, and throw it on your development board. Either it does what you expect, or 
it doesn’t. And if it doesn’t do what you’re expecting, then it becomes a challenge to understand 
why. You’ll look at your VHDL design and think everything looks fine – it synthesizes, it imple -
ments – but you still can’t figure out what’s going on. Well, that’s where virtual simulations come 
into play, and they’re very important because now, instead of having a breakpoint, you can see 
your entire design operate. You can see, OK, when these inputs are coming into my design, how 
my signals are behaving – what they are doing, and at what times they are doing it. You can see 
all this information within the simulation. So, it’s a very powerful tool, especially for debugging, 
troubleshooting, or even generating documentation. If you’re developing a design and you want to 
explain to somebody how that design is working, or if you need to create a test report or something 
of that nature, these simulation tools are great because you can see the waveforms that show your 
ones and zeros, or if you have an integer, it shows the actual integer value. You can take pictures 
of it and put it in your document, saying: when you give it this input, here’s the output, here’s the 
timing based on our simulation. At that point, it gives you high confidence. Once you simulate 
your design, you gain high confidence and can go ahead and throw it on your actual FPGA board. 
Now, when you’re working with less complex designs – if you’re just doing real simple stuff – you 
don’t necessarily always have to simulate it. But when you’re working with very complex or more 
5
The Simulations in Vivado

elaborate designs, these simulations become much more valuable and much more time-saving in 
the long run. You have to invest a little bit of time to set up the simulation, but it can save you a 
lot in the end. Instead of going in circles trying to figure out what’s going on, you can just get to 
the point right away and say, OK, it’s doing what we expect, make the change, check it, and put 
it on the board – it’s complete. What will you need to do this? In order to perform a successful 
simulation, there are several things you’ll need. First is the completed VHDL design – whatever 
your design may be. If you’re working with the Button_LED and the blinking LED project, you’ll 
need all associated files – whether it’s one or multiple. You must have the completed design that 
you want to simulate. Second, you’ll need to have a test bench. A test bench is a VHDL design 
that is not synthesizable. Its sole purpose is to test the actual design you want to run on the board. 
What you’ll do in your test bench is called component instantiation – you’ll instantiate your actual 
VHDL design inside your test bench so that you can then apply what’s called stimulus. This is 
effectively just signals where you determine when they’re on, off, or what values they’re going to 
be. You then monitor the output from your test bench. Within your test bench, you can do this in 
several ways. You can calculate what the value should be and do a comparison to tell you if you 
passed, or you can just apply a stimulus and monitor the output via the waveforms you’ll see in the 
Vivado simulator. So, there are various ways you can do that. For this section, we are going to use 
an example of a shift register. We’ll generate a design – a shift register – and also design a test bench 
to test that shift register. Then we’ll do the simulation demonstration in Vivado using the Xilinx 
Arty A7 development board. Now that you understand what this section is about and how we’re 
going to do it, let’s go ahead and get started with designing the shift register.
5.2   The Shift Register Design
This section is about the shift register design. In this section, we’re going to explain how we want our 
basic shift register to operate, as well as how we’re going to design and write the VHDL code for this 
shift register. Now, the shift register we are going to implement is a standard serial-in and parallel-out 
shift register, which means we’ll have one input data line and four output data lines. We’re then going 
to use the processor to develop a test bench, which will then be used to simulate this in Vivado. So, for 
our shift register design, we want to have a serial-in and parallel-out architecture. Let’s draw a block 
diagram of what this register would approximately look like. Sometimes, having a block diagram helps 
us get a better understanding of what we’re trying to do. So, we’re going to have our serial-in defined 
by a value we’ll call data_in. Then we’ll have a clock and a reset. The way we want our shift register to 
work is on data_in, which is the single standard logic vector – it’s going to be a single bit. We then have 
our clock, which is going to, on every clock cycle, shift the outputs. Since we have four outputs, we’ll 
label them A, B, C, and D. This is a parallel-output shift register – A, B, C, D – so we want to assign the 
labels A, B, C, and D. On each clock cycle, we want to list out the things we want to happen – we want 
A to be equal to our data_in, since this is our input; B will be equal to the value of A, because we’re 
going to take the value here and shift it every clock cycle; C is going to be equal to the value of B; and D 
is going to be equal to the value of C. Now, once we shift our value, it will essentially be discarded – it 
won’t be going anywhere. So, on each clock cycle, the value of data_in will enter at A and shift over 
time to give it a clock shift. Next, we want to look at Figure 5.1. Given what we have described, we can 
now proceed with the VHDL design for this shift register.
To do that, we will begin designing our 4-bit shift register in VHDL using the behavioral 
architecture. Now, the key thing to note is that we have not yet covered all the topics needed to

complete the shift register. As a result, there will be parts of the design that you may not completely 
understand right now. However, we’ll just explain them as we go, and once you encounter them 
later, you’ll get a better understanding of how these different architectures in VHDL are being 
used. First, we’ll start with our libraries. We’re using the IEEE library and the following packages: 
IEEE.STD_LOGIC_1164.ALL and IEEE.numeric_std.all. Then, we’ll define our entity as shown in 
the following codes.
-- Libraries
library IEEE;
use IEEE.STD_LOGIC_1164.ALL;
use IEEE.numeric_std.all;
-- Entity Declaration
entity Shift_Reg is
port(
    A : out std_logic;
    B : out std_logic;
    C : out std_logic;
    D : out std_logic;
    data_in : in std_logic;
    reset : in std_logic; 
    clk : in std_logic);
end Shift_Reg; 
So, now we define our architecture as the following codes.
-- Architecture
architecture behavior of Shift_Reg is
-- Defined Signals
signal A_reg, B_reg : std_logic := ‘0’;
signal C_reg, D_reg : std_logic := ‘0’;
-- Begin Architecture
begin
     -- Signal Assignments
     A <= A_reg;
     B <= B_reg;
     C <= C_reg;
     D <= D_reg;
     -- Process that is used to shift the values
     reg_process: process(clk)
     begin
         if(rising_edge(clk)) then
              if(reset = ‘1’) then

That completes the entire design of our actual shift register. We have our libraries called out, our 
entity where we define our outputs and inputs. We have our architecture for the behavior of our 
shift register, and we define the signals we’re using. Then we use the begin architecture and our 
signal assignments, so we assign our signals. We take the registers and assign values to our A, B, 
C, and D registers, which are then connected to the output – the output of our design, the actual 
product of our shift register. At this point, we have our shift register completely designed. Now, 
we simply need to define and design our test bench so that we can then take the shift register and 
simulate it.
5.3   Shift Register T est Bench Design
This section is about the shift register test bench design. Here, we’re going to walk through and 
explain what we’re going to try and do with the shift register test bench, and then proceed to imple -
ment and design it. We’ll be using Notepad++, though you can use any text editor that allows you 
to save a file with the .vhd extension, or you can also use the Vivado model, which allows you to 
actually write and edit VHDL designs. For our design, we have the shift register, which includes 
input_data clk, reset, and outputs A, B, C, and D. In the test bench, we don’t actually have any 
inputs or outputs. All we’re doing is instantiating the shift register inside the test bench design. 
From there, we’ll analyze and look at the outputs – A, B, C, and D – while applying stimulus or data 
to the data_in, clk, and reset signals to see how the outputs respond. If we think about it visually, 
we can imagine our test bench design containing signals A, B, C, and D. We can enclose signals 
A, B, C, and D in a small box to indicate that they are our designated signals. The data_in signal 
will connect sequentially to each of the signals – starting with signal A, then to signal B, and so 
on. We also need to define our stimulus. For the stimulus, we’ll create signals named data_in, clk, 
and reset, and we’ll name them the same as our signals on the shift register so it’s easier to follow. 
In the block diagram shown in Figure 5.2, the stimulus is represented. So, data_in is tied to the 
data_in signal, clk is tied to clk, and reset is tied directly to the reset signal, as shown in Figure 5.2.
So, we’re going to apply values to the stimulus – our data_in, clk, and reset – and while we do 
that, we’ll observe the output signals inside the simulation. We can see our signals A, B, C, and 
D and make sure they’re doing exactly what we want. Now, if we think about our test bench, it 
encompasses the entire design. The design shown in Figure 5.2 is our Shift_Reg, and in this case, 
we’re going to call it test_Shift_Reg. In our test bench, there are no inputs and no outputs – it 
is completely self-contained inside the architecture. So, there’s nothing coming in or going out. 
Everything we’re looking at is internal. All we’re doing is applying stimulus internally to test the

So, the entity is empty – there are no inputs or outputs in the design. We’re only working with the 
architecture, and that’s where we observe our signals to see what’s going on. In our test bench, there 
are no inputs or outputs because a test bench is not synthesized. You cannot actually load it onto the 
FPGA; it’s strictly used to simulate and test. So, the architecture code would be written as follows.
architecture test of test_Shift_Reg is
component Shift_Reg

signal data_in: std_logic := ‘0’;

        wait for 150 ns;
    end process data_stimulus;

At that point, that is our entire design from start to finish. This is our top-level test bench design. 
We want to save it with the name, test_Shift_Reg. You don’t have to follow the convention, but 
we like to use that naming convention so that when we put everything in the same directory and 
simulate using Vivado, it’s clear. The design file is Shift_Reg, and by adding the keyword “test” in 
front of it, we know that that’s the test bench for the shift register. So, you don’t have to follow that 
naming convention, but that’s just commonly what we like to use. So that is the entire test bench 
for the shift register design.
5.4   Vivado Simulator T ool Introduction
This section introduces the Vivado simulator tool. Here, we’re going to briefly explain and intro -
duce the Vivado simulator, which is a simulation tool integrated into Vivado. No external tool 
needs to be downloaded; when you download Vivado with the free license or the web version, 
the simulator is already included. All you have to do is bring in your test bench and run the simu -
lation; it’s all included in one big package. So, it’s nice that you don’t have to download a separate 
tool or anything. It’s already included in the same project, so you don’t have to have two separate 
projects. One of the nice things about Vivado is that everything is integrated into a single tool. 
With the Vivado simulator, you can view your waveforms and analyze how signals behave in 
relation to each other. For example, when one signal goes high, you can see when another signal 
goes high or low. You can also measure the timing differences between those signal transitions. 
Additionally, you can save waveform images, zoom into specific portions, and set the simula -
tion runtime. One feature we really like is the ability to simulate at different design stages. First, 
there’s behavioral simulation, which simulates your VHDL design. Then, after synthesis, you can 
simulate your synthesized design as well as your implement design. Your implemented design is 
going to be as close as a simulation can get to your actual FPGA device, because with your imple -
mented design, it’s going to know all of the specific delays – from when a certain event occurs to 
when the next event occurs – because it’s already routed on your FPGA. They’ve already got the 
routing mapped out, and they’ll have a really good estimate on the certain timings and delays. 
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

5.5 Running a Simulation in Vivado
 47
So, you can go ahead and run a simulation after that to verify that you’ll meet your timing. So, 
the first step is to do a behavioral-type simulation, and that’s just to verify that you’re logically 
doing exactly what you want your design to do. For example, you’re pressing a button – you want 
to make sure that lights up an LED. In this case, you want to say, “OK, we’ve got input A; we want 
to make sure when input A goes high, output B goes high.” Behaviorally, we’re just going to make 
sure that happens. Now, when you implement and run that simulation, that’s going to make sure 
when we press A and B goes high, what’s that time delay between them – does it meet the timing 
specifications? If you specify those timings, the tool will make sure they are met. But you can 
then simulate and verify that there are no issues with things like that. When you’re working with 
the Vivado simulator, it can only be used on Xilinx designs. You have to create a Vivado project. 
You can pick just some sort of generic device, make a generic design, simulate it, and verify it 
works. Then, you can take that design out. If you’re working with Xilinx-specific features, such 
as internal memory, you want to use Vivado because it can simulate things like that. It under -
stands the initialization files you can place to say that we want our memory initialized with these 
values. So, for simulation, we can account for those, knowing they’re in there and how we are 
actually going to use them. A lot of time, we’ll be using the simulator tool. For example, we’re 
going to use it to simulate our shift register design. So, we’ll be needing two files: the shift reg -
ister design file – the actual physical design we want to test – and the test bench. We will load 
the test bench and tell it to run the simulation. It will simulate the actual design we’re going to 
be implementing. We want to use this to verify that our designs are behaving as expected, which 
is the main overall purpose of using VHDL simulations. In the future, you can use this whether 
you’re working at your job or on other projects at home. If you’re working with Xilinx devices 
and using their tools, you can use this understanding to apply these simulations. The shift reg -
ister simulation is a very simple and basic simulation, but it’s just here to give you the first steps 
in understanding the simulation process and how to run a simulation and design a test bench. 
You can then apply this to future projects or more complex things you may be working on. So 
now you have an understanding of what the simulator tool is and how we’re going to be using it. 
Let’s get started – the next step is running a simulation in Vivado.
5.5   Running a Simulation in Vivado
