specifically written to test this shift register. Once you have your project set up – in this case, a 
basic project with the shift register already created or implemented – you’ll begin by locating the 
design source file. Under the Design Source section in Vivado, you’ll see a file named Shift_Reg.vhd. 
Double-clicking on the file opens it and shows the shift register design you have designed. Next, 
under the Simulation Sources, you’ll also see the shift register (Shift_Reg). As shown in Figure 5.3, 
Vivado recognizes the shift register as the top-level module for simulation. This is because it is cur -
rently the only module defined for simulation within the project.
So, what we want to do is import the test bench. To do that, we’ll select the Simulation Sources, 
right-click, and click on Add Sources. Make sure you select the third option: Add or create simula-
tion sources, as depicted in Figure 5.4.
Then, we’ll click Next, and leave the specified simulation set as the default, sim_1. This allows 
you to run different simulations, such as sim_1, sim_2, etc. But for our cases and everything we’re

5.6   Navigating Vivado Simulations
This section explains how to use the Vivado simulation tool. So, the tool in Vivado that we use to sim -
ulate is all part of the Vivado tool, which is referred to as the Vivado simulator. Within this tool, there 
are many different options, capabilities, and features. We’re not going to go through every single one 
of those, as that would take quite a bit of time. There’s a lot you can do with the Vivado simulator. 
We’re going to show you, at a high level, how to simulate, so that throughout this project you can use 
this tool to verify that your designs are working as expected – before you go ahead and load them onto 
the FPGA. Once you’re there, it’s not like working with regular software where you can pause, debug, 
or set breakpoints. You don’t quite have those same capabilities, and unfortunately, once it’s on the 
FPGA, you also do not have any indicators like LEDs or UART output. So, this is a great way to test 
your design before deploying it to the FPGA. To start, we already know how to run our simulation in 
Vivado. You have created a project, written a test bench, and launched the simulation. Now, you have 
your simulation running, as shown in Figure 5.12. You can save the simulation window and re-open 
it later, but for now, we’ll just leave it running. The first thing we’re looking at is how you can arrange 
windows or expand your design view. You can click and drag the window edges to make the display 
area larger. It’s helpful to see everything clearly. You don’t want to scrunch everything together, as it

let’s say we want to look at the range from 60 ns to 70 ns. We can click and drag again to define that 
red window, and that zooms us into 60 ns to 70 ns. All of this depends on how long you run your 
design, how fast your clock (clk) is running, and what exactly is happening during that time. If 
everything you need to see is within 100 ns, there’s no point in running the design for 500 ns. Just 
run it for 100 ns and focus on what’s happening in that specific range. Now, if we’re trying to find 
the exact point where something is happening, but we’re not sure when it occurs, we can go back 
to our Full View. From there, we can look at specific edges. For example, if we select a signal – like 
the clock (clk) – as soon as we select the clock (clk), you’ll see the outline edge of that signal high-
lighted, as shown in Figure 5.18.
If you’ll notice, when the signal is high, it’s shaded underneath; when it’s low, there’s no shade. 
This makes it much easier to see when a signal is high versus when it’s low. You can pick out those 
transitions pretty easily. However, they are also tools to help you identify transitions. At the top, 
there’s a little toolbar. We know we can zoom in by right-clicking, or we can click on the zoom 
in or zoom out buttons. You can also use Zoom Fit, Go to Cursor, Go to Time Zero, or Go to Last 
Time. These are just shortcut buttons that let you move around the simulation timeline. Now go 
back to Full View and bring the cursor right here. We have transitions, and we’re looking at the 
next transition. Say we’re on the clock (clk). If we go to the next transition, it’ll take us from a low

to a high. A transition is defined as when your signal changes from one state to the next, and for 
each of these signals, they only have the state high or low. So, a transition is going to be when you 
go from a low to a high or from a high to a low. You can keep clicking through the transitions. On 
the clock, it transitions every 10 ns: up, down, up, down. Now, take another signal, say A. We can 
see that our next transition is from high to low. If we go to our next transition, it moves to the next 
one. We can also do a previous transition to go back. If we click it again, it will take us back to 
the very beginning. Once you reach the end, it won’t take you any further. So that’s kind of a nice 
way if you have a signal that you’re looking to see what happens only when that signal is making 
a transition, where you can use that tool to kind of figure out where you’re at. So, it’s a nice little 
way to see the different transitions. Next, we will look at how to measure time between two events. 
For example, let’s say we want to know the time difference between when output A goes high and 
when output C goes high. We can look at the scale and estimate that A goes high around 40 ns and 
C goes high around 80 ns, so we can calculate it out. But when you’re working with different sig -
nals – especially in time-critical applications –something might be running at two different clock 
rates, or a signal might not be occurring exactly on a clock rate. You might be looking at an asyn-
chronous signal coming in, and you’ll want to check that timing. So, it’s a nice thing to be able to 
do. In order to do that, you’ll want to take your marker and select the first edge. For example, we’re 
going to select our A signal. We’ll go ahead and move to that transition – so now A goes high at  
40 ns – and we’re going to click the Add Marker button. That’s going to add a marker at our 
transition A, which gives us a point at 40 ns. Now, we’re going to select our marker, make sure we 
have our cursor selected, and we want to select C. So now we’re at our signal C and we want to 
know the time at which C goes high. We’ll go ahead and select that transition. That gives us our 
two transitions. At this point, we know the first marker is at 40 ns, and the second is at 80 ns, as 
depicted in Figure 5.19.
So, the difference between them is 40 ns. We can go ahead and add another marker and continue 
exploring. For instance, let’s say we want to add a marker here – these markers simply indicate 
specific time points. Our start time is 40 ns, which is the first marker, and 80 ns is our second 
marker. This is a nice way to measure the time between certain events, as illustrated in Figure 5.20.
It requires you to do a little math, but you can see that it’s 40 ns and 80 ns, and that tells you the 
difference. So, that’s a little indicator to say, OK, this occurred at exactly this time right now with 
this design. It works out so that these things happen exactly at nice times, like 40 ns versus 80 ns. 
In some designs, you’ll have things where, for example, it may have started at 42.87 ns and the next 
one started at 82.46 ns, so when that’s the case, it’s a lot nicer to be able to see the marker on your 
map, do the math right away, and understand if that’s the time you’d expect to see.

350 ns, so it’s pretty short. But, for example, let’s say all we’re interested in – if there’s a lot going

6.1   Introduction
This chapter is about the introduction of buttons and LEDs on the Arty A7 boards. In this 
chapter, we’re going to explain what we’re trying to do with the project buttons and LEDs. For 
this project, we’re going to look at the Arty A7 development board. Our goal is to incorporate 
the four push buttons on the Arty A7 development board, a single slide switch, and the four reg -
ular LEDs on the board. What we want to do is use an enable signal, which is going to be used 
by one of the slide switches. When enabled, pressing a button should cause the corresponding 
LED to light up. For example, if we press button zero (BTN0), which is the button on the farthest 
right, we want LD4, the regular LED closest to the right, to light up. Similarly, for button one 
(BTN1), we would like the second LED (LD5) to light up. If we press all four buttons at once, 
all four LEDs light up at the same time. That is the goal for this chapter. This project incorpo -
rates two different types of inputs: four push buttons, a single slide switch, and then four LED 
outputs. It serves as a basic introduction to the workflow of using tools for Vivado, developing a 
basic VHDL design, including the entity, the architecture, and the basic ins and outs of standard 
VHDL design, as well as the process to implement it using the tools, program it, and run it on 
this Arty A7 development board. That is the overview of the introduction to the development 
board for the buttons and LEDs project.
6.2   Buttons and LEDs Complete Design Walk-through
This section is about the complete design walk-through. In this section, we’re going to show you 
how and what we do to have all these things in the Button_LED project, what everything is doing, 
and why it’s there. So, in the Button_LED project, we know that we have our specific inputs – our 
buttons and an enable – and our outputs, which are the LEDs. So, at the very beginning, let’s just 
walk through the design and explain everything we have in the design and why we have it, as 
shown in Figure 6.1.
So, at the very top, we have some comments that you can fill out to help explain the design. 
This way, when you look at it later, you get an understanding. For example, there are fields for 
the company name, the engineer’s name, the design name, and the project name. The impor -
tant thing you include here is a description explaining what this file actually does. That way, 
if you open it up, you can just read the description and get an understanding of what’s going 
on. Additionally, there is a revision, which is useful for keeping track of all the changes that

have been made. Next, we have the library and we’re using the IEEE library, which is very

we’re using the standard logic 1164 package, which gives us access to standard logic as well as 
the standard logic vector data types. It also allows us to perform different Boolean expressions, 
such as a logical AND, OR, NOR, and other logical operations. Now, we have our entity, which 
we just call BUTTON_LED. We define our entity with a generic, which is NUM_BUTTONS, 
and this value is going to change based on the development board being used. For the Arty A7 
development board, NUM_BUTTONS is set to 4.
So, when we say we have four buttons, that means we have four output LEDs and four input 
buttons. We also have Enable, which is just one. The Enable is just a standard logic data type, 
while Led_Out and Button_In are standard logic vectors. This defines the entity for our Button_
Led project design. Next, we have the architecture, which we have named Behavioral. The 
Behavioral architecture is describing the BUTTON_LED entity, and then we have the begin–
end pair to just describe the actual architecture of what’s going on inside our design. For this 
design, we use a when-else statement to control the output of the LEDs. We set the output 
of the LED (Led_Out) to match the button input (Button_In) when Enable is equal to zero;  
otherwise, the output is equal to zero. If the buttons have a high pull-up resistor, they are 
high by default. So, you just have to do an inversion on the buttons input, and that’s just done 
by putting a not in front of Button_In. So, all this statement is doing is basically deciding  
the output of the LEDs, if it’s a Button_In or if it’s a zero, based on Enable, which is tied to a 
slide switch. That provides an overview of our design and a complete walk-through from top 
to bottom, what we’re trying to do with this Button_LED project, that is the entire VHDL code 
needed to get the project to run successfully.

ming file is a .bit file or a bit, and this file holds all the configuration data derived from our VHDL

design, our constraints for the pin-out location, timing constraints, and more. It uses all this 
information to configure the FPGA to perform exactly as specified. The steps to generate this file 
begin with ensuring that a top-level design file is defined. In this case, the top-level design file is a 
VHDL file. However, it could also be a block design, IP core, or whatever you have in your top-level 
design. You’ll notice it by the three squares – two blue and one green on top. If you select and

enough to know the top-level file. In addition, you’ll also need to have a constraints file before gen-
erating a programming or a .bit file. We already have one there, as shown in Figure 6.8.
A constraints file contains the pin-out locations, timings constraints, and any other types of con-
straints you want to apply to your project. These are defined in your .xdc file. So, the first step is 
to go ahead and click Run Synthesis, as shown in Figure 6.8. This’ll bring up a small window, as 
depicted in Figure 6.9. Simply click OK, because we will go ahead and run the synthesis.
Synthesis is the process of analyzing your VHDL design to verify that you have the correct syntax 
and that the design makes logical sense. There are several steps involved, and regardless of the type 
of tool you’re using, you’ll need to synthesize your design and then do a fitting or implementation. 
In the next step, it is going to ask us if we want to implement our design, as illustrated in Figure 6.10.
Now that the synthesis has been completed successfully, and there are no errors or warn -
ings, this means our VHDL is syntactically correct, and the compiler – or the synthesizer for

Vivado – understands it and can implement the VHDL design we have described. So now, we want

click OK because we’re going to go ahead and run the implementation. This is going to take our syn -
thesized design and attempt to fit it on the actual device. You could have a VHDL design that passes 
synthesis but takes up so many resources that you can’t actually fit it on your FPGA development 
board. If that happens, you’ll get a failure or error thrown, saying, “Hey, the implementation failed 
because we can’t fit it on that design.” For example, it might take over 100% of the lookup table or it 
could define more IOs than are available on that FPGA, which will result in a failure there. If your 
.xdc file does not define any of the IOs, it’s going to throw a lot of warnings. It will indicate that while 
the design can be implemented, it doesn’t know where to place the inputs or outputs on FPGA. So, in 
this case, our implementation has completed successfully, as shown in Figure 6.11.

or warnings and that our implementation is successfully completed. The final step is to generate 
the bitstream.

Click on Generate Bitstream and select OK, as shown in Figure 6.12. This process takes our syn-

A lot of times, when you’re working with Code C/C++, people tend to focus on the number of 
lines of code or how much memory it takes up. However, with FPGA, it just comes down to how 
many resources the design uses, which makes a big difference. You can have a lot of lines of code 
or just a few, but that doesn’t necessarily make a big difference. Now, our bitstream generation has 
completed, as depicted in Figure 6.13.

program the physical device as shown in Figure 6.15.

look at the left side of the Vivado interface, you’ll see the Flow Navigator, which outlines the flow 
of your project. At the very beginning, you can see project settings. This is where you can define 
parameters depending on the type of project or a specific device. If you have specific constraints

7.1   Introduction
In this chapter, we are going to review some VHDL statements, including constants and signals, 
VHDL processes, VHDL if statements, as well as an example of if statement priority. Finally, the 
blinky LEDs project is implemented with the Arty A7 development board using Vivado.
7.2   Constants and Signals
This section is about signals and constants. In this section, we’re going to explain how signals as 
well as constants are used in VHDL. Now, a signal or constant is not a data type. You can have  
a constant that is an integer, a standard logic, or a standard logic vector. Similarly, you can have a 
signal that is an integer, a standard logic, or a standard logic vector. Thus, a signal and a constant 
are types of object in VHDL that we use to define our designs. First, we’re going to start with 
constants. What constants do in the VHDL language is give you the ability to have a better docu-
mented code. For example, if you’re using a baud rate throughout your design, you could just type 
in that value. So, if you’re running at 9600, or 9600 bits per second baud rate, you could type that 
in everywhere you want to have baud rate. But if you use a constant, it just makes it much easier 
to read, where in one line you calculate what the baud rate is or you define a constant, which 
would be like an integer to a specific baud rate, and then everywhere else in your code where you 
reference that baud rate, you just use that constant name. So, the big thing about a constant – the 
key thing to know – is it allows you to document your code much better, and it looks much easier. 
When you come back later and look at the code, you’ll be able to understand what’s going on 
more clearly. The syntax for a constant is just “constant” spelled out. To define it, you would write 
“constant” , then the constant name, a colon, the constant’s data type, an equals sign, and finally, 
a semicolon. We’ll show some examples of how to define constants. The next thing we’re talking 
about is a signal. A signal is different from a constant. It’s an object that you define in your design. 
Signals are used to connect different parts of a design – you can think of them as internal wires. In 
a physical design on a breadboard, you use wires; however, in VHDL, you use signals. For example, 
the entity of one design can be connected to the entity of another design using signals. It’s a way 
to connect dynamic data between designs. So, if you have two AND gates, and the output of one 
AND gate goes to the input of another AND gate, that would be a signal because that’s dynamic 
data. That value is constantly changing based on the inputs. The keyword for defining a signal is 
just signal. You give it a signal name, followed by a colon, and then the signal type, which would be 
7
The Blinky LEDs Project
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 91 ---
76 7 The Blinky LEDs Project
an integer (unsigned or signed), a standard logic vector, or a standard logic. You can then option-
ally assign it an initial value using colon equals. If you don’t give it an initial value, the compiler 
will resolve it to a default value or to whatever value you specify during reset, which becomes the 
signal’s initial value. Now, let’s take an example and go through a signals and constants example. 
The first thing we’ll look at is a signal example. Before we do that, we are going to sketch a rough 
digital design. It will include two AND gates and one OR gate, as shown in  Figure  7.1  . This is just 
a very basic VHDL design.     
 So, we have three logic gates, and we know we have our entity, which defines our inputs and 
outputs. We have four inputs, which we’ll call A, B, C, and D, and we’re going to have one output, 
which we will call E. So, our entity will define these four inputs – A, B, C, and D – as well as the 
output, E. Now, we have two signals that come from the outputs of the AND gates, and they go 
to the inputs of the OR gate. These are what we would call signals, because the inputs and out-
puts have already been defined, but these internal signals are communicating dynamic data. We’ll 
just name these signals f and g, and if we know that, those are going to be our signals. From a 
VHDL perspective, we have our entity, which defines the inputs in this case, a single output in 
architecture, which would define the two AND gates and the single OR gate, and the inside of 
our architecture, which defines two signals, f and g. This is an example of how signals are used. 
You can think of them as internal wires inside your actual logic design. Next thing we look at is a 
constant – how or why you would use a constant? There are many different ways you could use a 
constant. In this example, we’re going to use a comparator. Let’s say we have a two-bit comparator. 
So, the two-bit comparator has to have four inputs. We’ll have our A and B inputs, and since it’s a 
two-bit comparator, A and B are both two bits, and we need to make sure we specify that they are 
two bits. The way the comparator works is it’s going to compare our A and B inputs and output a 
greater than, an equal to, or less than. So, we are going to label them as G, E, and L, as shown in 
 Figure  7.2  .     
 So, if our A is greater than B, which we will define here with two bits, then we have a one on our 
G output. If our A is equal to B, then we’ll have a one on our E output, and if our A is less than B, 
we’ll have a one on the L output. Now, in this case, we’re going to say our entity is just A and B, and 
the G, E, and L. So, in this case, we have this B, which is an internal value. Now, for this specific 
case, if we make B a signal, we would have to tie it off to something else. So, you can give it an 
initial value and it never changes, which, in that case, is by definition a constant. So, for this case, 
if we wanted to make B a constant, we can write the following code. 
  constant B: std_logic_vector(1 downto 0) := “11”; 
  So, in this case, we know that we have our B, which is a “11.” We’ve defined the constant B as a 
standard logic vector, so anywhere we use B in our design, it’s going to be a two-bit value of “11,” 
or 3 in this case. On the other hand, A is changing because it’s part of our entity. So, this value (A) 
is changing, and it’s always going to be compared to our constant B, which is defined as “11.” Based 
on that comparison, our output will be greater than, equal to, or less than – so that’s an example

We want to go ahead and set up our entity, and our libraries are already defined in the packages. 
We have our architecture defined, except we don’t actually have anything inside it. In order to use 
constants and signals inside our architecture, we need to define them before the begin statement. 
So, before the begin and after the architecture keyword, we want to actually put our signals and 
constants. The first thing we want to do is define our constants. We’ll start by defining a constant 
called maximum value. This will be used to calculate the count value required to achieve the blink 
rate, which we have defined in our entity. We’re going to calculate this count value to determine 
the blink rate from the generic by taking our clock rate and dividing it by the blink rate, which 
gives us the maximum value we need to calculate since we need to count in order to achieve the 
specified blink rate. The next step is to create a constant called bit depth, which will determine the 
number of bits required to count to whatever value because our count value is going to be chang -
ing, and that’s based on the clock rate and blink rate, the number of bits that are required to count 
that will change as well as the following codes.

So, that bit depth is going to tell us how many bits are required to count to the maximum 
value. The next thing we’re going to need is a signal, which we’ll call “count_reg” . This will be 
used to hold the actual count value. As we increment our count, the value will be stored inside 
this signal. So, we want to make it a signal because it’s going to be constantly changing. If we 
made it a constant, we can never change it. So, this one has to be a signal and we’re going to 
make it an unsigned type. The reason we use unsigned is that it allows us to perform addition 
and subtraction operations on it. With a standard logic vector, you can’t take a standard logic 
vector and add one to it, since you have to physically make it do that. So, an unsigned type is a 
lot easier for doing counting type of operations, and we want to make it a bit depth minus one 
down to zero, and we will give it an initial value of zero. Since we don’t know what the depth 
is, we can’t manually type out all the zeros. Therefore, we will have to use the others keyword, 
and this will select all the bits that we haven’t defined, which in this case are all of them, and 
we’ll set them all to the value we specify, which we are going to tell it to set all the values to 
zero. Now, we need one more signal, and this signal is going to be what we would call led_reg 
or LED register, which is going to hold the value that’s going to be output on our LEDs. So, this 
will make a standard logic vector, and we’re going to make it number of LEDs (NUM_LEDS) 
minus one down to zero, and we’ll give it an initial value in this case, because we know that all 
of our LEDs we have defined in this case is four. If you have eight, you have to put eight zeros 
here. However, in this case, we have four LEDs. Now, we could use the others keyword as well. 
But the reason we’re going to go with zeros is that if we want to have it where we toggle and 
say instead of having it all zeros and ones, we want it to be 1010, and we can do that by just 
changing the definition we have of our signal “led_reg.” Now, at this point, we have our four 
blinky LED design, and we have the constants and signals that we need for our project defined 
in our VHDL design file. At this point, all we’re going to do then is just update the comments 
to include those. But we’ll leave that up to you because you can add whatever makes the most 
sense in terms of helping you come back later and understand what these actual constants and 
signals are doing.
7.3   The VHDL Processes
This section is about the VHDL processes. In this section, we’re going to explain what a pro -
cess is in VHDL. In VHDL, we can use logical statements such as AND gates, OR gates, or 
any type of operation, and these are evaluated instantaneously. For example, if we have an 
AND gate with two inputs and one output, any time one of the inputs changes, the output 
changes almost immediately. However, if we want something to occur at a specific time or in 
response to a specific event, such as a signal changes, we would use a VHDL process. So, what 
these processes do is define a portion of your design that you want to be evaluated whenever 
a certain signal changes state. There are several parts to a VHDL process. First, there’s the 
process label, which is used to name your process. When you have multiple processes in your 
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 94 ---
7.3 The VHDL Processes
 79
design, giving them labels helps you identify each one easily. Then, there’s the sensitivity list. 
Inside the sensitivity list, you include all the signals that you want to be monitoring. Whenever 
they change, you evaluate your process. Most commonly, the clock signal is included in the 
sensitivity list. So, you want the process to be evaluated any time the clock changes. When the 
clock transitions from a low to a high state, the process is evaluated; likewise, if the clock goes 
from a high to a low, you’re going to evaluate the process again, and on the same side, you 
can put any type of signal in there. For example, if you have a standard logic vector with eight 
elements, you might include that in the sensitivity list. When any one of the elements in that 
standard logic vector goes from a low to a high or from a high to a low, you will then evaluate 
that process, and so the only time a process block is evaluated is whenever any one of the sig -
nals in the sensitivity list is evaluated. The syntax for a VHDL process statement is illustrated 
in Figure 7.3.
We have the process label, which is optional. You don’t have to give it a label – you can just 
call it a process. But a lot of times, we think it’s best to actually give it a label. It helps make your 
code more readable, and when you come back, you can give it a comment. Plus, the label helps 
explain what’s going on. So, if your process is counting, it makes sense to just say something 
like count and then process, so that when you come back and look through something, you can 
say, “OK, this process is implementing a counter.” Next, you have to have the colon and then 
the keyword process, and then inside of the brackets you want to insert a sensitivity list, where 
you’ll include all the signals that you want to be looking at. Whenever any of the signals inside 
that sensitivity list change from high to low, you’ll then evaluate everything inside your process. 
Underneath that, you have your process declarations. This is where you can declare variables, 
signals, or constants. If you declare them within the list, they’ll only be seen inside your actual 
process and not throughout the entire design. As we know from our signals and constants, once 
we define those before the begin statement of our architecture, we now then evaluate and use 
those inside our process statement. Then we have the begin, which is referred to as the sequen -
tial statements section. After the begin is where we can include constructs such as if statements 
or any other type of statements that evaluate what the output of our process should be. We then 
use the end process, which completes the begin–end pair of the VHDL process. After that, you 
include the process label again to indicate that the process defined earlier is officially ending. 
Once you open the blinky LED (BLINKY_LED) project, we are going to add our processes into 
the project. We already have the libraries and entities defined, as well as the architecture. We’ve 
got defined the constants and signals we’ll be using. Now, we need to define the processes. So 
underneath the begin, the first thing we’ll do is assign our output LED values. Therefore, we’ll 
create a concurrent statement. We’ll do this by assigning Led_Out the value of led_reg. This 
means that at all points in time, Led_Out, which we have defined in our port as an output, will 
reflect the value of led_reg, which we have defined as a signal. This setup allows us to assign 
a value to led_reg within our process, and that value will automatically be passed to Led_Out, 
While we could assign a value to Led_Out directly, using led_reg gives us the advantage of being 
able to read its value. In contrast, we can’t actually read the value of Led_Out directly, since it’s 
[process_label:] process (sensitivity_list)
      process_declarations
      begin
      sequential_statements
      end process [process_label];
Figure 7.3   The VHDL process statement syntax.

See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 95 ---
80 7 The Blinky LEDs Project
an output port. We don’t know what that is, but we can read the value of led_reg, and by reading 
that, we know what is on our output. So, for the first process, we’re going to create a count pro -
cess. We’ll add a little note that says process increments the counter on every rising clock edge. 
To start, we’ll give it a label – let’s call it count_proc – followed by a colon and then space and 
we use the process keyword. After that, we’ll define the sensitivity list. In this case, we only care 
about changes on the rising clock edge of the input clock (Clk), which is defined in our inputs. 
So, Clk will be the only signal in the sensitivity list. With the sensitivity list complete, we won’t 
be defining any specific signals within this process, so we move on to the begin and end pro -
cess. We’ll label the end of the process with count_proc, completing the begin–end pair for the 
first process. Later, inside the structure, we will define what we actually want to happen each 
time the clock toggles. So, this is going to be a process that counts, and what we want to do is 
increment our count register to determine when we reach our max value. The next process we 
want to define will basically toggle the LED output every time our count reaches the calculated 
maximum value. In the first process, we’re simply going to increment the count register. Then, 
we’ll check whether count_reg is equal to our max value. When it is, we’ll toggle the led_reg. So 
that’s going to toggle between all zeros and ones and therefore present the blinky LED output 
we’re desiring. So, we’ll add a comment that says “process that will toggle the output every time 
the counter reaches the calculated MAX_V AL.” It’s a good practice to include these kinds of 
comments to make your code look more readable and understandable. Now we want to define 
our actual process. We’ll give it a name – output_proc – because it defines the LED output. So, 
we’ll write output_proc, and use the process keyword. Then, we need to give a signal for sensi -
tivity list. In this case, we want to evaluate the process on every clock cycle, so we’ll include the 
clock signal (Clk). After that, we’ll use the begin and end process pair, ending the process with 
the label output_proc. Inside this process, we’ll check if count_reg is equal to MAX_V AL. We’re 
going to want to toggle the signal led_reg, to give us that blinking effect. These are the processes 
described in the video, and they represent the processes we’ll use in our blinky LED project, as 
shown in the following codes.
-- Architecture
architecture behavior of BLINKY_LED is
constant MAX_VAL: integer := CLK_RATE / BLINK_RATE;
constant BIT_DEPTH: integer := integer(ceil(log2(real(MAX_VALUE))));
signal count_reg: unsigned(BIT_DEPTH – 1 downto 0) := (others => ‘0’);
signal led_reg: std_logic_vector(NUM_LEDS – 1 downto 0) := “0000”;
begin
    -- Assign output LED values
    Led_Out <= led_reg;
    -- Process that increments the counter every rising clock edge
    count_proc: process(Clk)
    begin
    end process count_proc;
    -- Process that will toggle led output every time the counter 
    -- reaches the calculated ‘MAX_VAL’
    output_proc: process(Clk)
    begin
    end process output_proc;
end behavior;

7.4 The VHDL If Statements
 81
7.4   The VHDL If Statements
This section covers VHDL if statements and how to use them within the VHDL language. The 
VHDL if statements are used to conditionally execute sequential statements based on whether a 
given condition evaluates to true or false. For example, if a condition is true, a certain operation 
will be executed. If it’s false, a different operation will be executed. Just like if you’re working 
in C/C++ or almost any type of programming language, the if statement in VHDL works the 
same. Now, when you’re working with the if statement, the caveat is you need to have it in a 
process. You can’t have this statement just flowing in an architecture, because inherently, an if 
statement does form a sequential type of operation. So, in your architecture, you need to have 
all these consecutive assignments where they happen simultaneously. If you have a bunch of 
AND gates or logic gates, they all happen at the same time. However, with an if statement, 
you have your if else and end if, and it has to happen sequentially. So you need to put these in 
a process – that’s an important part of the if statement. When you’re working with them, the 
keywords are if, then, elsif, and else. You know, with else if in the if statement, it is spelled 
“elsif” – there’s no “e,” which is slightly confusing. But when we show you the syntax of how it 
actually works in the VHDL language, you’ll see it, and it’ll make more sense what we’re saying. 
You can test with your statements; you can have multiple “elsif.” So, you can have an if …  
end if where, if it evaluates to true, you’ll operate on that condition, and if not, then you’ll just 
ignore it. You can have an if … else, or you can have an if … elsif … else … end if statement. You 
can have a bunch of elsifs – there’s no limit on that amount. You can also do nested if state -
ments – you can have an if statement and then inside that statement have another if statement. 
When you’re working with if statements, they do infer priority, and what that means is if the 
first condition evaluates to true, then that condition gets executed and exits out of that state -
ment. So, if you have three conditions that you’re checking against and if they’re overlapping 
where the first and third conditions evaluate to true, it doesn’t matter because it’s going to exe -
cute on that first condition and then ignore the next two. So, when designing your logic, place 
the highest-priority condition at the top of the if statement – that’s an important thing to know. 
Figure 7.4 illustrates the syntax for if-then-end if, and if-then-elsif-end if constructs as used 
in VHDL.
So, if we look at the first one, we have our if statement, and then a condition – something that 
must evaluate to either true or false. For example, if we have an unsigned data type, we can say 
if this unsigned data type is greater than or equal to a specific number, then a sequential state -
ment where we set an output or write an input, which is something that we’ll evaluate to a true 
or false. Next, we have the if-then-else structure. In this case, we’re still checking one condition, 
but if that condition evaluates to false, the second sequential statement inside the else block 
if condition then
  sequential_statements
end if;
if condition then
  sequential_statements
else
  sequential_statements
end if;
if condition then
  sequential_statements
  elseif condition then
      sequential_statements
  else
      sequential_statements
end if;
Figure 7.4   The VHDL if statements syntax.

See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

82 7 The Blinky LEDs Project
will execute instead. If the condition evaluates to true, then the first sequential statement will 
be executed. Now, if you have multiple conditions that you would like to check, you’ll need to 
use the if-elsif-else-end if structure. In this case, the first condition is checked, and if that eval -
uates to true, the first sequential statement is executed, and the program exits the conditional 
block – no further conditions are evaluated. However, if the first condition evaluates to false, 
it will check the second condition. If that condition is true, its sequential statement will be 
executed. If both the first two conditions evaluate to false, then the statement in the else block 
will be executed. This structure is very similar to if statements used in most programming lan -
guages, which is a common tool for controlling flow based on logic. Next, go ahead and open 
your blinky LED project. Within that project, we’ll insert the if statements. As mentioned ear -
lier, these statements can only be placed inside a process block. So, inside our count process, 
the first thing we’re going to do is ensure that our operation is performed on the rising edge of 
the clock, as shown in the following codes.
count_proc: process(Clk)
begin
   if rising_edge(Clk) then
   else
   end if;
end process count_proc;
So, this is our first if statement block. If we have a rising edge clock, then we want to do an oper -
ation; otherwise, we want to do another operation. Now, if we look at those codes on falling edge, 
we’re not actually doing anything, so we’re going to get rid of that else condition. We’re going to 
just have a straight if and end if condition as the following codes.
count_proc: process(Clk)
begin
   if rising_edge(Clk) then
   end if;
end process count_proc;
So, we have our if rising edge clock – what do we want to do? Well, we’re going to include a 
second if statement. The first thing we will check is the Reset, which is an input to our design. If 
Reset is equal to zero – or in this case, if it’s equal to a logical low – then we’re going to use a logical 
“or” such that reset is equal to zero or count_reg is equal to MAX_V AL, which we have defined as 
a constant. So, if count register is equal to the max value or reset is equal to zero, then we want to 
perform the following operation, as shown in the following codes.
count_proc: process(Clk)
begin
   if rising_edge(Clk) then
      if((Reset = ‘0’) or (count_reg = MAX_VAL)) then
         count_reg <= (others => ‘0’);
      else
         count_reg <= count_reg + 1;
      end if; 
   end if;
end process count_proc;
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

7.4 The VHDL If Statements
 83
So, we have a nested if – we check if it’s the rising edge of the clock, then we want to see 
if Reset equals zero or count_reg equals MAX_V AL. If that’s the case, we want to perform 
a specific operation – which, in this case, is updating our count_reg. Since count_reg is an 
unsigned data type, we can perform mathematical operations on it. If the condition is true, 
we want it to be equal to zero. To do this, we’ll use the others assignment, because we don’t 
know what size it is. The size of count_reg is defined by BIT_DEPTH, which is derived from 
MAX_V AL, which itself depends on CLK_RATE and BLINK_RATE, all of which are passed 
in through generics. So, since we don’t know the size of it, that forces us to use “others” state -
ment, which is just going to set count_reg equal to zero. Now, if we detect a clock signal, and 
we’re not in a reset or enable state, and if count_reg does not equal MAX_V AL, then we want 
to increment count_reg. That’s done by assigning count_reg to itself plus one. Since count_reg 
is an unsigned data type, we can do an addition of one. So, this is effectively just creating a 
counter where this process is just going to be constantly, continually evaluated every time our 
clock goes off and we’re just going to be counting up count_reg until it equals MAX_V AL, at 
which point we reset it. So, count_reg just goes from zero to MAX_V AL over and over, unless 
reset is active, in which case we stay in a zero or reset state. Next, we have our second process, 
which is the output process (output_proc). We want to do the same thing here –the first thing 
we do is check if it’s the rising edge of the clock, because we want to do everything on either the 
rising or falling edge (in this case, we’re using the rising edge). If we’re on the falling edge, we 
don’t want to do anything. So, we’re not going to use an if-else condition – just an if condition 
for the rising edge. Inside that, we’ll have a second if statement, checking if count_reg equals 
MAX_V AL. This condition could also occur during reset, which is why we want to make sure 
that this is done in a separate process. Although we already check count_reg is equal MAX_
V AL in the first process (count_proc), putting LED toggle logic there would cause the LEDs to 
toggle every time we reset, which we don’t want. That’s exactly what we’re going to do here. 
So, if count_reg equals MAX_V AL, we’ll go ahead and perform the toggle. We don’t include an 
else statement here, because if count_reg does not equal MAX_V AL, we simply don’t want to 
do anything. We’ll then tab over and assign the LED output. We say that led_reg, which is the 
value that gets sent to the actual LEDs, is equal to not led_reg, as shown in the following codes.
output_proc: process(Clk)
begin
   if rising_edge(Clk) then
      if((count_reg = MAX_VAL)) then
         led_reg <= NOT led_reg;
      end if; 
   end if;
end process output_proc;
So, all that’s going to do is take whatever value is on our led_reg and invert it. Since we start 
with all zeros in led_reg, inverting that will result in all ones. The reason we don’t want to specify 
something like “set all to zero” or “set all to one” is because using a single inversion statement is a 
lot simpler and easier. The reason we have a second process is exactly what we discussed earlier – if  
we were to include led_reg in the first process, count_proc, where we’re checking if the reset is 
zero or if count_reg equals MAX_V AL, then every time we reset, we would end up toggling the 
LEDs, which we don’t want to happen. So, that is how you use the if statement in VHDL and how 
we’re using it to conditionally check and blink the LEDs for our blinky LED project. We can now

7.5   The If Statement Priority Example
This section is about the if statement priority example. Here, we’re going to demonstrate how an if 
statement can infer priority. We’re going to simulate a priority encoder. The way a priority encoder 
works is that you have an input, and the output tells you which is the highest bit that is set to ‘1’ . 
For example, if our input is 8 bits wide, ranging from bit 0 to bit 7, and bit 7 is high, then the output 
will be a 3-bit value (from 2 down to 0) that indicates bit 7 is the highest active bit that is set. In 
this case, the output would be 111 to indicate that bit 7 is high. Because if we look at this, we have 
an encode process where we’re processing a_in, and when signal changes, we define our output. 
If our seventh bit is high, our output goes to seven (111). Now, if our seventh bit and sixth bit are 
both high, the encode output just says 111, or tells you seven. It tells you the highest bit or the most 
significant bit that is actually high. So, the idea behind this is that it shows you all of these con-
ditions can evaluate to true. But if our seventh bit is high, that’s the one we want to know. So, the 
way this operates is by using “if” . It’s going to check if bit seven is equal to one, then our encode 
output is equal to 111. Then we’re going to end this if statement – we do not even check the rest of 
the statements, as shown in the following codes.
library IEEE;
use IEEE.STD_LOGIC_1164.ALL;
    
entity Priority_Encoder is
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 101 ---
86 7 The Blinky LEDs Project
Port(
     encode: out std_logic_vector(2 downto 0);
     a_in: in std_logic_vector(7 downto 0));
end Priority_Encoder;
    
architecture behavior of Priority_Encoder is
    
begin
   enc_process: process(a_in)
   begin
       if(a_in(7) = ‘1’) then
           encode <= “111”;
       elsif(a_in(6) = ‘1’) then
           encode <= “110”;
       elsif(a_in(5) = ‘1’) then
           encode <= “101”;
       elsif(a_in(4) = ‘1’) then
           encode <= “100”;
       elsif(a_in(3) = ‘1’) then
           encode <= “011”;
       elsif(a_in(2) = ‘1’) then
           encode <= “010”;
       elsif(a_in(1) = ‘1’) then
           encode <= “001”;
       elsif(a_in(0) = ‘1’) then
           encode <= “000”;
       else
           encode <= (others => ‘X’);
       end if;   
    end process enc_process;   
     
end behavior;
So, if our seventh bit is zero, then we check the next one. If our sixth bit is high, we disregard the 
fifth, fourth, third, second, first, and zeroth bits, setting them to zero. We disregard all of those and 
just say, “OK, our sixth bit is high,” and move on, disregarding the rest. Then we check again: if the 
sixth bit goes low, we say, “OK, that fifth bit is high,” and we just keep going down the line. We’re 
going to simulate this to show how it works. Otherwise, if you just looked at this, you might think 
that if all seven bits are high, every one of these conditions in the statement would evaluate to true, 
which would mean if you go through it, you’d say the encode is equal to 111. But now, our encode 
is equal to 110 – you just keep going down the line. If all your bits are high, your encode would end 
up being zero (000), because the last “elsif” statement would be the one that gets evaluated. That’s 
how it works: you evaluate the conditions one by one, and as soon as you find one that’s true in an 
if-elsif-else type of statement, the rest are skipped. You only execute the code associated with the 
first true condition. In this case, we’re just setting the encode output to some value, and that ends 
our process. So, we’ll go ahead and run the simulation to show what this actually looks like when 
simulated.
We can copy and paste the following test bench code into test_Priority_Encoder.vhd, and it’ll 
update accordingly. Now, it recognizes the test bench as test_Priority_Encoder, which is a top-level 
design, as shown in Figure 7.6.

Now, what we’re going to do is restart the simulation (Ctrl+Shift+F5). We know we only need to 
run it from zero to approximately 100 ns. Next, we’ll set bit seven to high by forcing a constant with a 
binary value of 1. We’ll cancel that force after about 20 ns, as shown in Figures 7.8 and 7.9, respectively.
We’re just going to force it to be high for a short period and then say, “OK, now let’s run it for 
100 ns.” After switching to the full view, we can see that encode out is set to 7, as illustrated in 
Figure 7.10. The reason it’s 7 is because both bit seven and bit zero are high now, and are one.
If we look at our priority encoder, we can see that both bit seven and bit zero are set to one. So, both 
conditions evaluate to true. However, the condition for bit seven comes first; for example, it has higher 
priority. So, the higher the priority you want a bit to have, the earlier you place its conditions in the if 
statement. So, bit seven has the highest priority, but bit six has the second highest priority. Since bit 
seven is a one, the output is set to seven, and it’s going to disregard the rest of the bits. Bit seven has 
the highest priority, which we can see is exactly what’s happening right there. So, our bit seven has 
priority because it’s high, and then as soon as we drop it low, whichever bit is high – in this case, bit 
two – now our encode output is two. So that just shows you this is a very good example explaining how 
these if statements infer priority. So, in this exact example, when you’re designing a priority encoder, 
it makes the most sense. An if statement will make the most sense as a way of implementing that. But 
when you work with different designs, if you have specific situations where you say, “OK, we want

to make sure that there’re multiple conditions that could be true. But if this one is true, then we want 
this to be evaluated,” then the next statement, which has lower priority, should go below. So, the first 
condition you test, the big thing is when you’re working with these statements, the first condition you 
check is your highest priority, in this case, bit seven. The last condition you check, in this case bit zero, 
is the lowest priority, and it just goes in descending order: highest priority, next highest, and all the way 
down to the lowest priority. And that’s an example of how the if statements infer priority.
7.6   The Blinky LEDs Project IO Placement
This section is about the blinky LEDs Project Arty A7 IO placement. Here, we’re going to show you 
where and how to place the specific IOs required for the blinky LEDs project to run on the Arty A7 
board. So, we open up the Arty A7 blinky LEDs project. We’ve already gone through the complete 
design walkthrough and understand the entire VHDL code and what’s going on. The next step is to 
assign the IOs. Then we’ll run the synthesis, run the implementation, and generate the bitstream. Let’s 
go ahead and assign the different IOs. If you open up the attached Arty_A7_Master.xdc file, you’ll see 
that it contains all the different IOs you can work with on the Arty A7 development board. Go ahead

8.1   Introduction
In this chapter, we will review the pulse width modulated (PWM) signals and explain what a PWM 
signal is, because we are going to use PWM signals for the LED brightness project. We’re going 
to use VHDL to generate an actual PWM signal, and the first thing we’re going to explain is what 
PWM stands for. The PWM signal, as mentioned earlier, stands for pulse width modulated signal. 
All this means is that you have a standard digital signal with a value of one or zero, and what 
we’re doing is varying the duty cycle of that specific signal. This technique can be used for various 
applications. The application we’re currently focusing on is using it to vary the brightness of an 
LED. On the Arty A7 board, we have standard LEDs as well as an RGB (red, green, blue) LED. We 
will send these PWM signals to the RGB LED to vary its brightness, allowing us to generate a wide 
range of colors. If you’ve ever worked with colors on a computer, you know that you can mix dif-
ferent parts of red with different parts of green and different parts of blue to create various colors. 
In order to get different intensities of red, blue, or green, you vary the amount of time the signal is 
high versus the time it is low, or vary the duty cycle of each signal being sent to each corresponding 
part of that LED. Another common application of PWM is in servo motors. In robotics or RC air -
planes, you’ll have a servo motor where you send a certain duty cycle of a signal to that motor, 
and that duty cycle corresponds to a specific position. For example, if you have a motor that can 
rotate 180 degrees, you might define zero degrees as a duty cycle of 50% and 180 degrees as a duty 
cycle of 90%. Therefore, by varying the duty cycle of 50% and 90%, you can vary between zero and  
180 degrees. Now, we’ll take a closer look at what an actual PWM signal is. Let us make a few notes 
about PWM. To generate a PWM signal, we know that this is simply where we are varying the duty 
cycle. So, this is a key thing to know – by modulating, we’re just varying the duty cycle. The next 
logical question would be: what is duty cycle? The formula for duty cycle is equal to time on (t on ) 
divided by time on (t on ) plus time off (t off ), and this is for just one period of your signal. So, let’s take 
an arbitrary digital signal that goes high for a certain amount of time, then low, then high again, 
and so on, as shown in Figure 8.1.
So, we know that we have one period, which would be from our first rising edge to our second 
rising edge, and so between this period right here, we have a time on, t on , which is effectively the 
time that your signal is high. So, this is your time on and your time off, as you can see in Figure 8.1. 
So, for example, if we take the signal we have shown right here, and our time on is 50 ms and our 
time off is 50 ms, we can solve for our duty cycle by taking our time on. So, we know that’s 50 ms, 
and we divide that by our time on plus our time off. So, the duty cycle would be equal to 0.5, and 
duty cycle is almost always represented as a percentage. So, that would be equal to a 50% duty cycle 
8
The LED Brightness Project
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. of that signal, and that’s commonly seen in clocks, where you have a signal that’s high just as much 
as it’s low, or 50% duty cycle, you just have a nice square waveform that aligns. Now, if you have 
a signal where the duty cycle varies – for example, let’s say your time on is 70 ms and your time 
off is 50 ms – then we know that our time on is greater than the time off. So, we’re going to have a 
higher duty cycle. Using the same formula, the duty cycle is equal to 0.583. Since we represent it 
as a percentage, that’s equal to 58.3% duty cycle. So, the wider this width is, the brighter the LEDs 
will be. In the case of a standard RGB LED, this controls brightness. Now, if you’re controlling a 
robot, the width of this is going to determine the position of the servo or the motor you’re trying to 
control. And that is an introduction and explanation of PWM.
8.2   The PWM VHDL Design
This section is about the PWM VHDL design. Here, we are going to determine how to design a 
PWM controller in VHDL. We already know that PWM stands for pulse width modulation, and we 
understand that it involves varying the duty cycle of a standard digital signal, where we have a zero 
and one, and that is going to vary the amount of time the signal stays high. So, in order to do this, 
we understand there are two components: the signal frequency and the duty cycle. We’ll be output -
ting a signal at a specific frequency, and that’s an important thing to remember and know. We must 
define the frequency and the duty cycle, which is the key factor. While we can fix the frequency, 
we want the design to allow the duty cycle to be adjustable. This will make the design more flexible 
and reusable for other projects beyond just controlling LED brightness, allowing it to be used in a 
variety of applications. So, in order to do that, we want to make sure we define the frequency as a 
generic and the duty cycle as a port. So, what this allows us to do is, when we create our design via 
the generic, we can, on the fly, specify the duty cycle through the port. This allows us to set the fre -
quency to 50 Hz, but the duty cycle can be changed just on the fly throughout the design. Another 
important thing to note is that we have to define the entity. When we define the entity, we need to 
focus on two key parts: generics and the port. For the generics, we want to have a bit, and this will 
determine how many bits we use to represent the duty cycle. For example, if we had one bit to rep -
resent the duty cycle, we would only be able to represent two cycles: 100% or 0%, because we would 
only have 0 and 1. The more bits we use, the higher the resolution and the finer the granularity we 
can get for both the output duty cycle and the input clock. This is important because it will define 
the input frequency of our clock. For example, on the Arty A7, the input clock would be 100 MHz. 
This clock is used to derive the output frequency that we generate in our design. The frequency is 
going to specify what our output signal should be. Our entity will also define the actual duty cycle 
of that signal, but this is the actual frequency of that signal. For the entity, we need one output: 
the PWM output, which is our pulse width modulated output signal. This is a standard logic signal 
where the duty cycle determines the width. The width of this signal is going to be determined by 
bit depth. We’ll also need a clock input, which is the input clock signal defined by the generic input 
clock frequency. So whatever frequency we specify here will be the frequency of a clock that feeds 
into our design. Additionally, we need an enable input. It’s always good to have the ability to enable 
ton

or disable your design. So, we’re going to make it an active low, meaning that when we pull it high, 
the output will stay low and will no longer be active. So, in order to use this pulse width modulated 
design, we’ll want to have to put a zero on the enable to allow it to actually run. So now let’s take a 
look at how exactly we’re going to implement this. We’ll call these our PWM design notes, which is 
just to outline our ideas so that we know what we actually need to do to generate this PWM design. 
The first thing we want to do is generate a block that explains what’s going on at a high level. We 
will create a block for our PWM design because, initially, we care about the inputs and outputs, 
and later we’ll work on making the inputs produce the desired output. This is our PWM design, as 
shown in Figure 8.2.
We know that one of the key inputs we want to have is the duty cycle. So, this is going to be one 
of our inputs. We’re going to have the duty cycle, and we’re going to have a clock input – the clock 
that comes into our development board. Then, we want to have an enable signal, which allows us 
to enable or disable our design. The clock is going to be a signal that toggles at a very high rate. 
So, it’s going to look something like this – likely with a 50% duty cycle or more. But that doesn’t 
necessarily matter. We’re just going to be looking at the rising or falling edges of that clock signal. 
The important part of our design is the output, which will be defined as PWM_Out. That’s our 
output, and it’s going to have the varying duty cycle. So, that’s where the duty cycle will change 
on the fly, based on the duty cycle input to the design. The amount of time the signal stays high is 
determined by the duty cycle value we input. So, what does that actually mean? How are we going 
to make this happen from our duty cycle right here? Well, let’s first look at a standard waveform. If 
we have a waveform, it will be high for a certain amount of time, then low for a certain amount of 
time, repeating periodically. If we look at the signal, we know it has a specific frequency. Because 
in our generic redefined, we want to be able to set the output frequency regardless of what the duty 
cycle is. So, we need to know the timing between rising edges. So, from one rising edge to the next, 
we’ll have a time interval, and we’ll call this max_freq_count. This is what we’ll use to determine 
the frequency of our PWM design. It’s constant and will be generated based on our input clock and 
the frequency defined in the generic. So, based on the speed of our input clock and the desired fre -
quency, which we’re going to set in our generic, we calculate this max frequency count. The next 
key thing is the actual PWM, or more specifically how long the signal stays high. So, we’re going to 
have a second counter that determines how long our signal is high. We’ll call this PWM_count and 
that’s going to determine this width right here, and that’ll be the same as right there, as depicted 
in Figure 8.3.
Now, pwm_count is going to be a dynamic value that can change because it is defined by our 
duty cycle. What this means is: if we think about it, we’re going to have a signal that is going 
to reset or disabled below. Then, as soon as we enable it, we are going to pull our signal high. 
We’re going to have two processes at the same time: we need to track both pwm_count and max_ 
freq_count. The first thing we’ll look at is the frequency count (max_freq_count). This is derived by 
pulling the signal high, which we’ll call pwm_out, and now, we’ll pull that high at the beginning of 
our max frequency count (max_freq_count). Once we reach our max frequency count again, we’ll

pull that signal high again. If we have 100% duty cycle, this would be high the entire time, and even 
though it’s already high, we’re just going to pull it high once again with our max frequency count. 
So, this count simply tracks up to the max_frequency_count and triggers the pwm_out to go high 
again. Simultaneously, a second process is tracking pwm_count. This counter counts up until our 
pwm_count value is reached, then it’s going to pull our signal low. So, we have two opposite things 
going on: pwm_count, once that’s reached, will pull low, and then our signal will stay low until our 
max frequency count (max_freq_count) is reached. That’s when it’s going to pull up our pwm_out. 
It’ll pull it higher, then our pwm_count is going to count again and it’s going to pull low. So, the 
max_freq_count and the pwm_count are going to start at the same time. They’re both starting on 
this rising edge, and so we have two opposite things going on. You have your max_freq_count, 
which is always pulling high, and pwm_count, which is always pulling our output signal low. So, 
based on the duty cycle, the higher the number, the higher your duty cycle is, and the larger this 
pwm_count value will be. As this pwm_count value reaches all the way to the max_freq_count, 
that’s going to be 100% duty cycle. If you have a 0% duty cycle, your count just won’t occur, and 
you’ll have a signal that stays low all the time. So, the next thing you want to think about is: how 
are we going to handle doing all of this in VHDL? This is a very important thing to think about. 
You’re going to have all kinds of stuff going on. So, we have to worry about our inputs, our outputs, 
and then we know roughly what we want to do inside our architecture. So, the first thing let’s do 
is open up whatever tool you’re using – you’re working with Vivado – so open it up. We’ll want to 
define our libraries. We’re using the IEEE library, just like all of our other designs and projects. 
And we want to figure out what packages we want to use. We know that our output signal is a 
single bit and uses standard logic, so we know we want to use IEEE.STD_LOGIC_1164.ALL. We 
want to use this library to allow us to use the standard logic and standard logic vector data types. 
We also want to use IEEE.numeric_std.All, and this will allow us to use the unsigned and signed 
data types. In this case, we want to use unsigned, as we’re doing our counting and want to perform 
addition, which is a key part. If you’re using just standard logic, it’s hard to do a straight addition 
and comparisons. So now we want to define our entity. We know we’re going to give it a name, and  
we’ll call it PWM. That seems to make the most sense. We know we want to define a generic, and 
for a generic, we know we want to have BIT_DEPTH. This will be an integer data type, and by 
default, we’re just going to give it “8.” That gives us eight bits of resolution, which is a pretty stan-
dard and common number. That allows you to go from 0% to 100% duty cycle across 255 values. 
Next is our input clock (INPUT_CLK), and this is used to determine the clock frequency. This is 
also an integer, and we’re going to give it 50 MHz by default. This value will depend on the clock –  
whatever one you’re working with. For the final generic, we’re going to define FREQ, which is 
the actual output frequency you want to have. This will also be an integer value, and we’ll give 
it a default of 50 Hz to start with. Then we want to define the port, so our port should have the  
following: a PWM output, a duty cycle, a clock, and an enabler. So, we have a PWM output, which 
is a standard logic. We have a duty cycle input, which is of width BIT_DEPTH minus one down to

-- Libraries
library IEEE;
use IEEE.STD_LOGIC_1164.ALL;
use IEEE.numeric_std.all;
-- Entity
entity PWM is
    Generic(
        BIT_DEPTH: integer := 8;  
        INPUT_CLK: integer := 50000000;  -- 50MHz default
        FREQ: integer := 50);   -- 50Hz default
    Port(
        Pwm_Out: out std_logic;
        Duty_Cycle: in std_logic_vector(BIT_DEPTH – 1 downto 0);
        Clk: in std_logic;
        Enable: in std_logic);  -- Active Low
end PWM;
So, there we have the beginning of our PWM design. We know what we want to do – we have our 
libraries and packages defined. Also, our entity is defined, and so the final part of this is to actually 
implement and design the architecture of our PWM design. We are going to finish the PWM design 
we started in this section. We have discussed the design and clarified what we’re trying to achieve. 
At this point, we have the libraries and packages we’re going to use. We have all our comments, and 
we have our entity defined. So, what we need to do is define our architecture. We already know 
what we’re trying to do with our architecture, we just need to define it. So, we make a note to know 
where the architecture begins and use the architecture keyword. We’ll call it behavior, which is a 
common name for the architecture. Next, we define the constants we’ll be using. The first constant 
is max_freq_count, which is the maximum frequency count. Make this an integer and that’s going 
to be equal to the input clock divided by the frequency. So, this is going to tell us what we need to 
count to generate our specific frequency. We know we are working with two things here: frequency 
and PWM or duty cycle. So, the next constant we want to create is pwm_step, which will also be an 
integer. It will be equal to maximum frequency count divided by two to the power of BIT_DEPTH. 
This tells us the size of each step for the PWM, based on how we’re taking our maximum frequency 
count and dividing that by 2 to the power of BIT_DEPTH, or how many different individual steps 
we have inside for our PWM. So, for one-hundredth of a duty cycle, this PWM step is like the 
maximum it can be. So, we now want to define our signals: in the first signal, we have a signal 
PWM_value, and this is a standard logic. We will initialize it to a value of zero. In our second 
signal, we have what we’re calling our frequency_count, and this is an integer. It’s going to be 
ranged from zero to our maximum frequency count. We initialize it to a value of zero. The next 
thing we want to have is our signal PWM_count. We’ll make that an integer as well, and it’s going 
to range from zero to two to the power of BIT_DEPTH, and we’ll initialize it to the value of zero as 
well. We also want to have another signal called max_PWM_count, and that would be in your 
range from zero to two to the power of BIT_DEPTH as well, and in the next signal, we want to cre -
ate one more signal, and this is our PWM_step_count. This is an integer, ranging from zero to 
max_frequency_count, and we initialize it to a value of zero. So now we have our constants and 
signals – all have been defined. We will use the begin statement, and the first thing we want to do 
is have a conversion where we’re going to convert the duty cycle to our maximum_PWM_count.

at the correct frequency. So, we need a process that runs our signal out at the correct frequency. 
Now we’re going to do this entire process as a single process because, ultimately, we care about the 
Pwm_Out, and we don’t want to have multiple processes driving the same signal. So, instead of 
having separate freq_counter and PWM processes, we’ll do everything in one process. However, we 
want to have it in the same process because we’re both trying to drive the Pwm_Out signal. So, we 
will then end our process freq_counter, and we’ll do it in process freq_counter. Now, within this 
process, we first want to do a check, on the rising or falling edge. So, we are just going to pick 
rising_edge, and you could do this on the falling edge, too. There’s no reason for that; we just prefer 
to do on rising edge, and then if we’re not on the rising edge, we don’t want to do anything. So, we’ll 
end that if statement, and the next we want to do is we need to check if our Enable is zero. If our 
Enable is not equal to zero; otherwise, we’re disabled then our PWM value is going to equal to a 
zero, and then we’ll do our end if, so first thing we have going on here is we are checking if we’re 
at the rising edge and at the rising edge of “Clk,” we’re checking to make sure our design is enabled, 
and if our design is not enabled, then we’re going to set our PWM value out to zero. So, if we disable 
it, our PWM value signal is just going to ride low and stay that way. So, the next thing we want to 
do is check if the frequency count is less than our max frequency count. So then if it is, we’ll have 
to do multiple things; if it’s not, then we’re going to set our frequency count to zero and our PWM 
count to zero. So, we need to check if our frequency count is less than our max frequency count; if 
it’s not, then we’re greater than or equal to the max frequency count. So, we need to set our fre -
quency count and set that to zero and we want to set the PWM count to zero as well. So, if we’re 
greater than that, it means we need to do something else. So we need to set them back to zero. Now, 
inside of it, we want to check if our frequency count is left there, max frequency count. The next 
thing we want to do is – within our next if statement, if our frequency count is less than our max 
frequency count, we want to set our frequency count equal to our frequency count plus one – we 
want to increment that frequency count. Now we want to do another check to see if our PWM 
account is less than our max PWM count. So if our PWM count is not less than our PWM max 
count, then we want to set our PWM value to zero. Now, we need to have the parentheses there 
because we know that this is a standard logic data type. So, for PWM, if count is less than our max 
PWM count, then we want to set our PWM value to a one, and that makes sense because if  
our PWM count is less than the max PWM count, we’re high. So, we’re going to count for how long 
we stay high; otherwise we set it down to low. The next thing we want to check is if our PWM step 
count is less than our PWM step, then we want to perform the following and take our PWM step 
count and increment it. So, we’ll say our PWM step count is now equal to our PWM step count and 
add one or increment it. Otherwise, we want to set our PWM step count to a zero and then set 
PWM count to a zero as well. That gives you the entire design process of PWM design, so we end 
our behavior to finalize our design. At this point, this is our PWM design. We have our entity and 
our architecture and in here we’re just checking multiple if statements, because the ultimate goal 
of this is to output the PWM out values. So, it’s going to evaluate this entire if statement logical 
processing and determine what the value of this PWM is, which we care about. So, we’re checking 
if our frequency count is less than max frequency count, then we increment our frequency count.

8.2 The PWM VHDL Design
 99
If our PWM count is less than our max PWM count, then the PWM value stays high. However, if 
it’s not, then the signal goes low. We also check if the Enable is disabled or if the PWM value is 
zero. In addition, the PWM value goes to zero; if our PWM count is less than the max PWM count, 
then our PWM value goes low; it shows this right there. The way this is setting up our PWM value 
is going to be high and low based on the duty cycle, so the input or duty cycle is going to determine 
the amount that our PWM value output signal is high versus how much it’s low. Therefore, the 
codes for our PWM design are as follows.
-- Architecture
architecture behavior of PWM is
-- Constants
constant max_freq_count: integer := INPUT_CLK / FREQ;
constant pwm_step: integer := max_freq_count / (2**BIT_DEPTH);
-- Signals
signal pwm_value: std_logic := ‘0’;
signal freq_count: integer range 0 to max_freq_count := 0;
signal pwm_count: integer range 0 to 2**BIT_DEPTH := 0;
signal max_pwm_count: integer range 0 to 2**BIT_DEPTH := 0;
signal pwm_step_count: integer range 0 to max_freq_count := 0;
begin
    -- Convert Duty_Cycle to max_pwm_count
    max_pwm_count <= to_integer(unsigned(Duty_Cycle));
    Pwm_Out <= pwm_value;
    -- Process that runs signal out at the correct frequency
    freq_counter: process(Clk)
    begin
       if rising_edge(Clk) then
          if (Enable = ‘0’) then
                if (freq_count < max_freq_count) then
                  freq_count <= freq_count + 1;
                  if (pwm_count < max_pwm_count) then
                     pwm_value <= ‘1’;
                     pwm_count <= pwm_count + 1;
                     if (pwm_step_count < pwm_step) then
                         pwm_step_count <= pwm_step_count + 1;
                     else
                        pwm_step_count <= 0;
                        pwm_count <= 0;
                    end if;
                 else
                    pwm_value <= ‘0’;  
                 end if;
              else
                 freq_count <= 0;
                 pwm_count <= 0;
              end if;
            else
               pwm_value <= ‘0’;
            end if;
         end if;
     end process freq_counter;
end behavior;
