8.3   The VHDL Generate Statement
This section is about the VHDL generate statement; in this lecture, we’re going to explain how the 
generate statement is used in the VHDL language. In this case, we’re going to be using the gen-
erate statement and a design for a counter, which is going to be used in our LED brightness project, 
and so what is a generate statement used for VHDL? Well, the definition of it is a mechanism for 
iterative or conditional elaboration of a portion of a description. Now, that sounds kind of con-
fusing, but basically what it’s doing is it allows you to iteratively place an object so you can use it 
in two different types of schemes in a for loop as well as an if statement, and generate statements 
consist of three main parts. These include a generation scheme, and that is either an if statement 
or a for loop, and then you have a declarative part and then concur concurrent statements. So, for 
example, if you have a design you want to replicate multiple times in a row, you can use a generate 
statement to do that. If you have a circuit that takes a lot of multiple components and just copy 
and paste, a for loop would be a way to do that. If you want to have some sort of condition where 
you’re checking a value such as and your generic, you have a value that’s telling you whether you 
do a synchronous or asynchronous type of operation; you would use it to generate an if statement. 
So, we’ll go through the examples of both of these. The next thing we will look at is the structure 
of a generate statement. So, the structure of a generate statement is the first part, where you have 
a label, and this is just any name you want to give it. That explains what this generate statement 
is doing. First, you use a colon and then you’ll have a for keyword for the generate statement and 
then you specify the parameter. There’s a keyword in, followed by the range. After that, you use the 
keyword generate. Then, you can include declarations, which are optional. That might be signals 
or constants that you plan to use. Then you have the begin statement, the begin keyword, and then 
the concurrent statements underneath the begin. After that, you have the end generate keyword, 
which is part of begin, and end pair, and you put the label that you’ve used to describe your gen-
erate statement. Now, the other way, if you’re using the generate an if type of condition, you still 
have your same label, your colon, then you have the if keyword, and you put the condition and the 
keyword generate, and then underneath that you have your declarations, which are your signals 
or constants. If you’re using those, those are optional and then you need the begin keyword, and 
underneath the begin keyword, you put your concurrent statements and then at the end generate 
and the label of whatever label you use to label that specific generate statement. Let’s take a look at 
some examples of what these look like. So, the first example we’re going to look at is the for loop. 
So, if we have a for loop, this will say a for loop generate statement because we know we have a for

loop, and if so, we’ll go with the for loop first, for loop example, and for this example, we are going

have or draw three of them, but we know we want multiple of these, so we’ll say up to n – 1, we’re 
going to use N to describe the number of AND gates we want to have 0, 1, …, N – 1. We have our 
inputs and we have our outputs. So, in order to place all of these AND gates, we could go in VHDL 
and specifically have the logical AND operation for N times, or what we can do is use a generate 
statement, which is a lot cleaner and easier in VHDL, because we could do this in just a couple of

place. Now, this example is for just an AND gate for simplicity, but it could be any design. It could 
be a completed design, like when we generate our counter, we could use a for loop to place AND

At a high level, we’re looping through from 0 to N times, generating AND gates, and for the first 
time, we have our zero AND gate, one AND gate, all the way up to the N-1 AND gate. So, this whole 
example here is just showing that you can place a lot of different AND gates by using a simple three 
lines of code, whereas if you would have to, if this N number is, say, 10 or 12, it could possibly take 
10 or 12 lines of code and your VHDL design versus just doing this in three lines. If you want to 
adjust the amount of AND gates replacing or whatever design it is, you just have to change this  
N value, and its N value could be a constant in your design. It could be a generic or whatever way 
you decide to pass this into your VHDL design. This is a number that you can change to change 
the number of AND gates you’re going to be creating in your design. So, the next example we want 
to take a look at is the if example. So, within our design, if we want to use an if example for the 
generate, we’ll call this our if example; let’s say we have a design that could use a JK flip-flop or a 
D flip-flop. For example, if we have a design, this could be any type of design, we have our J input, 
clock, and K input, which is just a JK flip-flop. We have our Q output and Q-not, and we also have 
a D-full flop. So, we have two different designs and this was our D input and our clock input. Then 
we have our Q and Q-not outputs. It’s a D flip-flop, so we could do this in several different ways. We 
could have two different designs: one that utilizes the JK flip-flop and the other that utilizes the D 
flip-flop and save them off as two VHDL designs, or we can have a single VHDL design that con -
tains both the JK-flip flop option and the D flip-flop option. The way we would do that is by using 
a generate example and using the if configuration. So, if we wanted to do this, we would have, for 
example, our design, where we’d have our entity and it could be whatever we decide, or inputs and 
outputs, or whatever our design is, then we’d have our architecture. Inside our architecture, we are

nothing crazy happening with a counter. You’re just effectively having a clock set on coming in

and you’re counting that clock signal. Now, what we want to do is do a little bit more than that. 
So, we have our standard counter, but we want to add a few more features to it. So, there are a 
couple of parameters or specifications we’d like to have. The first one being that we want to be 
able to have a counter be synchronous or asynchronous with regard to the reset. We’re going to 
count on the clock edge, but for the reset, we want to have an asynchronous or synchronous type 
of reset. A synchronous reset means that if you’re counting up, it won’t go to zero until the rising 
edge of the clock. An asynchronous reset, however, will go to zero as soon as the button or your 
reset signal is pressed, and so we’ll talk about how to design a counter to count for both of these 
different options. We want to have a configurable maximum count value. When you’re working 
with counters, one of the key things you need to do is understand what your maximum count value 
is. When you’re working with a counter, the goal is to create some sort of delay, and that delay is 
going to be defined by how high you count to. So, you have your input clock rate and you’re going 
to count to a certain value, which will equate to a certain time delay, and we also obviously want 
to be able to reset the counter if maximum value is, say, 100; we want to be able to, at any point 
in time, get to 50, be able to hit reset, and have our counter go back down to zero again. So, it can 
also be used like a watchdog timer where you have this counter that counts. Every time you per -
form a certain operation to check on your watchdog timer, you’ll just have to reset and know that, 
if we ever get at our time or ever reach the maximum value, we know we have an issue. So, we 
need to shut things down or generate a warning like a watchdog timer fault. Obviously, the most 
important thing is to have an output to indicate that our maximum count is reached. So, we’ll 
have an output that is zero, except more just count based on the input clock we have coming in. 
Once we reach the maximum count value, then our output will flag high and toggle to say, “Hey, 
I reached the maximum count now and then go ahead and start cycle over and bring our count to 
a zero and continue again.” So effectively, the output is going to be toggling high one clock cycle, 
every time it reaches the maximum count. Now, the important thing to think is in order to do this, 
we’re going to need to figure out how we are going to work with the generic and the entity. So, for 
the generic, we’re going to have a maximum value, which will be our maximum count value. We 
have a synchronous reset, and this is going to determine whether or not if we reset synchronously 
or asynchronously and this will be a Boolean value. So, it’s going to be a true or false to deter -
mine what type of reset we’re going to do, and that’s key for our design. Then, our entity is going 
to contain three different inputs and outputs. So, we have one output, which is our maximum 
clock count and our max count, and this will toggle high when we achieve our maximum value, 
which we define in our generic, and then we have our clock and reset. Our clock is whatever input 
clock you’re bringing in, which will most likely be the system clock on your board. So, it’ll be the  
100 MHz for Arty A7 development board. The reset determines when we reset the counter, and this 
is going to be correlated with our synchronous reset. If we set our synchronous reset to true, we 
want to have our reset take effect on the rising clock edge. If our synchronous reset is set to false, 
we want to reset to take effect as soon as we hit that reset value.
So, let’s take a look and go through and understand our counter design. For our counter design 
to get an idea of what we want to do, let’s just call this our counter design. The key thing for this 
is to understand the synchronous versus asynchronous reset. As far as indicating a maximum 
value in account, we know that we can have a process that evaluates on the clock and every 
rising edge increment. So, what we need to understand is: how does a synchronous versus asyn -
chronous reset occur? First, we’re going to create a signal that is going to be our clock and we’ll 
try to make it a 50% duty cycle and just a clock signal. So, we’ll just make several pulses here to 
show what that clock signal will look like. We’re going to have this be a steady signal that doesn’t 
ever change. It’s coming in from the board, from an external oscillator, so we won’t really ever

mine if it’s the rising edge of the reset, and you’re going to have to go low or reset your count. So 
that’s the two biggest differences; we’re going to have two separate processes: one for the asynchro -

going to use is the generate statement, because we’re going to have a generate, which checks if 
asynchronous or synchronous is true. This goes back to our generic value synchronous reset. If we 
set that to true, we’re going to generate a synchronous counter or a process that looks only at the 
clock and evaluates the reset inside that process. If our synchronous reset is false, then we need to 
have two processes where we look at the two signals that are our stimulus: the clock and the reset. 
If the reset changes state, we have to reset right away. So that is kind of the difference between 
asynchronous and synchronous clock cycles, and that’s exactly what we’ll be implementing in our

input and it is a standard logic, and our third is a reset (Reset), and this is an input and it’s a stan-
dard logic as well. So that gives us for our generic we have maximum value and synchronous reset 
and for our port we have our maximum count, our clock, and our reset. So, we’ll go ahead and do 
that end COUNTER. So, our counter entity has been officially defined. Now we want to define the 
architecture, so architecture and we would give it a name behavior of the word of COUNTER is, 
now we need to define any constants or signals we’re going to use so we have a constant. We want 
to use that here constant, and we are going to call that bit_depth and we will make it an integer and 
for this constant, we are going to calculate and determine the number of bits required based on our 
MAX_V AL. So right now, it’s going to be 30 because we’re taking you to 2 to the power of 30. But 
this max value can change and so we don’t know how it’s going to be 30. So, we want to make sure 
we don’t want to have more bits that are required just due to resources. So, we’ll say go to 
integer(ceil(log2(real(MAX_V AL)))). So that’s going to give us the number of bits required to count 
to the MAX_V AL, and we’ll go ahead and do a signal, which we will call Count_reg, for our 
count-register, and this will be an unsigned data type, and that’s because this will give us the flexi-

bit_depth minus one down to zero, and we are going to initialize that to a value of zero, so we will

library IEEE;
use IEEE.STD_LOGIC_1164.All;
use IEEE.math_real.all;
use IEEE.numeric_std.all;
entity COUNTER is
    Generic(
         MAX_VAL: integer := 2**30;
         SYNCH_Reset: boolean := true);
    Port(
         Max_Count: out std_logic;
         Clk: in std_logic;
         Reset: in std_logic);
end COUNTER;
architecture behavior of COUNTER is
    constant bit_depth: integer := integer(ceil(log2(real(MAX_VAL))));
    signal Count_reg: unsigned(bit_depth – 1 downto 0) := (others => ‘0’);
    begin
    synch_rst: if SYNCH_Reset = true generate
        Count_proc: process(Clk)
        begin
           if rising_edge(Clk) then
              if((Reset = ‘0’) or (Count_reg = MAX_VAL)) then
                 Count_reg <= (others => ‘0’);
              else
                  Count_reg <= Count_reg + 1;
              end if;
           end if;
        end process;
    end generate;
    asynch_rst: if SYNCH_Reset = false generate
        Count_proc: process(Clk, Reset)
        begin
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 124 ---
8.4 The Counter Design
 109
           if (Reset = ‘0’) then
              Count_reg <= (others => ‘0’);
           elsif rising_edge(Clk) then
               if(Count_reg = MAX_VAL) then
                  Count_reg <= (others => ‘0’);
               else
                   Count_reg <= Count_reg + 1;
               end if;
           end if;
        end process;
     end generate;
     output_proc: process(Count_reg)
     begin
         Max_Count <= ‘0’;
         if(Count_reg = MAX_VAL - 1) then
             Max_Count <= ‘1’;
         end if;
      end process;
end behavior;

We created a quick simulation just to show you what this counter looks like. So, we have our

8.5   The VHDL Component Instantiations
This section is about VHDL component instantiations. Here, we’re going to explain what a compo -
nent instantiation is, give an example of how it can be used, and show how to apply it in our LED 
brightness project. At this point, we have a PWM design as well as a counter design, and we want to 
take both of those individual VHDL designs and use them in our LED brightness project. First, let’s 
start off with what actually is a component instantiation; these allow your VHDL designs to take 
designs from other files. For example, if you have a project, whether it’s in Vivado or any other type 
of development tool, and you have multiple designs, you’ll need a top-level design. This top-level 
design is where you map your individual IOs. From that entity, you’re going to map to all the dif-
ferent IOs on the FPGA you’re using. However, you may have other designs written in separate 
VHDL files that are embedded inside your design. This approach follows a hierarchical structure: 
you have a top-level design, and within it, you can include or ‘pull in’ other sub-designs. These 
sub-designs don’t actually instantiate the entities, and those entities don’t actually get mapped to 
physical IO pins from the .xdc file or whatever type of the pin mapping file. Those can actually be 
mapped to signals inside your design, and this allows you to instantiate the design multiple times. 
For example, if we have created a counter module, we can use it multiple times in our design – say, 
20 counters – while only needing one VHDL file to define that counter; we simply instantiate it  
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 126 ---
8.5 The VHDL Component Instantiations 111
20 times. Now, when you instantiate it multiple times, you will use more resources. So, it’s going to 
be like you’re just taking, copying, and pasting that design several times. You just no longer write it 
out 20 different times. So, it saves you in that aspect and makes your designs a little nicer, and a lot 
of times when you’re working with more complex and bigger designs, this will become more and 
more relevant, especially if you’re working on a big project and you have multiple people designing. 
You have somebody working on this portion. They’ll say, OK, make this design, then we’ll instan-
tiate that component here. You’ll instantiate that component here, and you’ll work on the top-level 
or the wrapper, if you will, and take all those designs in time together into one VHDL design. When 
you define when you’re working with this component instantiations, there are several parts to it. 
First, you need to define the component, hence the name “component instantiation.” This is done 
before the architecture or inside the architecture, specifically before the begin and the declarative 
statement section. The component definition outlines its inputs and outputs. So then inside your 
architecture, if you want to use that component, you then just go ahead and do a port map and a 
generic map to map signals to go into that actual component itself. Next, we’re going to take a look 
at an example of this with some notes. The component instantiation is the term we call to repre-
sent a second design inside our design. For example, if we look at a block diagram, like the one 
shown in  Figure  8.9  , we can say this represents our top-level VHDL design. Let’s say one of them is 
called Example.vhd. So, this is a VHDL design that is defined somewhere in our project, but maybe 
somebody else wrote it, and we have a couple of inputs to it. It has two inputs and one output. The 
output gets mapped and comes outside of our top-level VHDL design. For the inputs, let’s assume 
we’re working with something like a JK flip-flop. This is just an example to illustrate the point. So, 
we might have signals like K, Clk, and J as inputs, and outputs like Q or Q-not. These inputs would 
be mapped to the external inputs, so we have these three inputs. Then we have another one, which 
we’ll call test.vhd. This is a second design that also uses the clock signal and has one input. It pro-
duces two outputs that we then we do AND together to form our final output. So, in our top-level 
VHDL design, we are instantiating two components: the first one is Example.vhd and the second 
is test.vhd. These are two separate VHDL files. Including the top-level file itself, we now have three 
VHDL files in total. The top-level file can be named something like Top.vhd. This Top.vhd file 
serves as the highest level in our hierarchy. It defines all the inputs and outputs that get mapped to 
the physical IO pins on the actual FPGA device. For example, these four inputs we see on the left 
and the two outputs we see on the right are shown in  Figure  8.9  .     
 The way this work is if we’re doing this within our VHDL design, we’re not going to write the 
entire design at once, but what we’re going to do is define the main parts. First, we have our 
entity, and within our entity, we define both generics and ports. Then, we have our architecture, 
and within our architecture, we would define the components. In this case, we would define the

Example.vhd and test.vhd components. By defining them, we’re going to do is to define what the 
inputs and outputs are. The actual design itself will be in separate files that contain the architecture 
definition. Here, we are also specifying the inputs and outputs, along with any constants or signals 
that will be used within the architecture. Then, within the architecture, we use the begin state -

The VHDL design is quite useful. As mentioned earlier, when you’re working with more com -
plex designs and utilizing multiple designs, it’s valuable to have a structured approach. This 
is especially true when you have components that you frequently use. For example, we have a 
counter and a PWM design that we use in our LED brightness lab. These two components can be 
reused in many other labs or whenever you need PWM. Once the design is completed, you can 
simply instantiate it in any of your other projects, saving time and effort. When designing compo -
nents or working on your own designs, it’s always a good idea to think about how you can make 
them more flexible, so they might be reusable in the future. Now, let’s open up the LED brightness 
project. Within the project, you’ll see that we already have the initial setup: descriptions, com -

the architecture name is already provided, along with the components to instantiate. Signals and 
constants have also been defined. The first step is to define the components. The first component 
we’re going to define is the PWM design. The key here is that defining a component is very sim -
ilar to an entity, except we use the keyword component. Then, we refer to the entity of our design. 
For example, in PWM.vhd, we can see that the entity name is PWM. So that is the name we want

-- Libraries
library IEEE;
use IEEE.STD_LOGIC_1164.ALL;
use IEEE.numeric_std.ALL;
use IEEE.math_real.ALL;
-- Entity
entity LED_brightness is
  Generic (
      INPUT_CLK: integer := 100000000; -- 50 MHz default
      NUM_RGB_LEDS: integer := 4;
      NUM_LEDS: integer := 4); 
  Port (
      Led_Out: out std_logic_vector(NUM_LEDS - 1 downto 0);
      Clk: in std_logic;
      LED_Enable: in std_logic);  -- Active Low 
end LED_brightness;
-- Architecture
architecture behavior of LED_brightness is
-- Components
-- Pulse Width Modulation Design
component PWM
   Generic (
      BIT_DEPTH: integer := 8;
      INPUT_CLK: integer := 50000000; -- 50MHz
      FREQ: integer := 50); -- 50Hz
   Port (
      Pwm_Out: out std_logic;
      Duty_Cycle: in std_logic_vector(BIT_DEPTH - 1 downto 0);

8.5 The VHDL Component Instantiations
 115
      Enable: in std_logic);

-- COUNTER
component COUNTER
   Generic (
     MAX_VAL: integer := 2**30;
     SYNCH_Reset: boolean := true);
   Port (
     Max_Count: out std_logic;
     Clk: in std_logic;
     Reset: in std_logic);
end component COUNTER;
constant LED_MAX_COUNT: integer := INPUT_CLK / 85;
constant SYN_RESET: boolean := true;
constant MAX_LED_DUTY: integer := 255;
signal led_max_cnt: std_logic := ‘0’;
signal led_pwm_reg: std_logic := ‘0’;
signal led_counter_rst: std_logic := ‘0’;
signal led_duty_cycle: unsigned(7 downto 0) := (others => ‘0’);
begin
-- Assign Outputs
   Led_Out <= (others => led_pwm_reg); 
-- Invert enable signal for counter
   led_counter_rst <= not LED_Enable;
-- LED Counter
   LED_COUNTER: COUNTER 
   generic map(LED_MAX_COUNT, SYN_RESET) 
   port map(Max_Count => led_max_cnt, Clk => Clk, Reset => led_counter_rst);
-- LED PWM Count update Process
   Led_Count_Proc: process(Clk)
   begin
     if rising_edge(Clk) then
        if (led_duty_cycle = MAX_LED_DUTY) then
           led_duty_cycle <= (others => ‘0’);
        elsif (led_max_cnt = ‘1’) then
           led_duty_cycle <= led_duty_cycle + 1;
        end if;
     end if;
   end process Led_Count_Proc;
end behavior;

-- Signals and Constants

constant LED_MAX_COUNT: integer := INPUT_CLK / 85;

constant SYN_RESET: boolean := true;

signal led_max_cnt: std_logic := ‘0’;
signal rgb_max_cnt: std_logic := ‘0’;
signal led_pwm_reg: std_logic := ‘0’;
signal red_pwm_reg: std_logic := ‘0’;
signal grn_pwm_reg: std_logic := ‘0’;
signal blu_pwm_reg: std_logic := ‘0’;

signal rgb_duty_cycle: unsigned(11 downto 0) := (others => ‘0’);

-- Assign Outputs
   Led_Out <= (others => led_pwm_reg);
   RGB_R_Out <= (others => red_pwm_reg);
   RGB_G_Out <= (others => grn_pwm_reg);
   RGB_B_Out <= (others => blu_pwm_reg);
 
  -- Invert enable signal for counter
  rgb_counter_rst <= not RGB_Enable;
  led_counter_rst <= not LED_Enable;

  LED_COUNTER: COUNTER 
  generic map(LED_MAX_COUNT, SYN_RESET) 
  port map(Max_Count => led_max_cnt, Clk => Clk, Reset => led_counter_rst);
   
  -- RGB Counter
  RGB_COUNTER: COUNTER 
  generic map(RGB_MAX_COUNT, SYN_RESET) 
  port map(Max_Count => rgb_max_cnt, Clk => Clk, Reset => rgb_counter_rst);
    
 -- LED PWM Signal Generator (8 bit, 50Hz)
  LED_PWM: PWM
  generic map(8, INPUT_CLK, 50)
  port map(led_pwm_reg, std_logic_vector(led_duty_cycle), Clk, LED_Enable);
   
  -- RGB LED PWM Signal Generator [RED] (4 bit, 50Hz)
  RGB_RED_PWM: PWM
  generic map(4, INPUT_CLK, 50)

  port map(red_pwm_reg, std_logic_vector(rgb_duty_cycle(3 downto 0)), Clk, RGB_

-- RGB LED PWM Signal Generator [GREEN] (4 bit, 50Hz)
  RGB_GRN_PWM: PWM

  port map(grn_pwm_reg, std_logic_vector(rgb_duty_cycle(7 downto 4)), Clk, RGB_

 -- RGB LED PWM Signal Generator [BLUE] (4 bit, 50Hz)
  RGB_BLU_PWM: PWM
  generic map(4, INPUT_CLK, 50)
  port map(blu_pwm_reg, std_logic_vector(rgb_duty_cycle(11 downto 8)), Clk, RGB_
Enable);
   
 -- LED PWM Count update Process
  Led_Count_Proc: process(Clk)
  begin
  if rising_edge(Clk) then
    if (led_duty_cycle = MAX_LED_DUTY) then
      led_duty_cycle <= (others => ‘0’);
    elsif (led_max_cnt = ‘1’) then
      led_duty_cycle <= led_duty_cycle + 1;
    end if;
  end if;
end process Led_Count_Proc;
    
-- RGB PWM Count Update Process
Rgb_Count_Proc: process(Clk)
begin
  if rising_edge(Clk) then
     if (rgb_duty_cycle = MAX_RGB_DUTY) then
        rgb_duty_cycle <= (others => ‘0’);
     elsif (rgb_max_cnt = ‘1’) then
        rgb_duty_cycle <= rgb_duty_cycle + 1;
     end if;
   end if;
end process Rgb_Count_Proc;
     
end behavior;

ness that gives you the completed project for your LED brightness. At this point, you’re ready to 
assign your IO and start your synthesis for your implementation and generating your bitstream or 
programming file to run this on your development board.

This section is about the LED brightness and Arty A7 IO placement. In this section, we’re 
going to show you how to place the IO for the Arty A7 development board. So, for this design, 
if we look at the entity, we can see that we have Led_Out, which is a standard logic vector

from the number of LEDs minus one down to zero. Now, on the Arty A7, we know that we 
have four regular LEDs and four RGB LEDs. We also have an input clock (INPUT_CLK)

Now, copy LED_Enable and we’re going to make that switch zero, just so we have them on 
opposite sides. You can put them on any slide switch you want. Just note that whichever 
switch you tie RGB_Enable and LED_Enable to will determine which switches control the 
RGB LEDs or the standard LEDs. Go ahead and do that. So now we have the clock, which we 
will go ahead and do next. Copy the clock. If we go to the .xdc file, we have the clock signal 
up top, and they define it as clock 100 MHz. We will go ahead and place that and just change 
the clock. On the second line, we’re going to create a clock, give it a name sys_clk_pin, and 
leave the period and waveform as default. Because by default, it describes the 100 MHz clock 
that is already coming down the Arty A7 board, so we don’t want to change that. We’ll high -
light both lines and click on the toggle comments. So now we have our clock. We’ve set the 
property for the specific pin, the clock is coming in on, and we’ve also created the syntax so 
that when it goes through the timing of the design, it’ll make sure that the clock can come 
in at 100 MHz with no issues. Now, in our design, we have RGB_B_OUT, which is the blue 
output of our RGB LED. Since we have four different RGB LEDs, the RGB_B_OUT has to  
be associated with each one of them. So, if we go to the master .xdc file for the RGB LEDs, 
we have LED zero, one, two, and three. Since we’re taking the RGB_B_OUT, which will be  
the blue output, we want to copy and paste that in place of the corresponding blue lines. 
So we want to take that and paste it into our blue, and we’ll set that to zero. Then we’ll go 
down to the next blue line for led1_b, paste it, and assign that to one. Then we move to the 
next blue line for led2_b, paste it, and assign that to RGB_B_OUT[2]. Finally, we paste again 
and again it in place of led3_b using RGB_B_OUT[3]. That gives us our blue RGB outputs: 
RGB blue output zero, one, two, and three. Now, for each of those lines, we’ll go ahead 
and uncomment them. So, with those four lines uncommented, we now have the blue LED 
output defined for the RGB LED. Next, we want to configure the green outputs. So, we copy 
RGB_G_OUT, and we’ll repeat the exact same steps we did for blue, just now for green. So, 
everywhere we have the green output set to zero, we’ll paste and make that zero because it’s 
a standard logic vector. Then we set the next one to one, then paste and set the next to two, 
and paste again to set the third element. After that, we go ahead and remove the comments 
from those lines. Now, we move on to defining the red output (RGB_R_OUT). So, copy that, 
and for red we want to: set our LED zero, make it the zero, then LED one, making that one, 
our LED two, make that two, and our LED three, make that value three. Then we go ahead 
and remove the hashtag for the comment at the beginning of all these, so that we have all 
of the RGB LEDs defined. We now only have to define the Led_Out, which we know – if we 
copy our number of LEDs – we only have four of them. So, we know that we will go from 
three down to zero. If we go to our LEDs, we have three to zero. So, in the first part of that, 
we just want to replace “led” with Led_Out, and we’ll do that for all four of them there. We 
can then just highlight the whole chunk and hit the toggle comment, and click on the Save 
button. So that gives us all of the IO that have now been placed for the Arty A7 on the LED 
brightness project, as shown in Figure 8.11.

9.1   Introduction
In this chapter, the UARTs are explained, and we’re going to explain what UART is and how it’s 
used at a detailed level. In this chapter, we have the UART demonstration where we are going 
to just try and use one of these development boards. They have a UART port, or they support 
UART to USB conversion, so they’ll take that, and you plug it into your computer. When we 
have a serial port emulator, we can send data back and forth from our computer to the FPGA or 
actual design, and when we do that, we want to explain and understand what you can see at a 
detailed level – what’s going on, what bits are being transmitted to send certain pieces of data. 
So first, let’s ask: what does UART stand for? If you work with microcontrollers or if you work 
in embedded systems or with computers, you’ve probably heard of UART before doing any sort 
of embedded systems type of application. And what that stands for is universal asynchronous 
receiver/transmitter. What that means is that it’s asynchronous, and by universal, it means you 
can use this for various transmission layers. So, if you want to go with RS-232, RS-485, 10BUS, 
or any of the other physical layers, you can do that with UART – you just need a transceiver. So, 
it’s universal in that it’s most commonly used. You send it into a different hardware chip; the 
mindset says it has a UART port; you go ahead and connect using this interface, and connect to 
it – the chip will transfer the data and send it out through RS-485 or whatever type of trans -
ceiver it is. Asynchronous means you can transmit and receive at the same time. There’s no 
clock source that is synchronized to a lot of communication protocols. When you’re working 
with them, you’ll have a specific clock source that you’re running at, and you need to have your 
data aligned with that clock source or you’ll send a clock and your data is correlated directly to 
that clock source. With UART, there’s none of that. There are only two signals: Rx and Tx. 
Asynchronous means Rx and Tx can be happening at the same time, and they’re not correlated 
by a clock at all. Obviously, you have the receiver and transmitter, meaning you have the Rx, or 
receiver, and Tx, or transmitter, and what do we actually use this for? So, if you use microcon -
trollers or development boards, you’ll hear this term UART a lot. What do you actually use that 
for? It is just a common way to communicate between devices. In this case, we’re going to use 
it to communicate between the FPGA development board and your computer. You can also use 
it to communicate between individual devices. If you have like two development boards and 
you want them to talk back and forth, you can use UART to do that. It’s a predefined language 
to talk back and forth – just like we’re talking to you in English, UART is predefined in  
these devices to both understand and communicate that way. These are most commonly found 
inside the microcontrollers or in integrated circuit chips. You’ll see them when you’re searching 
9

on DigiKey or any kind of computer distributor. If you’re building your circuit board and 
looking for different interfaces, UART is commonly listed as one of those interfaces. You can do 
this in various ways. You can implement UART using VHDL by physically describing when to 
set your bits high or low and do all of that the way we’re going to use it. Because on the Arty A7 
board, the UART circuitry is connected to a transceiver chip, so it takes that UART signal and 
converts it to USB and sends it to a computer. So, you just plug that USB in, and those pins are 
already physically tied. So, we can’t just map it to whatever we want. So, inside the actual 
FPGA, we are going to use a softcore processor or a hardcore processor to generate this data. 
That means we’ll be writing C code to process and transmit data. So, we’ll use a softcore pro -
cessor, which, if you’re using Xilinx, is the MicroBlaze, and we’ll use the different tools in there 
to write specific code that tells it to send out data. We’re going to explain here what that data 
looks like, and if you put a scope on it, you would see what those waveforms look like and what 
that actually means. So, there are some common things that let you know what UART is. There 
are two signals: Tx for transmitting, and Rx for receiving. As mentioned earlier, you can transmit 
and receive at the same time. It’s a full duplex system and there’re no clock signals to synchro -
nize. You just have these two signals. We’ll show you what that signal looks like. When you’re 
sending data, it’s transmitted in a predefined sequence. This allows UART communication to 
work without needing to interpret each condition individually. This means you have a pre -
defined sequence, meaning you have a start bit, a stop bit, a parity bit, and data bits. You can 
also define whether you’re transmitting little endian or big endian. Now, let’s take a look at how 
this actually looks like and what this will look like if you did have a scope or some sort of equip -
ment for that UART data. We’re going to take some UART notes. Just so when we go back, it 
gives us something we can refer to. So, if we take a signal and we are just going to generate an 
arbitrary signal, that’s going to toggle between 3.3 V and 0 V . So, when we’re idle, we’re going 
to say we’re high, then we’ll toggle it low for a specific amount of time, and then it’ll go back 
high and stay for a while. We’ll define this specific duration as the bit time, which is determined 
by the baud rate. Once we draw this entire signal, we’ll explain how to calculate this bit time or 
baud rate when you’re working with UART. That’s a common term you’ll hear: bit rate. So, we’ll 
get in to explain that. This first bit you’ll see is what we call our start bit, and afterward, we’ll 
explain how this all ties in. So, you have your start bit, which is defined when the line is idle or 
there’s no data being transmitted. You’re at your high level, which we’ll say is 3.3 V . It could be 
5 V or whatever logic will be running at near low 0 V , so we’ll stay high here, and we’ll say that 
we come down, that is the same. So, every time, we have the space between all of these dash 
lines, we’re not measuring exactly, but we’re going to say that’s all the same exact amount of 
time. So, the start bit is equal to this line is high for the equivalent of two start bits. So, here’s 
another time division that we’ll call when we have another time division, and the third time 
division comes to high. This is our start bit. Now, since this is high during this time division, 
we’re going to give that a value of one, the logic value of one. Now, that is low during that time 
division, so this gets a logic value zero, as well as two ones and three zeros. Now we’re going to 
say we’re high for one bit time. So, that gets a logic value of one and then we’re going to pull low 
again. Now, our next bit time has a logic value of zero, and the next one, because our next time 
division is the logic value of zero, and now at this point, we can see we have our start bit, and if 
we’re transmitting little endian, that just means we’re transmitting with our least significant bit 
first. So, we’re going to assume that this is transmitting little endian, and you can have little 
endian or big endian, and a little endian just means you’re the first bit you transmit at least a 
little bit. So, we’re going to write in top-level design what these are. So, that would be a bit zero, 
bit one, and so on until the bit seven, so we know from zero to seven, which gives us eight bits

9.1 Introduction
 127
and that’s another parameter that’s defined in UART protocols for us as little endian. Then it’s 
eight data bits, and that tells us that, OK, the first bit we see is that zero, because we’re a little 
endian. For a big endian, that would be seven, six, and all the way down to zero. So, we have a 
little endian, eight data bits, and we’re going to say we have even parity, and what parity is, 
there is a basic form of error correction. So, since we have even parity, that means in our trans -
mission of data, we have eight data bits; we need to give a parity bit that’s going to determine 
the number of ones we have, and we want to make them even. In this case, we have one to three 
ones, and since we’re even parity, we need to add a one to make it even, so we have four ones. 
So, we’ll take and add one, and this is our parity bit. So, it’s not part of our data, but it’s used as 
an error checking. For example, if our parity bit is set to zero, we would throw these data bits 
away, because we’re like, we’re even parity, but we only have three ones, including a parity of it. 
If this was a zero, we would then just discard this message saying, OK, something got corrupted 
along the lines, please retransmit, we don’t trust that, so that becomes a one, and then we have 
a stop that would just stop, and it is defined by a high. So, that’s just our bit time. Then we have 
another bit time, and this tells us this is our stop bit. So then after that, after our stop bit, our 
line is going to stay high and then wait to see if we’re receiving, and then we wait to see until 
we drop low again till we get another start. But once we get another start, we’ll start this whole 
process over again, and we send our data, our parity, and our stop bit. You have different options 
where you might define there’s no parity bit, so you just have two stop bits. If that’s the case 
where you have this parity bit, you’ll always see a one. Now, if we were odd parity, if that was 
what was going on, we would make the parity bit to a zero because we have three ones, which 
is an odd number of ones. So, we’d make that a zero, but since we’re even parity, we want to 
make this one. Now our actual data, if we look at our data we sent, we set down and if we look 
at it in binary, you would think your one is your most significant bit, but it’s not. So, it’s kind of 
a backward order. But if we look at it, it would look to us something like the following: 00100011. 
So, if we take that, we could say, you know, that’s equal to a hex number of 0x23. If we got this 
UART transmission, the actual value you’d process or the data you would see is this hex number 
23 (00100011); that’s the actual data you would work with. Now, in our project design, we are 
working with ASCII because we would look up to see where this ASCII code of 23 is and that 
be the character that you received and you would send most of these down. So, if you’re sending 
down like hello or my name is whatever, and whatever you want to send down, you would send 
down the first character for hello. So, you look up the ASCII character for h and send down that 
because it’s an 8-bit value, then the ASCII character for e, the ASCII character for l, and so on, 
and you just keep going down that line. So, you’d have multiple of these packets being set – one 
of these packets for each individual character. Now we talked about these time divisions here, 
and this is defined by your baud rate. So, baud rate is similar to a bit rate. So that’s the number 
of bits you can send per second. For example, if we have a 19200 baud rate, we can send out 
19200 bits a second. So, in order to determine this time, you would say, OK, that’s our bit rate, 
and our time is determined by one divided by our baud rate, or 19200, which is equal to 52.08 μs. 
When you’re working with it, there’s always a bit of a tolerance level, so if you’re not exactly at 
52.08 μs, it’ll still work as long as you’re within a certain tolerance. For example, if you made 
this like 100 μs, then you would get all sorts of errors because nothing would match up, and you 
get all your data checking would not work out, or you would just end up seeing weird charac -
ters on your terminal. So that being said, each one of these individual time stamps means the 
bit time is 52.08 μs, and the reason this is important is because you have two devices: one device 
that’s transmitting this and another device that’s receiving this. So, the guy that’s receiving this 
is going to look and see that while his line is high or at 3.3 V , he is going to disregard it, and

going to divide that by 2, and so approximately at 26  μs right here is where we’re going to

to correct that. The very first and most rudimentary is this parity bit. Now, you could send the 
wrong data at your parity, but might still say it’s valid. For example, if we have even parity and 
we sent three ones. Well, what if we accidentally sent five ones (and also parity of 1)? We would 
still send this bit; even parity bit would be fine. So, it’s a very first line of defense, and a lot of 
times what you’ll do is if you’re working with a different protocol, they’ll send a CRC checksum 
or something afterward to verify that it is actually the right data. Now, with our project, we 
don’t need to have that so much because we’ll see it clearly on our terminal that if we’re trying 
to say Hello World and we see Hello World, we know the data was transmitted correctly, and we 
don’t have to worry about the error checking. Now, to show you the lines, for example, if we 
have our FPGA board and a computer, it really doesn’t matter; the idea is just that you have two 
separate devices, so we can connect them via UART; you would have two lines that are impor -
tant here. Obviously, you have a ground, so they share a common ground. For the computer, 
this would be our Rx line or our receive line, and the transmit line, Tx line, goes from my com -
puter to the FPGA. Now, our FPGA board is going to be our Rx line, and that is going to be the 
transmit line (Tx). This is important because if you try to hook the transmit line from your 
FPGA board or from one device to the transmit line of a second device, that’s not going to work 
at all. You can see once again your transmitter (Tx) has to go to your Rx, and your Rx has to go 
to Tx. So, it has to make sense because it’s a very common mistake to take and connect both the 
Rx lines together and both the Tx lines together. But you don’t want to do that, because if you

9.2   The MicroBlaze Softcore Processor
This section is about the Xilinx MicroBlaze softcore processer. In this section, we’re going to 
explain what a MicroBlaze softcore processor is and how you can instantiate to use one for your 
Xilinx development board, inside of Vivado. If you’re using the Arty A7 development board, to 
do the UART demonstration project, you’re need to use a MicroBlaze softcore processor. So, the 
first step is understanding what a softcore processor is. If you’ve heard of it or familiar with pro -
cessors and microprocessors, you probably would have heard of Arduino or Microchip, or Texas 
Instruments, which are hard processors essentially baked into an actual integrated circuit. You 
can’t change them, and they’re not definable. But a softcore processor is a definable processor that 
is implemented from FPGA fabric, which, when you think of FPGA, you can think of as basically 
digital fabric. You can do whatever you want with them, and it’s just wide open. It’s gives the raw 
ingredients to create a digital design. One of those things you could create is a processor, and so 
Xilinx includes a drop in block that represents one of these processors. So, if you have an FPGA, 
you could drop multiple of these processors on there, which would then turn your FPGA effec-
tively into a multicore processor. So, it’s pretty cool, and what’s really cool is that we can define all 
of the resources. So, if we want to have more memory, we either attach external memory or use 
more of the internal block memory of FPGA. If we want more SPI ports, UART ports, or IO, we 
can do so by just defining that softcore processor and adding more. Now, there obviously are limits, 
but a lot of times when you get a development board that has a microcontroller on there or a pro -
cessor, you might only have three or four timers you can use. But if you develop an FPGA, you can 
configure the MicroBlaze processor to suit your needs. You can specify if you want more timers 
or less timers and the number of bits for each of those timers. On top of that, you have all that 
FPGA fabric around your processor. So, if you want to add functionality, you can create the design 
of the VHDL and a little interface such that the microprocessor can communicate directly to your 
ports in the VHDL chip. So instead of having a separate chip on the board, it’s all in one chip, and 
our communication is also very fast. So, there are a lot of advantages and benefits of using soft -
ware processors. Some of the peripherals you can add to the MicroBlaze softcore processors have 
various flavors, the high-end ones that use more resources, but they give you more features. They 
have a low end and are called MCS, which just has a few small subsets of features. For example, 
what we’re doing here, we’re just sending data out on UART port so we don’t need the high end, 
and all these super fancy features, we just need a UART port and that’s it. But you can add features 
such as, obviously UART, you have SPI or I 2 C or GPIO, which is general-purpose inputs, outputs, 
or timers, as well as additional features you can add, and another cool thing is we can create, as 
we talk about custom peripherals so they can create an interface in AXI is what they call this, and 
so if you generate AXI interface on your VHDL designs, you can now have, for example, the PWM 
we generated a PWM on block, and if we put an AXI interface on that, we could then have our 
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

MicroBlaze software processor start talking to this actual PWM input or design and actually talk 
to it so then we can interface it, just writing the C code, which is pretty cool. However, you can do 
it in much easier ways, and you would just put a general-purpose input output. We want to have 
a general-purpose output where we’re writing out eight bits, and those eight bits correlate to the 
duty cycle of my pulse width modulated (PWM) signal, or you can make it nine bits where one of 
the bits ties to the enable so you can enable or disable your PWM design. So, there’s a lot of differ -
ent things you can do with them. It’s very customizable, and the coolest thing about these softcore 
processors is that you’re not constrained to certain resources, you’re constrained to what you have 
available in your FPGA. But if you want to use up more resources, if you have the resources there, 
you can add those features and functionality. So, it’s a pretty cool thing, and just some things that 
are keynote to these processors, they’re going to use the following resources. On your FPGA, they’ll 
use the internal BRAM, which is Block RAM, and they’ll use a multiplier. So, in your processor, if 
you want to do a multiplication, that’s going to require a multiplier inside the arithmetic logic unit 
of that processor, as well as logic, which takes digital logic just to implement it. You have to have 
your memory tables and there’s a whole slew of information on it. But effectively, you can’t drop 
it out of processors and it is not free. It does take resources, so when you say you want to create a 
multicore processor on FPGA, you will be limited by the resources that are on that FPGA. If you 
drop in, say, five MicroBlaze softcore processors, you might be OK, but you might not be able to 
get six. You might have to upgrade to a larger package part, which just costs more money, and it 
takes more power. So, there are considerations to it, and you can’t just drop them in freely. Then, 
once you synthesize the design, the idea behind it is you’ll take MicroBlaze processor, define it, 
implement it in your design, and then once you synthesize that design, you can then export it into 
another tool, Xilinx software development kit (SDK) tool, where you can then write C code that 
then gets programmed onto that MicoBlaze processor and then we can take that C code and gen-
erate what’s called a.elf file, and that gets associated with your softcore processor so that when you 
program it on the board, you don’t have to program it and then program the C code, if you can just 
program one file, one bit file onto your FPGA board that will then do everything you described 
in your C code, and we’ll show you how to do that later. So, the next thing we want to take a look 
at is see open up your UART demo project, and within UART project, you’ll see that it is blank, 
and there’re no design sources. So, in the design sources, we want to create a MicroBlaze softcore 
processor. Now, if you want to do that directly with VHDL code, it’s going to be a challenge, and 
you have to use special scripts and special functions to get that. What we’re going to do is create 
a block diagram. So, we’re going to click on the Create Block Design and give our design a name, 
UART_Demo, and for directory, we want to leave that local project and specify source set as design 
sources, and then will say, OK, so this is going to create a block design for us, and this will allow 
us to drop in a MicroBlaze softcore processor, from which we then can generate a VHDL file to 
program, as depicted in Figure 9.2.
So, we want to click on Add IP , and in the search bar, type MicroBlaze. You should see three 
options: MicroBlaze, MicroBlaze Debug Module (MDM), and MicroBlaze MCS. For this project, 
we’ll use the MicroBlaze MCS, as illustrated in Figures 9.3 and 9.4, respectively.
The MicroBlaze is the one that has a lot of features and functionality, but it requires more 
resources and power to implement. While it’s fine to use and can be handled by the board, for this 
project, we’ll go with the MicroBlaze MCS instead. It’s easier to instantiate, less complex, and it 
can do everything we need. So, we can double-click on the MicroBlaze MCS. A small window will 
appear notifying that it’s adding that IP to the block diagram. Once it drops it in there, we want 
to go ahead and double-click on MicroBlaze MCS object to open a window “Re-customize IP.” 
Within this window, we are going to set and define all the parameters associated with MicroBlaze

processor. At the beginning for MCS, the memory size is important – it determines whether your 
code will run properly. If there isn’t enough memory, you’ll have issues. But all we’re doing is 
outputting our UART, so eight kilobytes should be more than enough, for the optimization, we’ll 
select Area. We don’t really have a huge performance issue, we’re just writing out your data on the 
UART port. We’re not doing some sort of signal processing. If we were doing signal processing or a 
highly intensive task, we want to go for Performance where we don’t care if it costs more area to do 
so, and we want the performance in this case. We want to optimize in our area, and for buses, we 
will leave both options blank, and for debug, we’ll select None, as shown in Figure 9.5.
Next, we’ll move to the UART tab. Here, we just want to enable the transmitter and define our 
baud rate to 115200. For Programmable Baud Rate, we’re just going to leave that unchecked. We’re 
going to have this as a very basic MicroBlaze processor, and we’re not going to have too many 
parameters in this one, so the rest of these will remain unchecked, as depicted in Figure 9.6.
The following tabs allow you to use a fixed interval timer (FIT) and the programmable interval 
timer (PIT). However, since we’re not using these in our project, we’ll leave them unchecked. 
Similarly, for the GPO (general-purpose output) and the GPI (general-purpose input), we’re not 
using any of those either, so we’ll leave all of those unchecked as well. Same goes for the Interrupts, 
so that option will remain unchecked as well. At this point, we can go ahead and click OK. This 
is going to take our configurations we have set, which in this case is a very basic setup. All we’re 
doing is just enable UART transmission. We’re not even using the receive line (rx) because for this 
demonstration, the only task is to transmit data from the FPGA board to the computer. Since we 
don’t need to have an Rx line, this is just a single line of data transmission. If you click on it, you see 
it out of this, UART plus, and if we pull that down, you can see UART_txd line. That’s our UART 
transmit line, as illustrated in Figure 9.7.

At this point, our MicroBlaze processor has been successfully configured and set within our UART 
demonstration project. If you want to add another processor, you would just go through the same 
process, look at IP , and add another MicroBlaze processor. You can add as many processors as your 
FPGA’s resources will allow. As mentioned earlier, each MicroBlaze core consumes resources. In our 
case, we’ve only assigned 8KB of memory. If your program becomes larger or performs more com -
plex tasks, it’s going to require more memory, and you’re going to run out of that. So, you can put as 
many as your resources can handle. This section explains the MicroBlaze softcore processor within 
the Xilinx device.
9.3   The Xilinx Software Development Kit
This section is about the Xilinx SDK. In this section, we’re going to explain what the Xilinx SDK 
is and how we’ll be using it for a project. The Xilinx SDK, or X-SDK, which stands for Xilinx soft -
ware development kit, is an integrated design environment that allows you to create embedded

134 9 The UART Demonstration Project
applications to run specifically on Xilinx microprocessors. If you’re working with the Texas 
Instruments or any other type of microprocessor, you can’t use this tool. It’s specifically for Xilinx 
microprocessors, and in our case, we’re using the MicroBlaze softcore processor, so it’ll work for 
that. However, you can’t use it for anything else, you can download this as a standalone tool, but 
if you download Vivado, it is by default included with Vivado webpack, or the free version. So, by 
default, this is included and it’s free. You should be able to use it without any problems. This is 
what we’re going to be using to write the code to run on the MicroBlaze softcore processors. For 
example, we’ll be using it in this UART demonstration project as well as in future projects in this 
book. This tool is based on the Eclipse development environment. If you’re familiar with a lot of 
code development tools that are based on eclipse, this will look very similar to those, with many 
of the same features, menus, and everything. It’s just Xilinx took the eclipse development envi-
ronment and modified it to match the tool that they need. So, in our projects, when we create a 
MicroBlaze softcore processor, we instantiate it in our design and it builds all the logic for it. But if 
you don’t program it, if you don’t write any code in the SDK to run on there, you’ll put it on your 
board, and once you start running, you’ll realize that nothing’s happening. That’s because it’s just 
like having a microcontroller or microprocessor on a development board: if you don’t program it 
power it on, it will seem like nothing is working because it’s not doing anything, and that’s because 
the effect is the same. You can successfully program your board, but if you don’t load any code 
into the MicroBlaze, it won’t do anything either, and it’ll just sit there. The SDK has some pretty 
cool features; it’s very powerful and allows you to do a lot of different stuff. It even has the ability 
to include real-time operating system. In this book, we will not be using that, but just letting you 
know a little background on it. They’ve got a free RTOS, which is a free real-time operating system 
that you can use and run inside the MicroBlaze softcore processors. So, if you’re working on a 
project that requires a real-time operating system, you can include this free RTOS, and it gives you 
the framework and structure to create a project based on that. You just create your tasks and have 
them work in a real-time environment. As mentioned earlier, it’s a very powerful tool, and we’ll 
be using it a lot. Right now, we’re going to go ahead and work with our UART demo project to see 
what we need to do to get started with our Xilinx SDK. So, if you open up your UART demo project, 
you’ll see that we have the MicroBlaze MCS, which we’ve already configured. Now, we need to add 
the IO and create a top-level VHDL design. After that, we’ll need to run the synthesis, run imple -
mentation, and generate a bitstream so that we can then go ahead and pour everything we need 
into the Xilinx SDK environment. So, let’s create our IO. We’ll need to create an IO for our clock 
and call it the input clock. If we right-click and select Create Port, it brings up a window where you 
can create inputs and outputs. In this case, we’re going to create a clock. So, we’re going to call it 
Input_Clk, select the direction we’re going to select, which is an input, and set the type to clock. 
Then, we’ll set the frequency to 100MHz and click OK, as shown in Figure 9.8.
So, we now have an input clock that we can drag and place right in line with the clock (Clk). 
If you hover over it and your mouse gets close enough, you’ll see a pointing finger. Once you get 
close enough, the cursor will change to a pencil. If you click when you see the pencil, it’ll show you 
what’s in place and where you can actually put it too. When we connect it, we can see a little green 
check next to the clock, that’s because we recognize it’s a clock signal. Since it’s a clock signal, it 
can be tied together, as depicted in Figure 9.9.

should be connected to a Reset, not to Clk, and vice versa. So, now let’s create our reset signal. We’ll

it Reset_Top; the direction is going to be Input, and for the type, we’ll select as Reset. Then, you

sometimes take a little bit. It’s just going through to verify there’re no issues with the code that 
everything is working as expected. So, we wait for that guy to finish up, so right there, our synthesis 
is complete. So now we want to go ahead and do the run implementation. Now that our synthesis 
is complete, it’s telling us there’re no errors in our design that it has synthesized. We’re going to 
run the implementation, which will take our design and actually physically figure out how it’s 
going to implement it on the board itself. Once that’s complete, we want to generate the bitstream, 
and then we’re going to have to go to our file and export our hardware. Once we do that, that will 
take our everything in our hardware and export it so that we can open it in the SDK environment, 
because then from here, we want to launch our software development kit, or SDK, they call it. So, 
wait for our implementation to complete, and now that’s complete. We’ll go ahead and generate a 
bitstream. When you’re running the route design, one of the big things that will come into play is 
the amount of memory you’re using. So, we’ve only specified eight kilobytes. If you specify more 
memory, that has to pull it the way our design is set up. We don’t have any interface for external 
memory on the board itself. There’s external memory, but we’re not using that. We’re only using 
the block RAM or the internal memory. So, it’s going to take a little bit to generate all that, so our 
bitstream generation has failed, as illustrated in Figure 9.16.

like to have something that prints to indicate the code is complete. So, we’re going to have integers 
i and j, initialize our platform, and then create a for loop. We’ll go from zero to nine, which runs a 
total of 10 times. Inside that loop, we want to print Hello World, followed by “\n\r” for a newline 
and carriage return. So, we print those 10 different times, and then we’re going to have a second 
for loop, which is going to act as a delay for us – and that for loop is empty. We’re not going to do 
anything; all we’re going to do is just wait. This is a quick and basic way to create a delay. Without 
it, we’ll loop through so fast that we won’t be able to see the Hello world come up multiple times. 
It will all show up at once because we’re using a high baud rate of 115200. Once the for loop is 
complete, we then want to print something that says our code is done. So, we will print a code for 
complete statement with an exclamation mark, followed by a new line return. This is the code that 
we’ll use to run on our actual FPGA, as depicted in Figure 9.24 and the following codes.

want to look at is the .elf file and that’s what we’re going to use to load when we generate our bit -
stream. That is going to contain the code that gets loaded inside the MicroBlaze. To find the .elf file, 
navigate to the directory where your project is located. In this case, it’s located under: Arty_A7 >  
UART_Demo > UART_Demo.sdk > Hello_World > Debug > Hello_World.elf.
This file is important because that’s what we want to use when we go back to Vivado to program 
our actual board, and we want to use this file to associate with it. At this point, we need to check 
the Hello_World.elf.size file. You can open it using Notepad++ or any other text editor. This just 
tells you the size of your file, but more or less the size of the code, as illustrated in Figure 9.25.
So, this decimal value (6192 bytes) is the size of Hello_World.elf. That means we’re using 6192 
bytes for this code. Now, if you remember, we only allotted eight kilobytes, so we have about two 
kilobytes left. As you can see, we used quite a bit of memory, and we didn’t even do much. That’s 
why eight kilobytes is not a lot of memory. If we had used C++ or written a bunch of code, we 
would’ve gotten an error when trying to build it. It would say, no, this won’t work because you’re 
using too much memory. In order to associate the .elf file with MicroBlaze, we want to go back to the 
UART_Demo Hardware project. Then, go to Tools > Associate ELF Files. For our design sources, 
we have microblaze_mcs, and currently, we have mb_bootloop_le.elf, as shown in Figure 9.26.  
That is just a basic piece of code – it doesn’t really do anything. So, we want to click the arrow, 
select the file, and add it. To do that, go to Add Files, navigate to the directory where Hello_World.
elf file is located, select it, and click OK. Then, click OK again, as depicted in Figures 9.27 and 9.28, 
respectively.
So now, when we look at our design sources, we have this Hello_World.elf file that is associated 
to our MicroBlaze MCS processor, as illustrated in Figure 9.29.
So, this section is demonstrating how to develop using the Xilinx SDK to write code that will then 
run on the MicroBlaze.

10.1   Introduction
This chapter is about the use of MicroBlaze GPIOs. In this chapter, we’re going to show you how to 
use the general-purpose inputs and outputs with the MicroBlaze softcore processor. At this point, 
we have used the MicroBlaze softcore processor to simply transmit data from our development 
board to the actual computer and read the message, which was just displaying “Hello World,” 
and the code was complete. Now, we’re going to take that a step further, where we must be able to 
transmit messages from the board to the computer as we did before, as well as be able to receive 
messages from the computer back to the actual development board and process those messages 
inside a MicroBlaze processor. For this project, we want to be able to receive commands to turn 
on the LEDs, read the slide switches, and read the pushbuttons. This will require us to do various 
things. First, we need to receive the message, which means that messages are going to come in 
from our UART interface. So, we need to enable our receiver on the UART side so we can actually 
receive the messages. Then, we want to process them to determine what we want to do next, and 
in that case, we’re going to have outputs to send to the LEDs. So, if we tell it we want to have a 
value of four, we display that four on the LEDs or light up the specific LEDs corresponding to the 
command we give it. We also need to be able to read values, which in this case are slide switches or 
pushbuttons. If they send us a command saying they want to know the status of the pushbuttons, 
we have to read the values of those pushbuttons and be able to display that information back out on 
the UART port, which we will then review using Tera Term or any other type of terminal emulator, 
whichever you decide to use. The big thing for this lab and what stands out here is general-purpose 
inputs and outputs, which are used in a lot of different ways, and just like the name suggests, they 
are general-purpose. So, you can use them for various different things. In our specific case, we’re 
using them to put data out on the LEDs and read data in. So, let’s go ahead and look inside the 
Vivado project and examine the MicroBlaze processor. So, we have our MicroBlaze MCS processor, 
and we want to show you all the properties of that processor that you want to have. So, we’re just 
using MicroBlaze MCS version 3.0, and for the general properties, we want to have the following: 
for the memory size, we selected 32 KB, and you can go with a larger size if you want. We think it 
should be more than enough to perform the operation we’re working with. But you can go larger, 
and they recommend you go with sizes that are powers of 2, which are 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, or 128 KB.  
For the optimization, we’re going with area optimization, and that’s because we’re not doing 
any sort of intensive processing where we’re dealing with an audio signal, image processing, or 
anything that requires intense CPU usage. However, we’re just implementing and reading UART 
signals and outputting on the UART. So, we’re going to go with area optimization so that we don’t 
10

send messages to the computer so that we can read them, and the receiver will allow us to receive 
messages typed in the terminal emulator so that we can read and process these data. For the baud

10.2   The UART IO Complete Design
This section is about the UART IO complete design walk-through. In this section, we’re going 
to walk you through the completed design used for UART IO project to run on the Arty A7 
development board. So, we’re going to walk through the design using the Vivado tool and open 
it up. We have a block diagram, and this block diagram contains the MicroBlaze MCS soft -
core processor, along with multiple IOs, as depicted in Figure 10.5. We’ve got our Input_Clk, 
Reset_Top, and UART communication – our receive and transmit signals – which allow us to 
communicate with the computer in order to transmit and receive commands. We have three 
different general-purpose input/output (GPIO) interfaces. GPIO_0 is an input from pins 3  
down to 0 and corresponds to the buttons on the Arty A7 development board. GPIO_1 is an output 
from pins 3 down to 0, connected to four LEDs controlled by the MicroBlaze processor. GPIO_2 
is also an input, connected to switches 2 down to 0 – giving us three switches used to read switch 
status. Although the board has four switches, one is reserved as the system reset (Reset_Top), 
and the remaining three are used for input. So, if we look at the block diagram, it contains our 
MicroBlaze with several inputs and outputs. What we do is take UART_IO.bd, generate a wrapper, 
and then let Vivado manage the wrapper and auto-update it, as illustrated in Figure 10.6.
If we navigate to our source file, we can see UART_IO_wrapper, which is simply the name of our 
block diagram (UART_IO) with an underscore wrapper to it. We can open UART_IO_wrapper, which 
is a VHDL file automatically generated by Xilinx based on the block diagram we created. So, if we 
look at the diagram in Figure 10.5, we can see that the entity of this UART_IO_wrapper contains all

For example, in this case, if you have multiple IO modules, you’ll have to initialize each one of 
them with a different ID. However, in this case, we only use one of them, so we can initialize 
it here, and this goes for just those three commands for initializing the module. The xil_printf 
function is just a Xilinx’s version of printf. So when you’re using a printf, if you use the standard 
C/C++ printf, it takes a lot of data to do that. Since we’re working on an embedded application, 
the ability to have a smaller memory footprint is very ideal. So, for the initialize module or after 
we do the module initialization, we can say that CFInitialize returned (0 = success), which 
means success. If we see a one, then we didn’t initialize successfully. Then, we go ahead and 
print a message to say, “Enter a command,” followed by a new line and return character, After 
that, we enter a while loop until the user enters the “finish” command. So, while our “done” 
variable is equal to zero – up here, we know we have an integer done and it’s equal to zero, and 
the only time it ever equals a one is when the command is “finish.” Inside this loop, we have 
other operations such as execute command, memory set, and byte count, which we set to zero. 
Here’s where we build up the message from our UART terminal. While our execute command is 
equal to zero, we are going to loop through. If our received data from the XIO module is equal to 
zero, our buffer is going to be equal to our temporary buffer. If our buffer byte count is equal to 
“\n” or a new line, then we are going to say execute command is equal to one. We keep looping 
through here until we recognize this “\n” or newline character, and what that does is when we 
send a command, we want to make sure that we send the command followed by “\n” so that we 
can give it the command “\r\n” . This way when we see that “\n” or the new line, we know to go 
ahead and execute the command we received as needed. So, then we go ahead and increment 
our byte count if we don’t enter that execute command (if received buffer is not equal to “\n”). 
Once we exit that loop, we proceed with these codes, where we build up our command arrays. 
For our commands, we have in our buffer zero, which we copy, and we perform three different 
memory copies from our command, LED command, and LED numbers. Now, what we’re going 
to do is to read the button and switch status. The button data will be a value that corresponds 
to the input from the button. So, if all buttons are zero, the value will be zero. If we have one 
button pressed and it’s on the far right or what could be the least significant bit, then we’ll get a 
one. If it’s the far left or the most significant bit, then we would have a four or the highest value 
we have. So, it’s going to take the four buttons we have and treat them as four binary bits. The 
same applies with the switch data – it’s going to take that data and treat it as binary values. Even 
though we have it as an unsigned 32, the highest number it can represent is a total of four bits, 
and the other unsigned 32 can only represent a total of three bits because we use only one bit of 
the switch for the reset (Reset_Top). Here’s where we actually execute the command we receive. 
To execute the command, we use the “strcmp” command, which is a string compare, so what it’s 
going to do is take one string and copy it to another, and if they match, the value will be equal 
to zero. Otherwise, it’ll give you a value of one or negative one, indicating that the strings do not 
match. The first thing we check is whether our command is equal to “led”; if it is equal to “led” , 
then what we’ll get is zero, and our led_date is going to be equal to this led_num[1] minus 48,  
and this is because the numbers we send them are in ASCII. So, if you subtract 48 from that 
number, you’ll get the actual corresponding number. For example, the ASCII representation of 
the number zero is 48. So, when you subtract 48 from 48, you’ll get your led_data to be equal to 
zero. Next, we perform a discrete write operation using the XIO module. We send the led_data 
to the XIO module’s discrete write output. This logic is implemented within a series of if-else if 
statements. First, we check whether the command is “led.” If it is, we take the led_num spec -
ified in the command and write it to the output port. Otherwise, if the command is “button,”  
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License
