 157
we print the button status by outputting the value of button_data, which was previously read using 
the XIO module’s discrete read command. This read operation captures the button and switch 
data. Every time we read these data, depending upon the command, the users ask to print either 
the button data or the switch data. When we do a Xilinx printf command, this is actually taking 
the value and printing it out onto the UART terminal. If we’re using Tera Term, which is what  
we used in this project (or any other terminal emulator), it’ll report the button or switch data. 
Also, if you type in the command “finish” , then all we do is set “done” equal to one. So, when 
we go down and re-enter our while loop, done will be equal to one. Since the condition while 
done is equal to zero (while(done == 0)) is no longer true, we exit that loop. Once outside of 
that loop, we will print a message saying “Exiting main” , then disable Xilinx D cache and Xilinx 
I cache, and finally return zero, signaling the processor to stop running. We then return and exit 
our main loop, and that is the softcore MicroBlaze MCS processor. The complete code for this 
project is shown as follows.
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#include "xparameters.h"
#include "platform.h"
#include "xil_printf.h"
#include "xil_cache.h"
#include "xiomodule.h"
   
int main()
{
      int done = 0;
      int byte_count = 0;
      int execute_cmd;
      char command[6];
      char led_cmd[3];
      char led_num[2];
      u8 tmp_rx_buf;
      u8 rx_buf[40];
      u32 button_data = 0;
      u32 switch_data = 0;
      u32 led_data = 0;
      u32 data;
      XIOModule iomodule;
      Xil_ICacheEnable();
      Xil_DCacheEnable();
   
      //Initialize module
    init_platform();
    data = XIOModule_Initialize(&iomodule, XPAR_IOMODULE_0_DEVICE_ID);
    data = XIOModule_Start(&iomodule);
    data = XIOModule_CfgInitialize(&iomodule, NULL, 1);
    xil_printf("CFInitialize returned (0 = success) %d\n\r", data);
    print("---Enter a command---\n\r");
   
    // While loop until user enters "finish" command
    while(done == 0)
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 173 ---
158 10 The UART IO and Register Access
    {
           execute_cmd = 0;
           memset(rx_buf, 0, sizeof(rx_buf));
           byte_count = 0;
   
           //Build up message from UART Terminal
           while(execute_cmd == 0)
           {
                 //Read UART data
                 while((data = XIOModule_Recv(&iomodule, &tmp_rx_buf, 1)) == 0);
                 rx_buf[byte_count] = tmp_rx_buf;
                 if(rx_buf[byte_count] == '\n')
                       execute_cmd = 1;
                 byte_count++;
           }
   
        //Build the command arrays
           memcpy(command, &rx_buf[0], 6);
           memcpy(led_cmd, &rx_buf[0], 3);
           memcpy(led_num, &rx_buf[4], 2);
  
           //Read the button & switch status
           button_data = XIOModule_DiscreteRead(&iomodule, 1);
           switch_data = XIOModule_DiscreteRead(&iomodule, 2);
  
           //Execute the received command
           if(strcmp(led_cmd, "led") == 0)
           {
                   led_data = led_num[1] - 48;
                   if((led_num[0] - 48) == 1)
                           led_data += 10;
                      XIOModule_DiscreteWrite(&iomodule, 1, led_data);
           }
           else if(strcmp(command, "button") == 0)
                    xil_printf("Button Status: %d\n\r", button_data);
           else if(strcmp(command, "switch") == 0)
               xil_printf("Switch Status: %d\n\r", switch_data);
           else if(strcmp(command, "finish") == 0)
                    done = 1;
           else
                    print("Command is invalid, re-enter below:\n\r");
    }
  
    print("---Exiting main---\n\r");
    Xil_DCacheDisable();
    Xil_ICacheDisable();
    cleanup_platform();
    return 0;
  }
So, we go ahead and build this, which generates the UART_IO.elf file we were talking about 
inside our Vivado design, as shown in Figures 10.10, 10.11, and 10.12.

master .xdc file. Within this file, we want to go ahead and place all of the IOs, and in this case, 
we have Input_Clk, Reset_Top, UART_Rx, UART_Tx, our btn from three to zero, led from three 
to zero, and our sw from two to zero. If we go to our Arty A7 master .xdc file, the first thing we do 
is set property CFGBVS VCCO in current_design and set property CONFIG_VOLTAGE to 3.3 in 
current_design. Now, for our clock signal, we have this package pin set to E3, and we set that to our 
Input_Clk. Then, we use this create clock add, and we name it Input_Clk with a period of 10 and 
a waveform from 0 to 5 and get_ports Input_Clk. That’s just going to help us when it goes through 
and does our synthesis, as well as the implementation, and it will generate and ensure that our 
Input_Clk, which is defined there, can meet the timing requirements of a 100MHz clock. The next 
line we will use is our switches. So, we will use all four switches on the Arty A7 board. The first 
switch, which, if you look at it, is switch zero, is our reset (Reset_Top). So, based on whether this 
is high or low, it is going to either allow our MicroBlaze softcore processor to run or not. It’ll put 
it in a reset state or run state. For the next pins, we have what would be our switches 1, 2, and 3,

11.1   Introduction
This chapter focuses on temperature sensor ADC processing. We’ll explain how various temperature 
sensors work, with a specific focus on the temperature sensor used in our ADC processing project. 
In this project, we aim to process an analog signal and convert it into a temperature value that can 
be displayed on a serial port and viewed on a computer, allowing us to monitor the actual tempera -
ture. There are various types of temperature sensors, which typically fall into one of two categories: 
digital temperature sensor and analog temperature sensor. Both types perform the same function – 
measuring and sensing temperature. However, a digital temperature sensor has a digital interface, 
where you simply read the bits and they tell you what the temperature is. This is usually done with an 
SPI or an I 2 C type of interface. On the other hand, an analog temperature sensor outputs an analog 
voltage that correlates to a specific temperature, at which point you need to take the analog voltage, 
perform an analog to digital conversion, process it, and convert it to the temperature. In this chapter, 
we’re using an analog temperature sensor, specifically the TMP36 from analog devices. The TMP36 
has three pins: a voltage source pin, an output pin, and ground. We’ll apply 3.3 V to the voltage source, 
connect ground appropriately, and the output voltage is where we’ll perform our analog to digital 
conversion. That output voltage signal will be used to determine the temperature. So, the way the 
sensor works is straightforward: as the temperature increases or decreases, the output voltage of the 
sensor will increase or decrease in correlation with the temperature. From there, we just have to per -
form the manipulation, where we’re going to use the MicroBlaze or the softcore processor to go ahead 
and perform the processing of that information. Also, with the analog to digital conversion, the way it 
works is: you take an analog voltage, and you output the digital representation of that voltage because 
we can’t actually process an analog value inside the MicroBlaze. We’re working in a digital world, so 
we need actual bits or values to process and work with. So, all that analog to digital conversion is doing 
is taking the analog value and converting it into a digital value, specified by the number of bits you 
have. With the Arty A7 development board, using the Artix FPGA, we have what’s called an XADC 
core built into the actual FPGA. What that means is that certain pins can allow for analog signals to 
come directly in, and the FPGA already has the analog to digital conversion built-in. So, within Vivado, 
all we have to do is drop in the XADC IP core into a block design. Then, we can figure that XADC core 
to operate on whatever channel we’re bringing our temperature analog signal into. We just have to 
tell it which channel we’re using and set the parameters accordingly so that we can then process that 
data with the MicroBlaze, and so it’s really nice. In fact, we’re going to create one design where we 
have a MicroBlaze softcore processor and our XADC core. They’re going to interface with each other, 
and all they’re going to do is: the MicroBlaze will continually read the analog to digital conversion 
11

value from that XADC core, and that XADC core will just take that analog value coming from the 
temperature sensor, convert it to a digital value, and continually do that with a MicroBlaze processor, 
which will determine the temperature and send it out to the serial port. So, the next thing is to take a 
look at the actual datasheet of the analog to digital devices, analog devices ADC converter CHIP , the 
TMP36. We’ll take a look at the datasheet and kind of run through it to get an understanding of what 
this device is and how it actually operates. So, the datasheet shows us it is called a low-voltage tem -
perature sensor. They refer to it this way because it can operate anywhere from 2.7 to 5.5 V . On most 
circuit boards, the voltages range from 1.8 to 5 V . So, 2.7–5.5 V covers a pretty wide range of voltages 
you’ll typically see on these development boards. Since we’re working with 3.3 V , we are right in the 
middle of this operating range. This means the temperature sensor should work just fine with our 
FPGA development board. We’re using the TO-92 package, which has three pins. Pin 1 is the positive 
voltage input, so we’ll connect that to 3.3 V . Pin 2 is V OUT , which is the actual analog signal we’re going 
to process and interpret to determine the temperature. Pin 3 is the ground. So, an important thing to 
note here is pin 2 is V OUT . When you’re connecting this sensor to your board, you’ll have to make sure 
you look at this package, understand which pin is which, and make sure you connect it correctly, as 
shown in Figure 11.1.
The next thing we’ll take a look at – with importance – is a chart included in the datasheet that is 
very important. It shows the temperature versus output voltage for three sensors: TMP35, TMP36, 
and TMP37, as depicted in Figure 11.2.
We’re using the TMP36, so we want to focus on line b. If we look closely, we’ll see that this is a linear 
line – actually, all these are linear lines – and they provide a clear relationship between temperature 
and output voltage. We’re going to read the output voltage, and based on this voltage, we can deter -
mine the temperature using the equation for line b, which we’ll show you how to solve. This will give 
us the temperature in degrees Celsius. From that point, we can perform a simple Celsius to Fahrenheit 
conversion in order to determine the temperature in Celsius or Fahrenheit. An important point to note 
when figuring out the actual equation for this linear line is found in the functional description section 
of the datasheet. They have a table showing the output characteristics, as illustrated in Table 11.1. The 
key values we want to look at are the offset voltage, which is half a volt (0.5 V), and output voltage 
scaling, which is 10 mV/°C. This means that at 25 °C or your room temperature, the output voltage for 
the TMP36 should be about 750 mV . So now, let’s take some notes and understand what exact equation 
we need to operate in order to perform this calculation.
So, if we get a voltage output in millivolts (mV), how can we actually convert that to a tempera-
ture? Let’s go ahead and take some notes that we’ll be using. Since we know this is a linear line, we 
can define it using the basic equation as follows.
 y =  mx  +  b  (11.1)
That  b  in Equation (11.1) is obtained as follows: we looked at Table 11.1, which told us that our 
offset voltage is 0.5 V or 500 mV , so that would be our  b  term. So, we know our  b  is equal to 500 mV , 
and that’s our offset. Also, it gave us the scaling factor, and it was 10 mV/°C, and that was equivalent 
to our m value, that’s like our slope, so our m is equal to the 10 mV/°C, and that would be our slope.

Then we also have our  x  value, which is going to correlate to a temperature in °C, and the reason for

temp_C = (voltage mV − 500 mV )/10 mV   (11.3)  
 That’s going to give us our temperature in °C, and that’s how we’re going to calculate our Celsius

important because this will tell you how accurate you can actually be. This is going to be based on the 
number of bits you have, as well as the voltage range you’re trying to sense. So, the way you calculate 
your resolution is by taking the voltage range, which in this case is 3.3 V , and then you divide it by  
the number of possible positions you have. So, in this case, we have 12 bits. So, if we have 12 bits, we 
take 2 to the power of 12, and that gives us 4096, and that’s 4096 possible combinations. So, the follow -
ing equation would give us our resolution:
 Resolution  =    3.3  V  _   2   12     =    3.3  V  _  4096    ≈  0.81 mV   (11.4)
W hat that means is that our temperature we’re reading, or our voltage reading, is approximately 
accurate to 0.81 mV , and if we look at our m or 10 mV/°C, then we are going to be more accurate than 
a degree Celsius. So, we’re within a very high level of accuracy because 10 mV is equal to a degree 
Celsius, and we’re even less than 1 mV , which is less than one-tenth of a degree. So, we have a high res -
olution of accuracy. Now, taking this concept, if we have the same number of bits and a higher voltage 
range – so, for example, let’s say we have another common case, which would be 5 V , and we’re still 
using our 12-bit analog to digital conversion, and we still have 4096 possibilities – then the resolution 
would be as in the following equation:
 Resolution  =    5 V  _   2   12     =    5 V  _  4096    ≈  1.2 mV   (11.5)
T he resolution is still really pretty good, and that’s still really quite accurate. We’re still accurate to 
within a degree – maybe not greater than a degree. But if you look at using the same analog to digital 
conversion, whether you’re using a 3.3 V signal on your input or a 5 V signal, you can see that the 
3.3 V gives you 0.81 mV resolution versus 5 V , which gives you 1.2 mV resolution. So, we get a decent 
amount better with the 3.3 V . Obviously, if you take this even higher to 10 V or any higher voltage, 
you can see that as you have a higher range, your resolution is going to decrease, which makes sense 
because the bits are basically telling you how many different possibilities you have in between each 
voltage range. So, that’s another thing to note with resolution, and that comes into play when you’re 
working with accuracy. If you have a circuit or something you’re working with where you’re trying to 
measure the temperature to a certain degree of accuracy, you’ll need to use this calculation to deter -
mine how accurate you can actually be. So, that is just an excellent explanation of the temperature 
sensor circuit and how we’re going to use it in our project.
11.2   Using the Xilinx XADC and MicroBlaze MCS
This section is about using the Xilinx XADC and MicroBlaze MCS. Here, we’re going to walk through 
how to set up the XADC core, which is a core provided by Xilinx to use the internal analog to digital 
converter on certain Xilinx chips. The chip we’re using on the Arty A7 board has one of these cores. 
So, within Vivado, we will drop in an XADC core and configure it so that we can read this analog to 
digital conversion values. We want to have it set up in such a way that we can interface with it using 
a MicroBlaze MCS. So, in this section, we’re going to show you how to set up the XADC core, and in 
the next section, we’ll show you how to set up the MicroBlaze so we can get them to interface together. 
Because on the chip, we have the capability of doing both processing and analog to digital conversion, 
and we’re going to show you how to get all these working together on the same chip. So, if you open up 
your project – the temperature sensor project – we’re going to place the XADC Wizard core. Go ahead 
and open it up, and we’re going to show you all the properties you’ll want to set on this core. The first

If we look at the user guide for XADC (UG480), it tells us the actual address locations for the column 
status registers. But for this DRP interface, it tells us that DADDR[6:0] from 00h to 3Fh contains the 
read-only status registers. If we go through these read-only status registers, when we get down to 
the V AUXP[15:0]/V AUXN[15:0] addresses, they range from 10h to 1Fh. So, the address for V AUXP4/ 
V AUXN4 will be 14h, which, when we convert that to decimal, gives us the constant value of 20. 
Therefore, we’re going to set it to 20, and that’s how we’re selecting the actual signal that will appear on 
our output, do_out[15:0]. This output is a digital signal that will then be fed into our MicroBlaze MCS 
processor. We have den_in, which enables our dynamic reconfigurable port (DRP). We’ll tie that to a 
signal, which then gets tied to a slide switch to enable or disable our XADC. The vauxn4 and vauxp4 
are our differential analog signals, which we will tie to specific IO, and these correlate to the actual 
single-ended analog input we have on the A0 pin for the Arduino header. Then, we have our clock 
and reset, and those are all the different IO we’re using for this XADC Wizard. There are several ports 
that are not being used, which is fine – we don’t have to use all of them. Even though we’re not using 
everything on this XADC Wizard, when you go ahead and synthesize, it might give you some warn -
ings. However, you can disregard them as it should optimize some of them away, but a lot of them will 
still be present, but that’s fine. Even though we don’t have them connected, we can leave them discon -
nected, and our project will still work and be able to configure just fine, as illustrated in Figure 11.9. 
So, that’s essentially how you use the Xilinx XADC Wizard within Vivado to start reading the analog 
signal and its output from the temperature sensor.

11.3   The MicroBlaze MCS ADC Configuration
This section is about the MicroBlaze MCS ADC configuration. In this section, we’re going to 
show you how to take the MicroBlaze MCS core and configure it so that we can do analog 
to digital conversion readings. Now, the MicroBlaze MCS core from Xilinx doesn’t have an 
analog to digital conversion built in. However, it’s on the same chip as the XADC. First, we’re 
going to see how to set up the XADC, so the XADC has been placed and configured. Now, we 
want to read that data from a parallel port. We’ve got these general-purpose inputs and out -
puts (GPIOs). We’ll take a 16-bit general-purpose input and use it to read the data out coming 
from the actual specific analog channel. So, we’ll go ahead and place the MicroBlaze MCS on 
the block diagram, and then configure it as follows. For memory size, we’re using 64 kB – you 
can increase this if needed. If you go beyond 64 kB, we suggest you go to 128 kB; however, that 
depends on how large you want to go. For MicroBlaze optimization, we’re going to optimize 
it for area since we’re only working with temperatures, which don’t change super often, so we 
don’t need any sort of super high performance. We’re just realistic, and all we want to do is to 
update our temperature once every roughly half a second or every second, every so many sec -
onds. Because the temperature is not going to change that fast anyway, we can optimize it for 
area just to save on resources. We’ll leave both of the buses unchecked, and for debug, we’ll 
select NONE, as shown in Figure 11.10.
For UART, we want to enable both the receiver and the transmitter. We don’t need the receiver 
right now, but we’re going to enable it anyway – just in case you want to add something later to the 
project that involves sending data. But right now, we’re just going to enable the transmitter, and 
we’re going to pull both of them, since we’re going to be transmitting the temperature data that we 
read from our board to the Tera Term terminal emulator, as depicted in Figure 11.11.
For our FIT or fixed interval timer, we’re not going to be using any of those, so FIT 1 through 
FIT 4 can all be left unchecked. The same goes for programmable interval timers, PIT 1, PIT 2,  
PIT 3, and PIT 4; we’re not going to be using those either, so leave them unchecked. For the 
GPOs, we’re not going to be having any general-purpose outputs since we’re only reading data. 
The only output we’re using is the UART output, so we’ll leave all the GPOs unchecked. For 
general purpose inputs (GPIs), we want GPI 1 to be our analog to digital conversion value. We’re 
going to take that ADC output value from the XADC and read it into GPI 1. For GPI 2, we’ll leave

our temperature in degrees Celsius (Temperature_C) and degrees Fahrenheit (Temperature_F). 
We also want to have another floating-point value, which will be Temperature_out, and this is

when we process. We don’t want to keep printing this temperature value so fast on our terminal

it is printed out, as well as Temperature_whole, which is going to be the whole digit number. 
So, we’re going to be using the Xilinx printf command, which will allow us to print decimals, 
but we cannot print a floating-point number. So, we’re going to have to take the floating-point 
value of our Temperature_out and convert it to a whole as well as a decimal portion. We’ll 
call it Temperature_hundreds, and that’s just because we’re going to show two decimal places, 
temperature hundreds. Now, we want to go ahead and have our XIO module, so we’ll call it 
XIO module, and this is just like we did in the UART_IO project. So, we’re following the same 
scheme, except we only have a general-purpose input, and we don’t have any general-purpose 
outputs. Now, we’re going to initialize our GPIOs, and we’ll do that by using our data, which is 
equal to XIOModule_initialize(&gpi, XPAR_IOMODULE_0_DEVICE_ID). So here we’re ini -
tializing our general-purpose input. Now, we want to go ahead and start that, so we will do that 
with the XIOModule_Start(&gpi) command put right underneath there. So that’s going to ini -
tialize all of our general-purpose inputs – in this case, just our inputs. We are going to comment 
out all of the remaining codes. So, we’ll just comment all of that out – if we want to use it later, 
we can. But right now, we’re not worried about it, and we don’t need to return anything because 
our call to main doesn’t have anything, and we’re never going to get there. So to have a return 
doesn’t really matter. We’re going to get into a while loop, where we never have to return. Now, 
we want to enter our infinite while loop. In this while loop, we are going to be doing various 
things: perform the ADC reading, calculate the temperature values, and determine whether to 
display it in Fahrenheit or Celsius. The first thing is to read the ADC value by taking the XIO 
discrete read and passing it to the handle of our general-purpose input, which we’ve already 
defined in the following code.
ADC_reading = XIOModule_DiscreteRead(&gpi, 1);
So that’s the actual raw analog to digital conversion reading, and it’s going to be the 16-bit value that 
we’re reading from our XADC module. Now we want to shift out the four least significant bits (LSBs), 
and that’s because our ADC rating is 12-bit wide. However, they give you 16 bits, and they tell you 
inside the XADC user guide to go ahead and shift out the four LSBs. So, we’re going to shift those out, 
and let’s make a comment so we know what we’re doing. The next thing we want to do at this point is 
have our actual ADC reading at this stage right here, and that is the actual raw ADC reading that we 
have and will work with. So, the next thing we want to do is use our slide switch to determine if we’re 
displaying our results in Fahrenheit or Celsius. That takes place using this variable to hold the selected 
degree, and we’re making that equal to our XIO module discrete read on channel 2, because that’s the 
slide switch that our general-purpose input is connected to, as shown in the following code.
Selected_Degree = XIOModule_DiscreteRead(&gpi, 2);
Then, right below that, we want to calculate our voltage in millivolts, and this is a floating-point 
value. So, in order to calculate this, we use the following code.
Voltage_mV = (float)(3.3/4095) * (float)ADC_reading * 1000;

We’re going to multiply that value by a thousand, and that is so we put it in mV . If we don’t 
multiply it by a thousand, the value would remain in volts. But since we want the millivolt 
reading, we’re going to multiply by a thousand. So, this whole equation is used to determine the 
voltage in millivolts. Now, we want to determine the temperature in degrees Celsius. We have to 
calculate this every time, even if the user has selected to display the temperature in Fahrenheit, 
because our formula to determine temperature is based on Celsius. Once we’ve calculated  
the temperature in Celsius, if the user has selected Fahrenheit, we’ll go ahead and perform the 
conversion. If not, we’ll just use the Celsius temperature directly. This logic is written as the 
following code.
Temperature_C = (Voltage_mV – 500) / 10;
So now, at this point, we’re going to set Temperature_out because Temperature_out is the 
value that we’re going to display via the UART terminal. So, Temperature_out is initially equal 
to Temperature_C. Now, what we need to do is check and determine what our Selected_Degree 
is. Selected_Degree, which we’ve already read in, tells us whether we’re reading in Fahrenheit or 
Celsius, so we need to use an if statement as shown in the following codes.
if(Selected_Degree == 1)
{
    Temperature_F = (Temperature_C * 1.8) + 32;
    Temperature_out = Temperature_F;
}
So, if the selected degree is 1 – meaning your slide switch is set to high, which means we 
want to display the output temperature in Fahrenheit – then our Temperature_out is now equal 
to the temperature in Fahrenheit. If not, the Temperature_out stays at the value in degrees 
Celsius. So, at this point, we’ve calculated the temperature in both degrees Celsius and degrees 
Fahrenheit, based on whether or not the user selects to display one or the other. Now, the final 
thing we need to do is write the output temperature so that the user can actually see what 
the temperature value is. If we don’t display it anywhere, we won’t know the temperature is 
changing, and we can’t verify that our design is working correctly. In order to display it, we 
can only print out whole decimal values. But we have a floating-point value, and we want to 
show the accuracy – specifically the changes in the hundredths position. So, to do that, we’ll 
say Temperature_whole is equal to Temperature_out, and Temperature_out is a floating-point 
value, and Temperature_whole is an integer value. This will take everything to the left of the 
decimal point and will be represented in Temperature_whole. So, let’s say our temperature 
is 46.87 degrees. At this point, temperature is going to be equal to 46. Now, what we need to 
do is get that 87 to display. To do that, we want it to be stored in Temperature_hundreds, and 
that Temperature_hundreds is equal to Temperature_out, which is 46.87, and we’re going to 
subtract Temperature_whole, which is 46. That gives us 0.87. We then multiply that by 100 
to get 87. So now, Temperature_whole is equal to 46 and Temperature_hundreds is equal to 
87. When we display this, we need to make sure that we put a decimal point between them to 
display 46.87. Finally, we need to determine whether we’re in Fahrenheit or Celsius because 
based on the selection, we want to print either a C or an F to let the user know that it’s degrees 
Fahrenheit or degrees Celsius. So, we’ll just use an if-else statement in the following codes.
if(Selected_Degree == 0)
   xil_printf(“Temperature: %d.%d C\n\r”, Temperature_whole, Temperature_ 
hundreds);

After this, we want to create a delay, and the reason we want to create a delay is that this is going 
to happen so fast that if we don’t have a delay, your screen will fill up so quickly that you won’t even 
be able to read or see what the temperature is. So, as the temperature changes, you won’t be able 
to see the rising or the lowering of the temperature. So, to create a delay, we’re just going to use a 
simple count. It’s a really simple and basic way to create a delay in an embedded type of system.
delay_count = 0;
while(delay_count < 50000000)
     delay_count++;
So, we’re going to keep counting, so something is going to count to a high number. In a way, you 
can change that number – the delay will change accordingly. If you make it a higher number, you’ll 
get a longer delay. If you make it a lower number, you’ll have a shorter delay. While it’s less than 
that number, we are going to increment our delay count, and then we’re going to continue in the 
outer while loop. So, our while loop will continue on, and we have this while(1) loop. So, while it 
is running, we’ll never actually get to our main, and actually, in our main, we are going to have our 
return 0, and we’ll uncomment it. We’ll go ahead and save the code, and the complete code for this 
project is shown as follows.

11.5 The ADC Processing Arty A7 IO Placement
 181
11.5   The ADC Processing Arty A7 IO Placement
This section is about ADC processing Arty A7 IO placement. Here, we’ll walk through all the 
different IO placements for the temperature sensor project. For this project, we have various 
inputs and outputs. Our inputs include adc_enable, reset, and temperature_select, which 
are tied to slide switches. If we go to our Arty_A7_Master.xdc file and look at the top, we 
can see the slide switches listed first. So, our switch zero, which we’ll tie to adc_enable, is 
going to enable or disable the analog to digital conversion. Then, we have reset, which is tied 
to switch two, and that’s going to determine whether our MicroBlaze and analog to digital 
conversion are held in a reset state. Our temperature_select is tied to switch three, and this 
will determine whether we’re going to output the temperature in Celsius or Fahrenheit. For 
our clock (clk), which goes into our XADC and our MicroBlaze MCS, we’re going to tie that to 
package pin E3, which is connected to an external oscillator on the actual development board. 
That’s just a 100 MHz clock that we’re bringing into the FPGA. We use the next line to create 
a clock and we’re just telling it the period and the waveform so that when it does an analysis,

and ck_an_p, and this is the differential analog signal that gets fed into our XADC core to per -

xdc file, we’ll find a section titled ChipKit Single Ended Analog Inputs, and then it goes on. The 
ck_an_p pins can be used as single-ended analog inputs with voltages from 0 to 3.3 V (Chipkit 
Analog pins A0–A5). These are six different analog channels, and we’re using A0. So, if you’re 
working with the ChipKit or Arduino, this would be your analog A0 input is, which is what this 
would be tied to. We can bring a signal from 0 to 3.3 V in. It takes that signal and turns it into a 
differential signal. So, one single-ended input from 0 to 3.3 V gets broken into two actual pins 
on the FPGA cell. By using analog input A0, we’re going to require two inputs: ck_an_n and 
ck_an_p. If we look, those are tied directly to vauxn4 and vauxp4. On XADC, that’s just one of 
the channels for analog to digital conversion. So, we’ll go ahead and tie those there. Next, we 
have our UART–USB interface, which we’ll use to transmit the converted temperature values to 
our computer screen. That’s just a standard UART interface – uart_tx and uart_rx – and we’ll 
place those in the UART–USB interface on pins D10 and A9. Once we do that, that is all we have 
for the temperature sensor project. So, we’re connecting ck_an_n and ck_an_p, which go out to 
A0 on the ChipKit header. This will connect and bring in the output signal from our tempera -
ture sensor, which is fed to the XADC. Then, it performs the conversion. The MicroBlaze reads 
that conversion, determines the voltage value, converts it to a temperature, and then reports 
that temperature on the serial port terminal or UART terminal. That is the ADC processing Arty 
A7 IO placement for Temp_Sensor project, as shown in Figure 11.20.

12.1   Introduction
This chapter is about using the SPI interface with the external flash memory. First of all, we’re 
going to explain and briefly talk about the external flash memory chip that is used on the Arty 
A7 development board. For the Arty A7 development board, there is an external QSPI flash chip 
located on the board, and typically what this is used for is to store the configuration of the FPGA. 
When you power up the board, if you think of an FPGA, a lot of times in our labs, we have to 
program it using the JTAG cable. Every time we power it off and then power it back on, we have 
to reprogram it. So, what these QSPI external flash memory chips are commonly used for is to 
hold that configuration. That way, as soon as the board powers up, it reads the configuration from 
that external flash memory and configures the FPGA automatically. Out in the field, you wouldn’t 
want to reprogram the board every time it is powered off – that would not be feasible. So, that’s 
typically what these QSPI chips are used for. The chip used on the Arty A7 board is a 16 MB flash 
chip, and the Arty A7 configuration file takes approximately 2 MB. That leaves about 14 MB free 
to work with. Now, we are only going to be using a few bytes since we’re just going to store a 
name and some additional information. But you could store up to 14 MB of data, so there’s a lot of 
information you could store on there. The whole idea of this lab in this project is to help you under -
stand how to use the SPI interface to communicate with a chip external to the FPGA. In this case, 
it’s a memory chip manufactured by Micron, which is an IC manufacturer that commonly makes 
memory devices. This is a nonvolatile external flash memory chip. They also manufacture RAM, 
SD-RAM, and various other types of integrated circuits you can work with. Don’t worry about 
overwriting anything in the QSPI flash during this project, because when we are working with 
the boards and all the projects we’re doing, we’re just loading them directly into the FPGA from 
the actual JTAG interface. So even if we write over something in the QSPI flash, we can always 
rewrite it later. If you visit the digital website, they provide you with the initial file that was prepro -
grammed on the Arty A7, so we can always reprogram or restore the original contents. Whatever 
you write to the QSPI flash, don’t worry – we can always erase or replace it. Now, we’re going to 
take a look at the Micron datasheet for the actual flash part that we’ll be interfacing with. On the 
first page, it gives us a quick summary of the device. It’s a Micron serial NOR flash memory –  
NOR refers to the type of architecture it uses. They can have NOR or NAND flash, and that’s just 
based on the gates or the architecture of how they construct the actual memory. It’s a 128  Mb 
device, which equals 16 MB (128 Mb/8 = 16 MB). It supports 4 kB sector erase, which means you 
can erase a whole 4 kB block at once, instead of erasing byte by byte, which would be much slower.

So, it tells us that’s an SPI-compatible serial bus interface, which is the interface we’re going to use

Also, it gives us a max clock frequency of 108 MHz, so this thing can run very fast. If you think 
about it, the Arty A7 development board has a 100 MHz clock coming in. So, no matter how fast we 
want to run this SPI device, we can run it super fast – it can go up to 108 MHz, and we’re only com-
ing in at 100 MHz. We’re not going to use a Phase-Locked Loop (PLL) to increase the frequency on 
the actual Arty A7 device. So, any speed we want to run at will be well within specifications. For 
example, if we run it at 50 MHz, we’ll be well within the specifications of 100 MHz it can handle. 
Also, it gives you additional details, such as the voltage range (2.7–3.6 V), which is important when 
you’re working with different flash memories. The main thing we need to worry about is making 
sure we understand the actual SPI characteristics in terms of voltages and currents. The team 
that designed and built the board already went ahead and verified all of that to make sure that 
everything works just fine. We just want to focus on understanding how to communicate with the 
chip – what we send down and what we can expect back. So, let’s go ahead and take a look at the 
description of the memory organization and the block diagram, as shown in Figure 12.1. The block 
diagram shows us the different signals: hold, write/Vpp, S, C, and DQ0, DQ1 to DQ2 and DQ3. 
These are used and fed into the I/O shift register. When you think about SPI, which we’ll discuss 
later, it’s actually a big shift register. You’re sending data and controlling the clock that shifts data in 
and out. So, we’re going to shift data into this I/O shift register, and based on the information we’re 
shifting in, it’s going to set the address register. There’s also a 256-byte data buffer and a status reg -
ister. We’re going to tell it where we want to write data or read data from, and it’s going to process 
that and send data back to us. This memory allows us to access and write single bytes at a time, or 
we can write in chunks. The next thing we will look at is the protocol, but then we’ll see the com-
mands, which shows us what we can actually write down and the data that we can read back. If we  
look at the supported protocols, they list extended, dual, and quad SPI protocols. This extended 
protocol is the one we’re going to use. In this mode, we just have one data bit on every clock cycle. 
In dual, you use DQ1 and DQ0, and in quad, you send four data bits on every clock cycle using 
the DQ3 down to DQ0. For our purposes, we’re just using DQ0 and they give you a quick descrip -
tion. So, this is the device default protocol from the factory and additional commands extend the 
SPI protocol. If we want to use these other protocols, we have to send down specific commands in 
this extended format so that we can use the dual or quad interface. But we are sticking with the 
extended protocol. So, when you look through the datasheet, if you want to understand how it 
works, just look at the extended protocol. Next, let’s take a look at some of those supported flash 
commands. They list different operations that we can do. If you notice, in the left-hand column, 
they provide the command, the code, and indicate whether it is extended, dual I/O, or quad I/O, 
along with the data bytes. So, we are using the extended mode. If we look at the table, it shows 
whether it’s supported or not. Some of these operations are only supported on certain interfaces. 
For example, this multiple I/O read ID is not supported in extended mode, but it’s supported in 
dual or quad modes. For our purposes, we only need to perform read and write operations. The 
read operation is supported in the extended mode, so we can proceed with that. Similarly, write 
operations are also supported. For example, the write enable command is yes, and it supports that. 
So, everything that we need to do is supported in the extended mode. So, that code column shows 
the actual data that we’re going to send. So, if we want to enable the reset, we need to send down 
the 66 h or this 8-bit value, so it’s in a hexadecimal format. We’re not going to go into detail on  
all these commands because there are quite a few of them here and their descriptions can be com-
plex. But once we get into writing the actual software and designing our SPI core, we’ll take a look 
at all these and make sure that we can support and send all these down correctly. One helpful

12.2   Overview of the SPI Interface
This section is about the SPI interface explanation. Here, we’re going to explain the SPI inter -
face, specifically how we’ll be using it in a project to communicate with an external memory chip. 
First off, what is SPI? It stands for serial peripheral interface, and SPI is a serial communication 
protocol used by different integrated circuits (ICs) on a circuit board to communicate with each 
other. When you think of UART, it’s another protocol ICs can use to communicate. However, SPI 
is another communication protocol. For example, if you have two microcontrollers on a circuit 
board, they can use an SPI interface to communicate with each other. Or in our case with an FPGA, 
you can have two different FPGAs on the same board communicating over SPI. Typically, SPI is not 
used for long-distance communication. Most of the time, SPI is used when two devices are located 
on the same physical PCB board. If you need to communicate over long distances, you’d have to 
put it in a different format, such as RS-45, or an ethernet-style format. So, the way we’re going to be 
using it is to have our device or our FPGA and processor to communicate with an actual memory 
chip. One advantage of SPI is that it allows for very fast data transmission. The disadvantage is 
that it uses more pins, but you can transmit data very quickly with an SPI interface. There are four 
common signals used in SPI communication: MOSI (master out, slave in signal), MISO (master in, 
slave out signal), SCK (serial synchronous clock signal), and the CS (control select). The CS signal 
is used when you have multiple devices on the SPI bus. So, when you have an SPI bus, you might 
have one device – your microcontroller, or whatever you want to call the master – and it will have 
several other slave devices attached to that same bus. They’ll have a separate CS line, but share 
the MOSI, the MISO, and the SCK lines. So, the master picks the device it wants to talk to and sets 
that CS line low. All the others CS lines will be high. That device knows, once the CS goes low, 
that the master is talking to him, so it starts listening on the bus. The MOSI is the signal that the 
master sends data out on, and it’s synchronous with the clock. Once the data is fully received and 
the command is sent to the slave, that slave returns the data, or responds based on whatever that 
message was. So, for example, in the case of the memory, if we want to read at a specific address, 
we’ll send that data down on the MOSI line. Then the actual memory device will just keep send-
ing our synchronous clock and the memory line on the MISO line. It’ll send the data back to us, 
and we’ll be able to read and interpret what that data means. You can run SPI very fast depending 
on the device. As long as both the slave and the master can operate at a given frequency, you can 
run your SPI at that speed. With SPI, there are several different versions: standard SPI, which is 
just a single bit of data transferred per clock cycle, and that’s where you send a synchronous clock, 
where you send one bit of data down to that same device. They have a dual SPI, where in every 
clock cycle, you send two bits of data to the same device, and when it responds, it’ll send two bits 
of data back. Then, they have the quad SPI (QSPI), where you send four bits of data every clock 
cycle. So, the quad SPI is obviously the fastest because you’re sending more data for less clock 
cycles. If you think about it, you have eight bits of data or one byte to transfer to the slave device. 
If you’re using a standard SPI, it’s going to take you 8 clock cycles to get all 8 bits of data down 
to the slave device. If you’re using dual SPI, it’ll only take you four clock cycles to get those 8 bits 
to the same device, whereas if you’re using a quad SPI, you’re only going to use two clock cycles  
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

12.2 Overview of the SPI Interface
 187
to get that full byte of data down to the same device, So, it’s much faster, and it just takes up more 
IO lines. Typically, when you’re working with FPGAs, you’ll have devices where the QSPI is very 
common because you have a big configuration bit file, so that when you power on, the FPGA is 
going to look into that key SPI device and pull a configuration file in, and you want that to hap -
pen pretty fast. You don’t want to turn your device on and have to wait for a couple of seconds for 
the JTAG type of programming to take place. If you use the QSPI, it’ll happen very quickly in a 
matter of milliseconds or microseconds in any type of situation. When you’re working with SPI 
bus, you can connect multiple devices to an SPI bus. So, you’ll have a master, and you can have 
multiple slaves. Each slave device you add requires a new CS pin in order to select that individual 
device. If you look at it from a high level, in the SPI interface, we have a master that selects a slave 
device it wants to talk to, and then the master controls the serial clock (SCK) and the MOSI line 
and transmits data down to the selected slave device, which it has selected from the control select 
line, and then slave responds by transmitting data back on that MISO line, so the master sends and 
it initiates the conversation. The slave only responds when the master sends information to them 
and requests something back. So, even if you have everything, depending on a protocol that’s been 
established or the actual device, you have to look at the datasheet; for example, we’re going to refer 
back to the memory chip again. If we want to read a memory location back, we’re going to send 
down the command, and we’ll send down the address, and then the chip is going to respond back 
with the actual contents in that address. If we go to a write command, we’re going to tell the chip 
that, OK, we want to do a write. We specify the address and the data we’re writing, and a lot of 
times, depending on the chip, you have to look at the datasheet. But they’ll tell you that once you 
send the write command down, it will either do nothing, or it’ll respond with an acknowledgment 
or give you some sort of response to say that write command has finished or has been successfully 
interpreted as a write command. In this SPI interface project, the FPGA is going to be the master, 
and the external memory chip is going to be the slave. So, we’re going to initiate communication 
with the external memory chip, where we’re going to ask the chip to read us back data at a specific 
address location, and we will write data down to the actual chip to where we want to write any 
actual data we want to write on that chip. So, let’s go ahead and see how the SPI interface actually 
works, and first off, we will talk about what the actual physical lines look like. So, if we have our 
master device, we’ll call it the master, and this could be anything. This could be a microcontroller, 
an FPGA, or any sort of chip. So, we’ll have a CS line. Also, we’ll have a SCK line, and we have our 
MOSI line, or master out, slave in data line, and our MISO line. We’re just assuming a standard 
SPI interface where we’re sending down one bit of data for each clock cycle. So, if we have a slave 
device, we have a CS as well. Also, we have a serial clock (SCK) line, a MOSI line, and a MISO line. 
So, the CS is driven by the master – that’s an output of the master and an input to a slave. Our serial 
clock (SCK) is the same – it’s an output of our master and an input to the slave, and that’s driven 
by the master as well. Our MOSI lines are master output, slave input, so that’s the data the master 
device transmits to the slave device, which is driven by the master as well. Then, the MISO lines 
come from the slaves, so it’s the master input, slave output that goes back to the master. So, when 
the slave responds, it sends the information back on the MISO line. So, that’s the connection that 
you have between your master and your slave devices, as depicted in Figure 12.2.
Next, we’re going to talk about what the actual waveforms would look like. So, the master – the 
way it’s going to work – has that control select (CS) line, and when it’s idle, it’ll be high. Then 
it’s going to drop low. When it drops low is when the slave starts looking at the serial clock line 
and the MOSI line to determine the data that’s coming in. So, we’ll then have our SCK clock, or 
synchronous clock, that’s high. Then, all of a sudden, when our control select line goes low, we’ll 
see when it occurs at that point. Our SCK clock will then go low, and it’s just going to toggle at

mitted on the rising edge of the clock, except instead of on the MOSI line, it’s on the MISO line this 
time, and in the whole time while this is going on, our MOSI line is going to just stay idle or stay 
high because we’re not going to be transmitting at this point, and we’re waiting for the response. 
This will just stay high throughout this entire time period and then our MISO line will come here 
and for our first rising edge, we’re high, so we know that is a one and then we’ll drop low, and go

the MISO line is low, that’s a zero, and so that our control select is going to stay low until around 
there once the master receives a response back from a slave and no longer wants to talk to it or talk 
to a different slave, it’s then that control select line will be high. So, at this point, if the master sends

So, the next thing is, if we want to have a master and two slaves, what would that look like? If we 
have a single master, we’re going to use a synchronous clock (SCK), a MOSI line, and a MISO line.

the number of CS signals we need. Since we have two slaves, we’ll have CS1 and CS2. For Slave1,

have a memory chip, an analog to digital conversion chip, or any type of integrated circuit on your 
PCB board. We’ll have the SCK, MOSI, and MISO lines, and each slave will also have a CS. The way 
this will be connected is: CS2 from the master goes to Slave2, and CS1 from the master goes to the 
CS of Slave1, as shown in Figure 12.4. The SCK clock, MOSI, and MISO lines are shared between 
the master, Slave1, and Slave2 – Slave 1, Slave 2, and any additional slaves. The CS lines determine 
which slave is currently active. If we have a third slave, we’d introduce a third CS line. That’s how 
you connect multiple devices together using the SPI interface.

interface. But in our case, we are going to implement SPI interface using VHDL. So, for our Micro

developed this VHDL SPI design, we’ll know specifically how many inputs and outputs we need, 
and what bit widths are required for the MicroBlaze MCS to communicate with the SPI design. As 
for our SPI design, we are going to implement it later. The most important is implementing this 
VHDL SPI design interface, so we can talk to the memory. We’ll just make a block here and call it 
memory. This is external to the chip on the board. This SPI interface will either write data to the 
memory or read information from the memory and feed it back into the MicroBlaze MCS softcore 
processor. We are also going to interface to a computer, so we’ll have our computer using a COM 
port where we’ll be using Tera Term or any other type of terminal emulator. That’s just a UART 
interface between the computer and the MicroBlaze MCS. We know the MicroBlaze MCS supports 
UART. So, we’ll have to transmit data to our computer and receive. Those commands will be sent 
down to the MicroBlaze processor to determine what values to write to memory. We’ll then use our 
general-purpose inputs and outputs to interface with the VHDL SPI design, and that, in turn, will 
communicate with the memory. So, it’s kind of a whole block chain, as depicted in Figure 12.5.
We’ve got our computer, which we’re going to use to send information to our processor. The pro -
cessor will then send that information to our SPI design, which will forward it to the memory, and 
then obviously, we’re going to have other generics, such as a reset and a clock that’ll come in there, 
and these are just kind of a global reset that we’ll probably just tie to a slide switch. We’ll have 
our clock as well, and our SPI design here will have a clock, and we’ll try to pull that same clock 
from there, which will then come into our SPI design. Next is UART, and we might also include 
another slide switch here that uses a GPIO to indicate whether we want to read from or write to 
the memory. So, we can do this in different ways, but what we will have here is a general-purpose

12.4   External SPI Flash Chip Memory Organization
This section is about the external SPI flash chip memory organization. In this section, we’re 
going to explain and help you understand how the Micron SPI chip that’s located on the Arty A7 
development board is structured. So, we have the Micron datasheet for the flash chip on the Arty 
A7 development board, and they have a block diagram that’s located in their memory organization 
structure, as shown in Figure 12.1. So there, you’ve got a block that they call control logic, and this 
is where you have the external interface, which is shown with this hold#, write#, S#, C, and your 
DQ0 through DQ3. This is the SPI interface. So, we know the memory chip supports the extended, 
the dual, and the quad SPI, and we were working with the extended SPI, which just uses your 
clock, your CS, your MOSI line, and your MISO line. These are used to interface with your control 
logic and specifically interface with the I/O shift register. The I/O shift register is what we use to 
come into here, where we have the actual block, this main block here, where they have 256 bytes 
(page size) from the 0000000h all the way up to your 00FFFFFF. That is the actual memory itself, 
and that’s what we’re trying to access.
So, we’re just shifting in command and information into this I/O shift register that we send down 
to this address register and counter, as well as a 256-byte data buffer, and we send down the data in 
multiple shots. So, this is a good thing – when you look at the Micron datasheet, this is something 
to review to get an understanding of what you need to do when working with this SPI flash chip, 
both in terms of how it works at a high level and the different commands you need to send down 
if you want to interface with it and get this type of information. So, what we’re going to do is show 
what this means for us. We’ll just call this the external flash memory, and we are going to give a 
high-level explanation of what we need to know or what’s important for us. If we have our external 
flash memory chip, let’s just take a look at it from a block diagram view, which is helpful when 
you’re working with memory or similar components. It’s good to get an understanding by looking 
at it from a block memory view. So, we will say that from up here we have our address starting at 
0x000000h (0 MB), and this is our address zero, all the way down to 0xFFFFFFh, which is like our 
16 MB. Now, what’s important is if you look up, there’s an app note (XAPP951 v1.3) that specifies 
when you load a configuration file – or in this case, take a project we created in Vivado and load it 
into this external flash memory – it’s going to start from address zero to wherever it sees a break. 
So, what that means is, if we want to store additional information or use this external flash or 
anything else, we need to set aside the space from zero to however large our .mcs file, or our con-
figuration file, is. For the Arty A7, they say it’s approximately 2 MB. Now, that’s kind of an approx -
imation based on your design and how much memory it consumes. In this case, we would just go 
ahead and put a line there at 0x1FFFFF ( ≈ 2 MB), and that marks approximately the 2 MB line. 
This is where we store the .mcs file. We get this file from the .bit files we’ve generated. So, using a 
tool (Vivado), we convert that .bit file into a .mcs file, which we then load onto our external flash –  
this right here is our external flash. If we’re going to write to this flash or in the future store any 
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

192 12 The SPI Interface Project
additional memory or information onto this flash chip, we want to stay out of the address regions 
from zero all the way up to the 0x1FFFFFh address (approximate 2 MB). You want to leave that 
alone for the configuration because the app note tells us that when the Arty A7 or the Arix FPGA 
boots up and is configured to read from the QSPI flash, it starts at address zero and reads through 
the flash until it sees the end of file indicator, signifying the .mcs file is complete. If we were to 
load this configuration file down here at the bottom, then it won’t load into the FPGA. Or if we 
overwrite any of this information – if our SPI interface stores information on this flash chip and 
overwrites some of the configuration, then erase and overwrite it – there will be an issue where it 
tries to reassemble the corrupted data, and your design won’t load because the checks don’t match, 
and there’ll be a lot of issues. So, the important thing here is you need to know where the config -
uration file is going to be stored. We approximate this to be 2 MB here. So, we’d still have around 
14 MB to store what we want. So, we would probably make a line somewhere at 0x7FFFFFF, which 
is approximately at the 8 MB mark, and say, “OK, this right here is safe for user flash use,” and 
that’s where you could store additional information. This gives you the buffer for safety. You could 
probably move it up a little closer since you’re not likely to use all of that. But just to be safe, give 
yourself 8 MB and use this space for storing in flash if needed. It’s really important to note that the 
configuration file should start at address zero and just keep writing until it’s full. So, you’ll want 
your configuration file to begin at address zero. This is important when loading it onto the flash, as 
it ensures the FPGA will automatically load the configuration or program we’ve created – without 
needing to be connected to a computer each time the board powers on. This becomes even more 
important if you’re working with a board that contains an FPGA along with a microprocessor, 
microcontroller, or other control logic. In that case, the microprocessor, microcontroller, or even 
the FPGA itself could take in a new configuration file, a new .mcs file, and write it to this loca-
tion. When it reboots, it’ll take on that new file. So, you could even have something like a multiple 
configuration file system, where your primary configuration file starts at address location zero. If 
you have a second configuration file, you might store that one starting at the 8 MB or 0x7FFFFFh 
address. In this setup, you’d store the old one at 0x7FFFFFh and the new one at 0x000000h. When 
you boot up, the configuration file that we take gets loaded, as illustrated in Figure 12.6.
It is very important to kind of understand the whole memory organization of the flash chip, because 
when you’re working with it, you want to be careful that you’re not erasing specific stuff in here (con -
figuration file (.mcs) area starting at 0x000000h address). This is very safety-critical, and you don’t

is part of the typical development flow: you first load your design onto the device using the JTAG 
cable, as we’ve done with previous projects. This approach is ideal for debugging, testing, and ver -

QSPI flash for standalone operation. Finally, click OK to complete the process successfully. So, if

13.1   Introduction
In the rapidly evolving landscape of digital design, the Xilinx Vivado Design Suite stands out as 
an indispensable tool for engineers and enthusiasts. Its powerful features cater to a wide array 
of applications, from simple field-programmable gate array (FPGA) projects to complex system- 
on-chip (SoC) designs. This chapter delves into a collection of miscellaneous projects that exem-
plify the versatility of Vivado, bridging theoretical knowledge with practical implementation. As 
we embark on this journey through various projects, we’ll explore different aspects of hardware 
design, ranging from basic circuit implementations to more advanced concepts such as signal 
processing and system integration. Each project showcases the unique capabilities of Vivado, high-
lighting its user-friendly interface, comprehensive design validation tools, and seamless integration 
with simulation and synthesis workflows.
13.2   The I2C Interface Project
This block diagram of the project is shown in Figure 13.1.
By right-clicking and selecting “Run Connection Automation” and also “Regenerate Layout,” 
we can easily connect and use the required IP cores. Then, we use the following Address Editor 
window to configure the data and instruction memory of MicroBlaze, as depicted in Figure 13.2. 
By right-clicking on microblaze_0, we can also select “Auto Assign Address.”
Then, we right-click on the block diagram file name and select Create HDL wrapper…, and then 
run synthesis, run implementation, and generate bitstream, respectively. In the next step, we go to 
the File menu, and select Export, and then Export Hardware… options. Finally, we choose Launch 
SDK from File menu to open the Xilinx SDK software. We create an application project by going to 
File> New > Application Project. In the New Project window, we enter a project name, select C as 
the language, leave others as default, and click the Next button. Then, we choose Peripheral Tests 
as our template and click the Finish button. Then, we copy and paste the following codes into the 
testperiph.c file.
#include <stdio.h>
#include "xil_printf.h"
#include "xparameters.h"
#include "XIic.h"

u8 readReg(u16 reg_addr)
{
      u8 write_buf[3];
      write_buf[0] = (reg_addr >> 8) & 0xff;
      write_buf[1] = reg_addr & 0xff;
      u8 read_buf;
      XIic_Send(0x40800000, 0x36, write_buf, 2, 0);
      XIic_Recv(0x40800000, 0x36, &read_buf, 1, 0);
      return read_buf;
}
   
   
int main()
{
   
    u8 data;
    data = readReg(0x300b);
    xil_printf("%x\r\n", data);
   
    return 0;
}
The code provided is a C program that utilizes a Simple I2C interface to read a value from a 
specific register of a device connected to an I2C bus. Let’s break it down step-by-step:

	 1.	Header Files:
	 ▪	 #include <stdio.h>: Includes the standard input–output library for functions like printf.
	 ▪	 #include “xil_printf.h”: A custom library provided by Xilinx for output on specific hardware, 
allowing formatted output similar to standard printf.
	 ▪	 #include “xparameters.h”: A header file usually generated by Xilinx tools, containing 
parameters for the hardware system, such as device addresses.
	 ▪	 #include “XIic.h”: Supports the use of I2C interface in Xilinx systems.
	 2.	Function Definition readReg(u16 reg_addr):
	 ▪	 This function reads a byte from a specific register address (a 16-bit address) via the I2C 
interface.
	 ▪	 The variable write_buf is defined with a size of three bytes, which is used to format the reg -
ister address into two bytes: a high byte (write_buf[0]) and a low byte (write_buf[1]).
	 ▪	 The address is divided into two bytes using bitwise operations. The high byte is obtained by 
shifting the address right by 8 bits, and the low byte is obtained by masking with 0xff.
	 ▪	 read_buf is defined to hold the single byte that will be read from the device.
	 ▪	 The function then utilizes the XIic_Send function to send the constructed address to the 
device at I2C address 0x36.
	 ▪	 After sending, it calls XIic_Recv to receive a single byte from the same device address, which 
contains the data from the specified register.
	 ▪	 Finally, the function returns the received byte.
	 3.	main Function:
	 ▪	 A variable data of type u8 (unsigned 8-bit integer) is declared.
	 ▪	 The program calls readReg(0x300b), which reads the data from register address 0x300b and 
stores it in data.
	 ▪	 It then uses xil_printf to print the hexadecimal representation of data followed by a carriage 
return and a newline.
In summary, the code is designed to communicate with an I2C device, read a single byte from 
register 0x300b, and print that byte in hexadecimal format. This code is suitable for embedded sys -
tems using Xilinx hardware and the I2C communication protocol.
Finally, we can build and generate the ELF file and then associate it with the Vivado project from 
Tools menu and Associate ELF Files… option.
13.3   The VHDL CORDIC Sine-Cosine Generator
CORDIC (Coordinate Rotation Digital Computer) is an algorithm used to perform a wide range 
of mathematical functions, particularly trigonometric, hyperbolic, exponential, and logarithmic 
calculations. It is efficient for hardware implementation because it requires only shift and add 
operations, making it suitable for FPGA architectures. In the context of VHDL (VHSIC Hardware 
Description Language) and Vivado, which is a design suite provided by Xilinx for FPGA and SoC 
development, CORDIC can be implemented as a VHDL design. This involves creating entities that 
define the CORDIC algorithm to compute various functions, such as sine, cosine, and tangent, 
with hardware efficiency. The key features of VHDL CORDIC in Vivado are listed as follows:
	 1.	Algorithm Implementation:
	 ▪	 The CORDIC algorithm can be implemented in VHDL to achieve various mathematical 
computations with a minimal number of hardware resources.
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 215 ---
200 13 Some Miscellaneous Projects with Vivado
	 ▪	 The VHDL code typically includes iterative structures to handle the rotation operations and 
convergence of angles.
	 2.	Static versus Dynamic Modes:
	 ▪	 CORDIC can operate in various modes, including vectoring mode and rotation mode, which 
can be selected based on the application requirements.
	 3.	Fixed-Point Representation:
	 ▪	 CORDIC typically uses fixed-point arithmetic, which is well-suited for FPGA implementa-
tions to balance precision and resource utilization.
	 4.	Parameterization:
	 ▪	 CORDIC implementations can be parameterized for different precision requirements 
(number of iterations, bit-width, etc.), allowing designers to optimize for speed or resource 
usage.
	 5.	Integration with Other Designs:
	 ▪	 CORDIC modules can be integrated with other digital signal processing (DSP) components 
in an FPGA design, such as filters, to enhance functionality.
Implementing CORDIC in VHDL within the Vivado environment provides an efficient way to 
perform complex mathematical computations with an FPGA. By taking advantage of the CORDIC 
algorithm’s properties, designers can achieve high performance while minimizing resource con -
sumption, thereby making it a popular choice for applications in signal processing, communications, 
and control systems. In this section, we are going to generate sine and cosine waveforms using the 
VHDL CORDIC using Vivado. The CORDIC IP core settings window is illustrated in Figure 13.3.
After locating the CORDIC IP core in the IP catalog and configuring the settings as demon-
strated in Figure 13.3, click the OK button. This action opens a new window, where we should click 
the Generate button, as shown in Figure 13.4.

We write the following code into the file sincos_tb.vhd.
library IEEE;
use IEEE.std_logic_1164.all;
use IEEE.numeric_std.all;
entity sincos_tb is
end sincos_tb;
architecture arch of sincos_tb is
constant CLOCK_PERIOD: time := 10ns;
constant PI_POS: signed(15 downto 0) := "0110010010001000";
constant PI_NEG: signed(15 downto 0) := "1001101101111000";
constant PHASE_INC: integer := 256;
signal clk: std_logic := '0';
signal rst: std_logic := '1';
signal phase: signed(15 downto 0);
signal phase_tvalid: std_logic;
signal cos, sin: std_logic_vector(15 downto 0);
signal sincos_tvalid: std_logic;
begin
sincos_inst: entity work.sincos
   port map(
     clk => clk,
     phase => std_logic_vector(phase),
     phase_tvalid => phase_tvalid,
     cos => cos,
     sin => sin,
     sincos_tvalid => sincos_tvalid
     );
process
begin
    clk <= '0';
    wait for CLOCK_PERIOD/2;
    clk <= '1';
    wait for CLOCK_PERIOD/2;
end process;
process
begin
    rst <= '1';
    wait for CLOCK_PERIOD*10;
    rst <= '0';
    wait;
end process;
   
process(clk)
begin
   if rst = '1' then
      phase <= (others => '0');
      phase_tvalid <= '0';
   else
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 219 ---
204 13 Some Miscellaneous Projects with Vivado
      phase_tvalid <= '1';
      if(phase+PHASE_INC < PI_POS) then
         phase <= phase+PHASE_INC;
      else
         phase <= PI_NEG;
      end if;
   end if;
end process;
end arch;

13.5   The LUT -based Sine Wave in VHDL
In this section, we are going to create a sine wave based on a lookup table (LUT) in VHDL using 
Vivado. This technique is useful for functions like PWM. We will use Google Colab, which is a 
hosted Jupyter notebook service, to generate the LUT values for the sine waveform, and we use the 
following codes.
import numpy as np
from numpy import sin, pi
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
x = np.linspace(0,1,500)
lut = sin(pi*x)*7500+7500
plt.plot(x,lut)
The generated sine half-wave signal is shown in Figure 13.51.

for i,val in enumerate(lut):
    if i % 20:

       print(int(val),end=',')
       print(' ')

signal lut_sig: unsigned(15 downto 0);
   
begin
   
sine <= std_logic_vector(lut_sig);
   
process(clk,rst) is 
variable cnt: integer range 0 to 500;
begin
    if rst = '0' then
       cnt := 0;
       lut_sig <= (others => '0');
    elsif rising_edge(clk) then
       lut_sig <= to_unsigned(LUT(cnt),16);
       if cnt > 498 then
          cnt := 0;
       else
          cnt := cnt + 1;
       end if;
    end if;
end process;
   
end Behavioral;

13.6 The Symmetrical PWM in VHDL
 229
13.6   The Symmetrical PWM in VHDL
In this section, we are going to implement a symmetrical PWM carrier for PWM generation, and 
compare it with a sine wave to generate two-level PWM signal, which can be used later in power 
electronics converters. We previously implemented a trial PWM carrier in Section 13.3. Now, we 
will make some changes in the trial PWM to be a symmetrical up–down counter. The codes for the 
file PWM_CAR.vhd are as follows.
library IEEE;
use IEEE.STD_LOGIC_1164.ALL;
use IEEE.numeric_std.all;
   
entity PWM_CAR is
    Port ( clk : in STD_LOGIC;
           rst : in STD_LOGIC;
           carrier : out STD_LOGIC_VECTOR (15 downto 0)
          );
end PWM_CAR;
   
architecture Behavioral of PWM_CAR is
   
signal car_sig: unsigned (15 downto 0) := x"0000";
signal car_dir: std_logic;
begin
carrier <= std_logic_vector(car_sig);
   
process(clk) is
begin
   if rst = '0' then
   -- Initial start up
      car_dir <= '1';
      car_sig <= (others => '0');
   elsif rising_edge(clk) then
    -- PWM direction flag
      if car_sig >= (15000-2) then
Figure 13.69   The added “sine_lut” module in the Diagram window.
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 245 ---
230 13 Some Miscellaneous Projects with Vivado
          car_dir <= '0';
          car_sig <= to_unsigned(15000,16);
      elsif car_sig <= 2 then
          car_dir <= '1';
          car_sig <= (others => '0');
      end if;
      -- Actual PWM counter
      if car_dir = '1' then
          car_sig <= car_sig + 2;
      else
          car_sig <= car_sig - 2;
      end if;
   end if;
end process;
   
end Behavioral;
Then we can run the behavioral simulation as shown in Figure 13.70.
We click on the Restart button in the opened window, then right-click on the “clk” signal, select 
the Force Clock option, and apply the settings as depicted in Figure 13.71.
Also, we right-click on the “rst” and force a constant value of zero, as illustrated in Figure 13.72.
We then click the Run for  100  μs   button once, and then change the Force value of “rst” to 1, as 
demonstrated in Figure 13.73.
We click on the Run for  100  μs   button multiple times and then click on the Zoom Fit button 
to view the entire waveforms window, as shown in Figure 13.74. Now, we can right-click on the  
“carrier” and select the Waveform Style as Analog, as depicted in Figure 13.75.
We can see in Figure 13.76 that a symmetrical triangular waveform is produced for “carrier” .
It is also better to right-click on the “carrier” and select the Radix as Unsigned Decimal as illus -
trated in Figure 13.77.

library IEEE;

entity PWM_CAR is

    generic(
        pwm_step: integer :=2
        );
    Port ( clk : in STD_LOGIC;
           rst : in STD_LOGIC;
           carrier : out STD_LOGIC_VECTOR (15 downto 0)
          );
end PWM_CAR;
   
architecture Behavioral of PWM_CAR is
   
signal car_sig: unsigned (15 downto 0) := x"0000";
signal car_dir: std_logic;
begin
carrier <= std_logic_vector(car_sig);
   
process(clk,rst) is
variable step_var: unsigned(15 downto 0);
begin
   if rst = '0' then
   -- Initial start up
      car_dir <= '1';
      car_sig <= (others => '0');
   elsif rising_edge(clk) then
    -- PWM direction flag
      step_var := to_unsigned(pwm_step,16);
      if car_sig >= (15000-step_var) then
Figure 13.77   Selecting the Radix as Unsigned 
Decimal for “carrier”.
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. 234 13 Some Miscellaneous Projects with Vivado
          car_dir <= '0';
          car_sig <= to_unsigned(15000,16);
      elsif car_sig <= step_var then
          car_dir <= '1';
          car_sig <= (others => '0');
      end if;
      -- Actual PWM counter
      if car_dir = '1' then
          car_sig <= car_sig + step_var;
      else
          car_sig <= car_sig - step_var;
      end if;
   end if;
end process;

library IEEE;
use IEEE.STD_LOGIC_1164.ALL;
use IEEE.NUMERIC_STD.ALL;
   
entity sine_lut is
     Port(

(
-- We do copy and paste 10000 LUT values here
);

sine <= std_logic_vector(lut_sig) when state = '0' else
        std_logic_vector(15000-lut_sig);       
   
process(clk,rst) is 
variable cnt: integer range 0 to 10000;
begin
    if rst = '0' then
       cnt := 0;
       state <= '0';
       lut_sig <= (others => '0');
    elsif rising_edge(clk) then
       lut_sig <= to_unsigned(LUT(cnt),16);
       if cnt > 9998 then
          cnt := 0;
          state <= not state;
       else
          cnt := cnt + 1;
       end if;
    end if;
end process;
end Behavioral;

14.1   Introduction
In the evolving landscape of digital design, the need for efficient, reliable, and rapid development 
methodologies is more critical than ever. This chapter delves into one of the most powerful inte -
grations available to engineers: the combination of Simulink HDL Coder and Xilinx Vivado. These 
tools represent a convergence of high-level modeling and hardware synthesis, enabling designers 
to transition seamlessly from abstract system representations to actual hardware implementations. 
Simulink provides a dynamic platform for simulating complex systems, allowing engineers to visu -
alize and refine their designs through intuitive block diagrams. Meanwhile, HDL Coder empowers 
users to automatically generate synthesizable VHDL code directly from Simulink models, streamlin -
ing the path to FPGA and ASIC deployment. As designers increasingly turn to high-level abstractions, 
understanding the nuances of VHDL generation becomes essential for leveraging the full capabilities 
of modern design environments. This chapter will guide you through the critical processes involved in 
harnessing Simulink HDL Coder to create VHDL code tailored for implementation in Xilinx Vivado. 
From setting up your Simulink model to configuring the HDL Coder settings and navigating the intri -
cacies of the generated code, you will gain insights into best practices that enhance design efficiency 
and performance. By the end of this chapter, you will not only appreciate the synergy between these 
powerful tools but also acquire practical skills that elevate your digital design workflow to new heights.
14.2   The Half Adder Circuit Design
In this section, we are going to design a half adder circuit in Simulink. In the command window of 
MATLAB, we type the following command.
>> simulink
After opening the Simulink environment, we use the following code to open the HDL Coder 
library browser.
>> hdllib
Then, using the HDL Coder library browser, we create a new model titled Half_Adder, as shown 
in Figure 14.1. The Half_Adder_HDL subsystem model is shown in Figure 14.2.
All the elements, except the rounding function and uniform random number generator, are 
available under the Commonly Used Blocks in the HDL Coder tab. In order to add the rounding

15.1   Flowchart of Multiplier
In this section, we are going to implement a multiplier using a flowchart in Simulink. The algo -
rithms based on state flowcharts in HDL Coder for VHDL programming using Vivado are dis -
cussed in detail. The flowchart of the multiplier (P = A × B) is shown in Figure 15.1. We assume 
that B ≠ 0 and P = 0 initially.
15
Application of Flowcharts in Simulink for VHDL Code Generation

Read A, B
P = P + A
B = B – 1
B = 0
