Figure 15.1   The flowchart of the multiplier (P = A × B).
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 287 ---
272 15 Application of Flowcharts in Simulink for VHDL Code Generation
We will use the fixed-point numeric type to define the parameters of the flowchart in a MATLB 
m-file. For example, if we write and run the following code, we will have the results as follows.
clear all; close all; clc
% Define 5-bit unsigned number
nt = numerictype(0,5,0);
a = fi(3,nt)
a.bin
   
a = 
     3
          DataTypeMode: Fixed-point: binary point scaling
            Signedness: Unsigned
            WordLength: 5
        FractionLength: 0
ans =
00011
When starting the flowchart in the Stateflow environment of Simulink, we begin with the initial 
state (S0) by initializing all outputs to zero or predefined values using the constants defined in the 
MATLAB m-file. Then, we implement the flowchart step-by-step, recognizing each step as a sep -
arate state in Stateflow. If we encounter a complex operation, we divide it into multiple steps or 
states for clarity and manageability. Constants defined in the m-file are set as parameters, while 
intermediate variables are defined as locals in the Stateflow Model Explorer. We assign inputs to 
local variables and similarly define inputs and outputs in the Model Explorer. When performing 
any arithmetic operation within a state, make sure to convert it into fixed-point format using the fi 
command. For example, we have the following code.
P = fi(P+A,0,14,0); % Perform P = P + A and save it as 14-bit unsigned number
B = fi(B-a1,0,7,0); % Perform B = B - 1 and save it as 7-bit unsigned number
We can also consider using MATLAB functions or Simulink functions to simplify and compact 
the flowchart. The designed Stateflow chart in Simulink is shown in Figure 15.2.
Figure 15.2   The designed Stateflow chart in Simulink.
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 288 ---
15.1 Flowchart of Multiplier
 273
Parameters (constants) are already defined inside the m-file (FC_Parameters.m) as the following 
codes.
clear all; close all; clc
N = 7; %nbits
nt1 = numerictype(0,1,0);
nt2 = numerictype(0,N,0);
nt3 = numerictype(0,2*N,0);
   
% 1-bit constants
a0 = fi(0,nt1);
a1 = fi(1,nt1);
   
% Inputs
Ain = fi(50,nt2);
Bin = fi(12,nt2);
   
% Initial values
A0 = fi(0,nt2);
B0 = fi(0,nt2);
P0 = fi(0,nt3);
To use these constants inside the Stateflow, we should run the m-file. The A0, B0, P0, a0, and a1 
are fixed-point parameters defined inside the m-file. The A, B, and P are local or intermediate var -
iables. Ain, Bin, and Start are the inputs, while Product and Done are the outputs. From View →  
Model Explorer →  Model Explorer, we open the Model Explorer window as depicted in Figure 15.3.
To add parameters, local variables, inputs, and outputs for the Stateflow design in Figure 15.2, 
in the Model Explorer window, click on the Add in the menu bar and select the Data option, as 
illustrated in Figure 15.4.
Figure 15.3   Selecting the Model Explorer option.

15.2 Flowchart of GCD Calculator
 283
15.2   Flowchart of GCD Calculator
In this section, we will implement the GCD calculator algorithm directly from its flowchart using 
HDL Coder and simulate the design in the MATLAB Simulink environment to verify its func -
tionality. Next, we will utilize the HDL Coder to generate synthesizable VHDL code from the 
designed GCD calculator implemented in Stateflow. Finally, we will synthesize and implement 
the generated VHDL code using Xilinx Vivado. The flowchart of the GCD calculator is shown in 
Figure 15.27.
To effectively implement the digital system design algorithm directly from the flowchart, we need 
to carefully analyze the flowchart and identify the inputs, outputs, constants, and intermediate 
variables. Also, we should initialize constants and inputs using a separate MATLAB script (m-file) 
using fixed-point numeric objects such as “numerictype” and “fi” , as described in the previous 
section. When starting the flowchart in Stateflow within Simulink, begin with the initial state (S0) 
by initializing all outputs to zero or to predefined values using the constants previously defined 
in m-file. Then, implement the flowchart step-by-step, recognizing each step as a separate state 
in Stateflow chart. If you encounter a complex operation, divide it into multiple steps or states 
for clarity and manageability. Constants defined in the m-file should be added as parameters, and 
intermediate variables should be defined as local variables in the Model Explorer of Stateflow in 
Simulink. Inputs should be assigned to local variables. Similarly, we define inputs and outputs in 
the Model Explorer. When you perform any arithmetic operation at any state, make sure to convert 
it into fixed point using the “fi” function, as discussed in the previous section. Also, it is better to 
consider using MATLAB functions or Simulink functions to simplify and compact the flowchart 
within Stateflow. The Stateflow chart of the GCD calculator is depicted in Figure 15.28.
Parameters (constants) are already defined inside the m-file (FC_Parameters.m) as the follow -
ing codes.
clear all; close all; clc
N = 7; %nbits
nt1 = numerictype(0,1,0);
nt2 = numerictype(0,N,0);
 
Start
Read A, B
A < B A > B
B = B – A A : B A = A – B
A = B
GCD = A
Stop
Figure 15.27   The flowchart of GCD calculator.

284 15 Application of Flowcharts in Simulink for VHDL Code Generation
% 1-bit constants
a0 = fi(0,nt1);
a1 = fi(1,nt1);
 
% Inputs
Ain = fi(65,nt2);
Bin = fi(13,nt2);
 
% Initial values
A0 = fi(0,nt2);
B0 = fi(0,nt2);
To use these constants inside the Stateflow, we should run the m-file. The variables A0, B0, a0, 
and a1 are fixed-point parameters defined in the m-file. A and B are local or intermediate vari-
ables. Additionally, Ain, Bin, and Start are inputs, while GCD and Done are outputs. From View 
→  Model Explorer →  Model Explorer, we open the Model Explorer window in order to add param-
eters, local variables, inputs, and outputs for the Stateflow design shown in Figure 15.28. In the 
Model Explorer window, we click on Add in the menu bar, select the Data option, and begin adding 
the variables defined in the Stateflow chart. The complete list of added variables for the Stateflow 
chart is shown in Figure 15.29.

15.3 Flowchart of Booth’s Multiplication
 291
15.3   Flowchart of Booth’s Multiplication
In this section, we are going to implement Booth’s multiplication algorithm directly from its flow -
chart. We will simulate the design in the MATLAB Simulink environment to verify its function-
ality. Then, we will utilize the HDL Coder to generate synthesizable VHDL code from the designed 
Booth’s multiplication algorithm implemented in the Stateflow, and finally, we will synthesize 
and implement the generated VHDL code using Xilinx Vivado. The flowchart for Booth’s mul-
tiplication algorithm is shown in Figure 15.42. In order to implement the digital system design 
algorithm directly from the flowchart, we must carefully analyze it and identify the inputs, out -
puts, constants, and intermediate variables. Constants and inputs should be initialized using a 
separate MATLAB script (m-file), where all values are defined using fixed-point numeric objects 
such as “numerictype” and “fi” , as described in the previous sections. We usually begin with the 
initial state (S0) by initializing all outputs to zero or predefined values, using the constants previ-
ously defined in the m-file.
Then, we implement the flowchart step by step, recognizing each step as a separate state 
in the Stateflow of Simulink. If we encounter a complex operation, it is better to divide it 
into multiple steps or states for clarity and manageability. Constants defined in the m-file are 
declared as parameters, while intermediate variables are defined as local variables in the Model 
Explorer of Stateflow. Inputs are assigned to local variables and similarly, inputs and outputs 
are defined in the Model Explorer as well. When we perform any arithmetic operation at any 
state, we should make sure that the values are converted to fixed point using “fi” , as discussed in 
previous sections. It is also better to consider using MATLAB functions or Simulink functions 
to simplify and compact the flowchart design in the Stateflow of Simulink. The Stateflow dia -
gram for Booth’s multiplication algorithm is depicted in Figure 15.43. You should note that 
Start
      A = 0, Q–1 = 0
    Count = n
  Q = Multiplier
M = Multiplicand
Q0Q–1
M = n-bit multiplicand
Q = n-bit multiplier
A = n-bit register
Q–1 = 1-bit D-FF
01
A = A + M
10
A = A – M
00 or 11
Arithmetic Shift Right (A, Q, Q–1)
Count = Count – 1
No
Count = 0? Note: Product = {A, Q}
Yes
Stop

292 15 Application of Flowcharts in Simulink for VHDL Code Generation
in Figure 15.43, Q −1  is represented as Qm1. To extract the least significant bit (LSB) of Q, we 
can use getlsb(Q) or bitget(Q,1). The command for performing a 1-bit right shift of variable A 
is bitsra(A,1). Also, in order to set the most significant bit (MSB) of Q (the seventh bit in this 
case) with the LSB of A, we use the command bitset(Q,7,getlsb(A)). Moreover, to concatenate 
the variables A and Q, we use the command bitconcat(A,Q), as demonstrated in Figure 15.43.
Parameters (constants) are already defined inside the m-file (FC_Parameters.m) as the follow -
ing codes.
clear all; close all; clc
N = 7; %nbits
n = ceil(log2(N));
ntc = numerictype(0,n,0);
nbits = fi(N,ntc);
c0 = fi(0,ntc);
 
nt = numerictype(1,N,0);
 
% Inputs
Min = fi(36,nt);
Qin = fi(24,nt);
 
% Initial conditions
Mi = fi(0,nt);
Qi = fi(0,nt);
Ai = fi(0,nt);
 
% 1-bit constants
nt1 = numerictype(0,1,0);
a0 = fi(0,nt1);
a1 = fi(1,nt1);

b3 = fi(3,nt2);
To use these constants inside the Stateflow, we first need to run the m-file. Mi, Qi, Ai, nbits, c0,

function [Ao,Qo] = ASR(Ax,Qx)
Qo = bitsra(Qx,1);
Qo = bitset(Qo,7,getlsb(Ax));
Ao = bitsra(Ax,1);
We can generate the VHDL code for this subsystem using HDL Coder as well. To do this, we 
write the following code in the MATLAB command window.
>> hdlsetup('FlowChart_Booth2')

industrial control system implemented in the Stateflow environment of Simulink. Finally, we will

tions for the system illustrated in Figure 15.57 is as follows:
	 •	 The box is initially positioned at LS1 (LS1 is closed).
	 •	 When the start button is pressed, the conveyor motor activates, moving the box to position A 
(LS1 opens).
	 •	 The conveyor transports the box to position A and then halts (the position is confirmed by 
eight off-to-on pulses from the encoder to the up counter).
	 •	 Following a 10-s delay, the conveyor resumes movement, carrying the box to LS2 where it 
stops (LS2 closes).
	 •	 An emergency stop push button is available to halt the process at any moment.
	 •	 If the process is interrupted using the emergency stop push button, both the timer and counter 
will automatically reset.

16.1   Introduction
In this chapter, we are going to implement a machine-learning regression model on FPGA using 
Simulink and Vivado. First of all, we download a dataset from Kaggle that is suitable for the imple -
mentation of linear regression. Using MATLAB, we train the linear regression model based on 
the least squares method. We use the admission dataset to predict the chances of admission to a 
university based on various input parameters. Then, we implement our trained machine learning 
regression model and generate synthesizable VHDL code using HDL Coder. Finally, using Vivado, 
we run synthesis analysis and simulation to verify the designed VHDL project.
16.2   The Kaggle Dataset
In this chapter, we will use the admission dataset from Kaggle to predict the admission chance in a  
university based on various input parameters. The weblink for downloading the admission dataset 
is as follows. 
https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/ks2001/admission-dataset
The overview of the admission dataset in Excel software is shown in Figure 16.1.

Xfeatures = d(:,2:end-1);
Y = d(:,end);
X = [ones(size(Y,1),1) Xfeatures];
AllData = [X Y];
       
cv = cvpartition(size(AllData,1),'HoldOut',0.2);
Index = cv.test;
 
Test_Data = AllData(Index,:);
Train_Data = AllData(~Index,:);
 
XTrain = Train_Data(:,1:end-1);
YTrain = Train_Data(:,end);
 
XTest = Test_Data(:,1:end-1);
YTest = Test_Data(:,end);
 
% Linear Regression
Xt = XTrain';
Th = (pinv(Xt*XTrain))*Xt*YTrain;
 
ypr = (Th')*XTest(1:end,:)';
ypr = ypr';
% Calculate MSE
disp('MSE Linear Regression')
MSE = immse(YTest, ypr)
 
NTe = length(ypr);
n = 0:1:(NTe-1);
% Plot graph
figure
scatter(n,YTest);
grid on;
hold on
plot(n, ypr);
legend('Testing Data','Predicted Data')
ylabel('Chance of Admit')
title('Linear Regression')

The MATLAB function (Convert_to_Fixed_Point.m) is defined by the following codes.
function xf = Convert_to_Fixed_Point(xin,hstep)
% Converting double type array into fixed type array
% hstep is optional parameter
Xmax = max(xin);
Xmin = min(xin);
IP_max = fix(Xmax);
IP_min = fix(Xmin);
fractional_parts = abs(xin - floor(xin));
non_zero_fractional_parts = fractional_parts(fractional_parts > 0);
FP_min = min(non_zero_fractional_parts);
if ((IP_max > 0) && (IP_min < 0))
    WL_int = ceil(log2((abs(IP_max) + abs(IP_min)) + 1));
elseif ((IP_max > 0) && (IP_min >= 0))
    IP_min = 0;
    WL_int = ceil(log2((abs(IP_max) + abs(IP_min)) + 1));
else
    IP_max = 0;
    WL_int = ceil(log2((abs(IP_max) + abs(IP_min)) + 1));
end
 
if nargin < 2
    hfactor = 10;
    if isempty(FP_min)
        FL = 0;
    else
        FL = ceil(log2(1/abs(FP_min/hfactor)));
    end
else
    FL = ceil(log2(1/abs(hstep)));
end
WL = WL_int + FL + 1; % 1 extra bit is for sign
s = 1;
xf = fi(xin,s,WL,FL);
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

16.3 Machine Learning Regression Model for Admission Dataset
 315
In linear regression, we assume that the output variable  y   (a scalar) can be described as a 
combination of the  n   input variables   x   1   ,     x   2   ,…  ,   x   n    .
 y  =  θ  0    +  θ  1    x   1    +  θ  2    x   2    + ⋯  +     θ  n    x   n     (16.1)
where we have the following expressions.
	 1.	 The coefficients   θ  0   ,     θ  1   ,…  ,   θ  n     are the parameters of the model.
	 2.	 The   θ  0     is the intercept (offset or bias) term.
The linear regression model described in Equation (16.1) can also be expressed in vector notation. 
Given the parameter vector  θ =    [    θ  0        θ  1   …  θ  n    ]      T    and the input vector  x   with an extra element 1 in its first 
position, Equation (16.1) can be rewritten as follows.
 y  =  [  θ  0      θ  1   …   θ  n   ]    
[
 
1
   x   1     …   
 x   n   
  
]
   =  θ   T  x   (16.2)
In order to find  θ , we use the following equation:
   ˆ θ   =  arg  θ  min      1  _  n      ∑  
i =1
  
n
  L (     ˆ y     (  x   i   ;  θ)   ,  y   i   )  (16.3)
The cost function  (J (θ))  is defined as follows:
 J  ( θ)    =   1  _  n      ∑  
i =1
  
n
  L (  ˆ y     (  x   i   ;  θ)   ,  y   i   )  (16.4)
Also, the squared error loss function is given by:
 L  (  ˆ y    (  x   i   ;  θ)   , y   i   )   =  (  ˆ y    (  x   i   ;  θ)    − y )  2    (16.5)
Using linear algebra, the solution to this problem is to determine    ˆ θ    using the following equation:
   ˆ θ   =  ( X    T  X )  −1   X    T  y   (16.6)
In summary, the linear regression method is described as follows.
Learning of linear regression is done with squared error loss as follows:
Input: Training data  τ =  { x   i   , y   i   } i =1   N    
Output: Learned parameter vector    ˆ θ    (computed by solving Equation (16.6))
Prediction with linear regression is done as follows:
Input: Learned parameter vector    ˆ θ    and test input   x    *  
Output: Prediction of    ˆ y   ( x    * )  using the following equation:
   ˆ y    ( x    * )   =    ˆ θ    
T
  x    *   (16.7)
When we run the MATLAB m-file RegModel.m, we can view the histogram plots of GRE Score, 
TOFEL Score, University Rating, SOP , LOR, CGPA, Research, and Chances of Admission. Also, the 
linear regression plot of testing and predicted data for Chance of Admit is depicted in Figure 16.2.

16.5 Using Generated VHDL Code in Vivado
 325
We can use the bin2float function to get the float value of a binary number, as shown in the  
following codes.
function out = bin2float(bin)
 
ind = find(bin == '.');
decpart = bin(1:ind-1);
floatpart = bin(ind+1:end);
 
out = bin2dec(decpart);
for n = 1:length(floatpart)
    if floatpart(n) == '1'
        out = out + 2^(-n);
    end
end    
So, if we enter the following code in the MATLAB command window, we get the following result.
>> bin2float('0.1111000100001111')
ans =
    0.9416

17.1   Introduction
In this section, we are going to implement a machine learning binary classification model on 
FPGA using Simulink and Vivado. First, we download a dataset from Kaggle that is suitable for 
the implementation of binary classification. Using MATLAB, we train the binary classification 
model by minimizing the negative log-likelihood cost function. We use the customer satisfac-
tion dataset to predict customer satisfaction based on various input parameters. Then, we imple -
ment the trained machine learning model of binary classification using HDL Coder and generate  
synthesizable VHDL code. Finally, using Vivado, we run synthesis analysis and simulation to 
verify the designed VHDL project.
17.2   The Kaggle Dataset
In this chapter, we will use the customer satisfaction dataset from Kaggle to predict customer sat -
isfaction based on several input parameters. The weblink for downloading the dataset is as follows.
https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/raminhuseyn/airline-customer-satisfaction
The overview of part of customer satisfaction dataset in the Excel software is shown in Figure 17.1.
17.3   The Binary Classification (Logistic Regression)
In this section, we describe the binary classification or logistic regression, and our aim is to learn 
function  g   (   x  )      parameterized by  θ , which predicts the binary class.
 z  =   θ  0    +   θ  1    x   1    +   θ  2    x   2    +  ⋯  +      θ  n    x   n    =   𝜽   T  x  (17.1)
The  z   takes values on the entire real line, but we need a function that returns a value in the 
interval [0, 1]. The logistic function is defined by the following equation.
 h  ( z )     =      1  _  1  +   e    − z     =     e    z   _  1  +   e    z      or g  ( x )    =    1  _______________  
1  +   e    −  θ   T  x  
   =     e     θ   T  x   ______________  
1  +   e     θ   T  x  
    (17.2)
The following codes are used to plot  h   (   z  )      with respect to  z   in the interval [−10, 10], and the 
resulted waveform is depicted in Figure 17.2.
17
Machine Learning Binary Classification Model on FPGA

See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

z = -10:0.1:10;
hz = zeros(1,length(z));
for i = 1:length(z)
   hz = 1./(1+exp(-z));
end
plot(z,hz,'LineWidth',2);
xlabel('z');
ylabel('h(z)')
The class prediction or binary classification is defined by the following equation.
   ˆ y    ( x,*)    =   {  1 if  g  (    ( x,*)    >  r     
0 elsewhere
     (17.3)
The decision threshold is  r  =  0.5  .
The parameter vector  𝜽  is found by the following equation.
 𝜽 =  min  J (θ)  (17.4)
The cost function (negative log-likelihood cost) in logistic regression is given by the following 
equation:
 J  ( θ)    =  −    1  _  n    [  ∑  
i = 1
  
n
   y    (i ) ln  ( h   θ   ( x     ( i )    )  )   +  (1  −   y     ( i )    )ln  (1  −   h   θ   ( x     ( i )    )  )   ]    (17.5)

17.4 Machine Learning Logistic Regression Model for Dataset
 329
The gradient of the cost function is a vector of the same length as  𝜽 , given by the following 
equation:
   ∂ J (θ) ________________  ∂ θ  j      =    1  _  n      ∑  
i = 1
  
n
     (   h   θ    (   x     (   i  )     )    −   y     (   i  )     )    x   j   (i )   (17.6)
Also, the logistic regression function is defined by the following equation:
  h   θ   ( x     ( i )    )   =  g  ( θ   T  x )   =    1  _______________  
1  +   e    −  θ   T  x  
    (17.7)
In summary, the logistic regression method is described as follows.
Learning of binary regression is done as follows.
Input: Training data  τ =   { x   i   , y   i   } i = 1   N     (with output classes  y  =    { 0,1 }) 
Output: Learned parameter vector  𝜽  (computed numerically by Equation (17.4))
Prediction with binary logistic regression is done as follows.
Input: Learned parameter vector   𝜽 , and test input  x * 
Output: Prediction of    ˆ y   (x*)  by the following equation.
Compute  g  ( x,*)    , by the following equation.
 g  ( x,*)    =     e    𝜽Tx *  _______________  
1  +   e     𝜽   T  x* 
    (17.8)
If  g  ( x,*)    >  0.5  , return    ˆ y    ( x,*)    =  1  ; otherwise, return    ˆ y    ( x,*)    =  0  .

classification model for customer satisfaction dataset.

% Discrete Numeric Data
NumD_Data = t(:,Num_Disc_Var);
NumD_Arr = table2array(NumD_Data);
NumD_str = string(NumD_Arr);
NumD_cell = cellstr(NumD_str);
% Continuous Numeric Data
NumC_Data = t(:,Num_Cont_Var);

% Text_Count = Text_Count(1:100,:);
% NumD_Arr = NumD_Arr(1:100,:);
% NumC_Arr = NumC_Arr(1:100,:);
 
All_Data = [Text_Count NumD_Arr NumC_Arr];
rnan = all(~isnan(All_Data),2);
All_Data = All_Data(rnan,:);
 
% Matrix presentation of entire data
Xfeatures = All_Data(:,2:end);
Yout = All_Data(:,1);
Xf = [ones(size(Yout,1),1) Xfeatures];
 
%Splitting data into training and testing
P = 0.8;
Pn = 1-P;
XTrain = Xf(1:round(P*length(Xf)),:);
YTrain = Yout(1:round(P*length(Xf)),:);
 
XTest = Xf(end-round(Pn*length(Xf)):end,:);
YTest = Yout(end-round(Pn*length(Xf)):end,:);
 
%Implementation of logistic regression for classification
[Rows, Columns] = size(Xfeatures);
Theta_initial = zeros(Columns + 1, 1);
 
%Initial value of cost function
[Ji, Gradi] = Cost_Function(Theta_initial, XTrain, YTrain);
options = optimoptions(@fminunc,'Display','iter','Algorithm','Quasi- 
Newton','GradObj','on','MaxIter',500);
[Theta, cost] = fminunc(@(t)(Cost_Function(t, XTrain, YTrain)),Theta_ 
initial,options);
 
%Final value of cost function
[Jf, Gradf] = Cost_Function(Theta_initial, XTrain, YTrain);

First of all, we plot the histogram of different columns in the customer satisfaction dataset. We 
have also dedicated 80% of the data for training and 20% for testing. The codes for the function 
“Cost_Function” is shown as follows.
function [J, Grad] = Cost_Function(Theta, X, y)
n = length(y);
z = X * Theta;
Grad = zeros(size(Theta));
h = 1 ./ (1 + exp(-z));
J = -(1/n)*sum((y.*log(h))+((1-y).*log(1-h)));
for j=1:size(Theta,1)
    Grad(j)=(1/n)*sum((h-y).*X(:,j));
end
end
Also, the same function “Convert_to_Fixed_Point” from the previous chapter is used here as 
well. The predicted and actual histograms for customer satisfaction are illustrated in Figure 17.3. 
Furthermore, the predicted and actual plots for customer satisfaction are shown in Figure 17.4.
The accuracy of logistic regression method is displayed in MATLAB command window as follows.

using 4 bits (1 bit for the sign). We use the command Thf = fi(Theta,1,16,12), where the word 
length is 16 bits and the fraction length is 12 bits. To evaluate each element of Theta to at least 32 
significant digits, we use the command vpa(Theta), which provides variable-precision arithmetic 
for floating-point numbers. The fixed-point values of  𝜽  are shown as follows.

MATLAB function “WeightedSum” with 5 bits for integer part (1 bit for sign) and 11 bits for 
fractional part is suitable. The MATLAB function “Sigmoid” codes are as follows.
function hz = Sigmoid(z)
zd = double(z);
hz = 1/(1+exp(-zd));

function y = fcn(u)
if u > 0.5
    y = fi(1,0,1,0);
else
    y = fi(0,0,1,0);
end

Another solution is to use the piecewise linear approximation of the sigmoid function as a 
MATLAB function using the following codes.
function y = fcn(u)
u_in = fi(u,1,16,11);
if u_in < -3
    y = fi(0,1,16,11);
elseif (u_in >= -3 && u_in < -2)
    y = fi(0.072*u_in + 0.22,1,16,11);
elseif (u_in >= -2 && u_in < 2)
    y = fi(0.21*u_in + 0.5,1,16,11);
elseif (u_in >= 2 && u_in < 3)
    y = fi(0.072*u_in + 0.78,1,16,11);
else
    y = fi(1,1,16,11);
end
Since the HDL Coder cannot generate code for the signal from workspace block of “Thf” , we use 
22 constant blocks and a multiplexer instead, as shown in Figure 17.15.

Finish button to create the “Binary_Classification” project with added generated VHDL files.

thesis is successfully completed, and we can click the Cancel button. Then, we open the schematic 
in the RTL Analysis tab, as shown in Figure 17.25.

code in the MATLAB command window.
>> XTest(100,:)

18.1   Introduction
In this section, we are going to deploy a deep neural network (DNN) model on an FPGA using 
Simulink and Vivado. First of all, we download a dataset from Kaggle that is suitable for deploying 
a DNN, and using MATLAB, we train the model based on minimizing the cost function. We use 
the diabetes dataset to predict whether the patient is diabetic or not based on various input param-
eters. Then, we implement the trained DNN model using HDL Coder and generate synthesizable 
VHDL code. Finally, using Vivado, we run synthesis analysis and simulation to verify the designed 
VHDL project.
18.2   The Kaggle Dataset
In this chapter, we will use the diabetes dataset from Kaggle to predict whether the patient is dia-
betic or not based on various input parameters. The weblink for downloading the diabetes dataset 
is as follows: 
https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/mathchi/diabetes-data-set
An overview of the diabetes dataset in Excel is shown in Figure 18.1.
18.3   Introduction to Neural Networks
A neural network is a computational model inspired by the structure and function of the human 
brain. It is a fundamental component of deep learning, a subfield of artificial intelligence (AI). The 
neural networks are designed to recognize patterns and make intelligent decisions, much like the 
human brain does. Here are some key concepts and components of neural networks:
	 1.	Neuron: The basic building blocks of a neural network are artificial neurons, also known as 
nodes or units. These neurons receive inputs, perform computations, and produce outputs.
	 2.	Layers: Neurons are organized into layers within a neural network. There are typically three 
types of layers:
	 a.	 Input Layer: This layer receives the initial data or features that are fed into the network. 
Each neuron in the input layer represents a feature or input variable.
18
Deploying a Deep Neural Network on FPGA
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 371 ---
356 18 Deploying a Deep Neural Network on FPGA
	 b.	 Hidden Layers: These layers lie between the input and output layers and are responsible for 
processing and learning patterns in the data. DNNs have multiple hidden layers, hence the 
term “deep learning.”
	 c.	 Output Layer: The final layer of neurons produces the network’s output, which could be a 
classification, regression, or some other task-specific result.
	 3.	Weights and Biases: Each connection between neurons has associated weights and biases. 
These parameters are learned during training and determine the strength of the connections 
between neurons.
	 4.	Activation Function: An activation function is applied to the weighted sum of inputs and 
biases in each neuron. It introduces nonlinearity into the network, allowing it to learn com-
plex relationships in the data. Common activation functions include sigmoid, ReLU (rectified 
linear unit), and tanh (hyperbolic tangent).
	 5.	Feedforward Propagation: The process of passing data through the network from the input 
layer to the output layer is called feedforward propagation. Neurons in each layer compute 
their activations based on inputs and weights, and this process continues through the layers 
until the final output is generated.
	 6.	Backpropagation: To train a neural network, you need a labeled dataset and a loss function 
that measures the difference between the predicted output and the true target values. 
Backpropagation is an algorithm used to update the weights and biases in the network to min-
imize this loss. It works by propagating the error from the output layer backward through the 
hidden layers, adjusting the parameters along the way.
	 7.	Training: Training a neural network involves iteratively presenting the training data to the net -
work, computing the loss, and using backpropagation to update the model’s parameters. This 
process continues until the model’s performance converges to an acceptable level.
Neural networks can be used for a wide range of tasks, including image and speech recognition, 
natural language processing, recommendation systems, and many others. They have demonstrated 
impressive performance in various fields and have contributed to significant advancements in AI 
and machine learning. A typical DNN is shown in Figure 18.2.
The mathematical model of a neuron is depicted in Figure 18.3.
In the term   w   ki    ,   k   represents the layer number and  i   represents the instance number. We also 
have the following equation.
 u  =   ∑

sum  u   is calculated, the neuron’s output can be computed by passing  u   to the activation function, 
as demonstrated in the following equation.
  y   k    = φ(u )  (18.4)
18.4   Piecewise Linear Approximation of Activation Functions
The sigmoid, tanh, and ReLU are common activation functions used in artificial neural networks. 
Each of these functions introduces nonlinearity into the network’s computations, allowing it to 
learn complex relationships in the data.
	 1.	Sigmoid Activation Function: The sigmoid function is defined by the following equation:

x = -5:0.1:5;

The function “sigmoid” is defined by the following codes.
function y = sigmoid(u)
if u < -3
    y = 0;
elseif (u >= -3 && u < -2)
    y = 0.072*u + 0.22;
elseif (u >= -2 && u < 2)
    y = 0.21*u + 0.5;
elseif (u >= 2 && u < 3)
    y = 0.072*u + 0.78;
else
    y = 1;
end
The actual and piecewise linear approximation plots for sigmoid function are illustrated in 
Figure 18.4.

If we type p2, p3, and p4 in the MATLAB command window and press the enter button, we get 
the following results.
>> p2
p2 =
    0.0713    0.2571
>> p3
p3 =
    0.2094    0.5000
>> p4
p4 =
    0.0713    0.7429
Here, the first element represents the slope, and the second element represents the offset of  
the line equation. We have 0.0713 ≈ 0.072, 0.2571 ≈ 0.22, 0.2094 ≈ 0.21, and 0.7429 ≈ 0.78 in 
Equation (18.6). Now, to implement the piecewise linear approximation of the sigmoid function in 
HDL Coder, we use the following m-file (sig_approx2.m).
clear all; close all; clc
dx = 0.01;
f = @(x) 1./(1 + exp(-x));
x = -5:dx:5;
 
for k = 1:length(x)
    y(k) = sig_pw_HDL(x(k));
end
plot(x,y,'LineWidth',2);
grid on;
hold on;
 
ya = f(x);
plot(x,ya,'LineWidth',2);
ylim([-0.1 1.1]);
xlabel('x');
ylabel('y');
legend('Approx','Actual');
title('Piecewise Linear Approximation of Sigmoid');
Here, the function sig_pw_HDL is defined by the following codes.
function ysig = sig_pw_HDL(x)
WL = 16;
FL = 10;
 
F = hdlfimath();
nt = numerictype(1,WL,FL);
 
xf = fi(x,nt);
 
m1 = fi(0.072,nt);
c1 = fi(0.22,nt);
 
m2 = fi(0.21,nt);
c2 = fi(0.5,nt);
 
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License


--- Page 379 ---
364 18 Deploying a Deep Neural Network on FPGA
m3 = fi(0.072,nt);
c3 = fi(0.78,nt);
 
if xf < fi(-3,nt)
    y = fi(0,nt,F);
elseif xf < fi(-2,nt)
    y = fi(m1*x+c1,nt,F);
elseif ((xf >= fi(-2,nt)) && (xf <= fi(2,nt)))
    y = fi(m2*x+c2,nt,F);
elseif xf < fi(3,nt)
    y = fi(m3*x+c3,nt,F);
else
    y = fi(1,nt,F);
end
 
if y <= fi(0,nt)
    ysig = fi(0,nt,F);
elseif y >= fi(1,nt)
    ysig = fi(1,nt,F);
else
    ysig = fi(y,nt,F);
end
If we run the m-file (sig_approx2.m), we get the plot as illustrated in Figure 18.8, which is exactly 
the same as the plot in Figure 18.4.

The same process is used for piecewise linear approximation of tanh function. The following 
MTLAB m-file (tanh_approx1.m) is used to obtain the piecewise linear approximation of tanh 
function.
clear all; close all; clc
degree = 1;
dx = 0.1;
f = @(x) (exp(x) - exp(-x))./(exp(x) + exp(-x));
x = -5:dx:5;
y =f(x);
plot(x,y);
grid on;
xlabel('x');
 
x2 = -2:dx:-1;
y2 = f(x2);
p2 = polyfit(x2, y2, degree);
 
x3 = -1:dx:1;
y3 = f(x3);
p3 = polyfit(x3, y3, degree);
 
x4 = 1:dx:2;
y4 = f(x4);
p4 = polyfit(x4, y4, degree);
 
zf2 = polyval(p2,x2);
zf3 = polyval(p3,x3);
zf4 = polyval(p4,x4);
 
x1 = -10:dx:-2;
zf1 = -1*ones(1,length(x1));
 
x5 = 2:dx:10;
zf5 = ones(1,length(x5));
 
x = -10:dx:10;
ya = f(x);
 
plot(x1,zf1,'LineWidth',2);
hold on;
plot(x2,zf2,'LineWidth',2);
plot(x3,zf3,'LineWidth',2);
plot(x4,zf4,'LineWidth',2);
plot(x5,zf5,'LineWidth',2);
 
plot(x,ya,'k','LineWidth',1);
grid on;
legend('y1','y2','y3','y4','y5','tanh');
xlabel('x');
ylabel('y');

Here, the function tanh_pw_HDL is defined by the following codes.
function ysig = tanh_pw_HDL(x)
WL = 16;
FL = 10;
 
F = hdlfimath();
nt = numerictype(1,WL,FL);
 
xf = fi(x,nt);
 
m1 = fi(0.18,nt);
c1 = fi(-0.64,nt);
 
m2 = fi(0.82,nt);
c2 = fi(0,nt);
 
m3 = fi(0.18,nt);
c3 = fi(0.64,nt);
 
if xf < fi(-2,nt)
    y = fi(-1,nt,F);
elseif xf < fi(-1,nt)
    y = fi(m1*x+c1,nt,F);
elseif ((xf >= fi(-1,nt)) && (xf < fi(1,nt)))
    y = fi(m2*x+c2,nt,F);
elseif xf < fi(2,nt)
    y = fi(m3*x+c3,nt,F);
else
    y = fi(1,nt,F);
end
 
if y <= fi(-1,nt)
    ysig = fi(-1,nt,F);
elseif y >= fi(1,nt)
    ysig = fi(1,nt,F);
else
    ysig = fi(y,nt,F);
end
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

 369
%Training and Testing Data
Prob = 0.8;
NTr = round(Prob*Len);
XTrain = X(:,1:NTr);
YTrain = Y(:,1:NTr);
 
XTest = X(:,NTr+1:end);
YTest = Y(:,NTr+1:end);
 
XTest = XTest'; % Features Along Column
YTest = YTest';
 
%% Set Neural Network Parameters
% Define sizes
[R,C] = size(XTrain);
inputSize = R;
hiddenLayerSize1 = 3;
hiddenLayerSize2 = round(R/2);
 
% Define Neural Network
net = feedforwardnet([hiddenLayerSize1,hiddenLayerSize2]);
 
% Set Training Parameters
net.trainParam.epochs = 1000;
net.trainParam.goal = 1e-6;
net.trainParam.max_fail = 200;
 
% Specify the logsig transfer function for each layer
ActFun_Layaer1 = 'tansig'; %tanh
ActFun_Layaer2 = 'tansig'; %tanh
OutputLayer = 'logsig'; %sigmoid
 
net.layers{1}.transferFcn = ActFun_Layaer1; % 1st hidden layer
net.layers{2}.transferFcn = ActFun_Layaer2; % 2nd hidden layer
net.layers{3}.transferFcn = OutputLayer; % Output layer
 
% Train the Neural Network
size(XTrain)
size(YTrain)
 
net = train(net,XTrain,YTrain);
 
% Generate Simulink Model of Trained Neural Network
gensim(net);
 
%% Extract Weights and Biases
W1d = net.IW{1,1};  % Input Layer
b1d = net.b{1};
 
W2d = net.LW{2,1};  % Hidden Layer 1
b2d = net.b{2};

If we uncomment and run the m-file (Train_NN_Model.m), the histogram plots of the diabetes 
dataset will be displayed. Also, we have used 80% of the data for training and 20% for testing. The 
neural network consists of an input layer with the size equal to the number of input features (eight 
neurons), hidden layer 1 (layer 1) with 3 neurons, hidden layer 2 (layer 2) with four neurons, and 
an output layer (layer 3) with one neuron. The activation function for layer 1 and layer 2 is tanh 
(‘tansig’); however, for the output layer (layer 3), the activation function is sigmoid (‘logsig’). Also, 
we save the trained neural network parameters (weights and biases) in the file “Trained_NN_
Model.mat.” Now, we can run the MATLAB m-file (Train_NN_Model.m) and view the neural net -
work training window, as shown in Figure 18.11.
Also, the Simulink model of the feedforward neural network is generated, as depicted in 
Figure 18.12.
If we type W1d, b1d, W2d, b2d, and W3d, b3d in the MATLAB command window, we get their 
values (W1d = 3 ×8, b1d = 3 ×1, W2d = 4 ×3, b2d = 4 ×1, and W3d = 1 ×4, b3d = 1 ×1). Also, if we 
write “>> view(net)” in the MATLAB command window, we can see our designed neural network 
as illustrated in Figure 18.13.

cannot generate code for the feedforward neural network block in Figure 18.21, and it is used 
only for simulation purposes. So, we should build our DNN manually. We define the function 
“NN_Layer_Tanh_HDL” by the following codes.
function ytan = NN_Layer_Tanh_HDL(Bias,Weight,X)
% This function implements NN_Layer using tanh function
% It is HDL supported, and can be used in HDL code generation
% Weight = they should be arranged as row vector
% X = Instances of input features should be arranged as column vector
% Bias = It can be row or column vector
% y = It is output vector arranged as column vector containing outputs 
% From all neurons of given layer
% Note: All inputs of this function should be in fixed point format
 
Nodes = size(Weight,1);
WL = 16;
FL = 10;
PM = fi(zeros(Nodes,1),1,WL,FL);
WS = fi(zeros(Nodes,1),1,WL,FL);
ytan = fi(zeros(Nodes,1),1,WL,14);
 
for k = 1:Nodes
    PM(k) = Weight(k,:)*X(:,1);
    WS(k) = PM(k) + Bias(k);
Figure 18.22   The waveform of y2 on the scope.

    y = tanh_pw_HDL(WS(k));
    ytan(k) = y;

The MATLAB function (Hidden_Layer1) is shown in Figure 18.23.
The codes for the function “Convert_Data_to_Fixed_Point” in MATLAB m-file (Train_NN_
Model.m) are as follows.
function XTest_FixedPoint = Convert_Data_to_Fixed_Point(XFeatures, XTest) 
% This function converts double type XTest (Test Data) into fixed point by
% considering the maximum and minimum values of XFeatures matrix along with
% fractional part.
% It is assumed that XTest is submatrix of XFeatures.
% Convert matrices to fixed-point representation based on statistics from
% XFeatures.
 
% 1. Find maximum and minimum values across all elements of XFeatures
max_val = max(XFeatures(:));
min_val = min(XFeatures(:));
 
% 2. Find maximum and minimum fractional values greather than zero across all  
elements of XFeatures
fractional_values = abs(XFeatures - floor(XFeatures)); % Fractional parts of all  
elements of XFeatures
fractional_values_non_zero = fractional_values(fractional_values > 0); %  
Filter non-zero fractional parts of all elements of XFeatures
if isempty(fractional_values_non_zero) % If XFeatures has only positive integers
    FL = 0;
    max_fractional = 0;
    min_fractional = 0;
else
    max_fractional = max(fractional_values_non_zero);
    min_fractional = min(fractional_values_non_zero);
    % To determine FL, consider the smallest non-zero fractional value and
    % ensure 
    FL = 1+ceil(-log2(min_fractional)); % 1 is added (manually)
end
    
Figure 18.23   The MATLAB function (Hidden_Layer1).
 Downloaded from https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/ by alex jounh - Oregon Health & Science University , Wiley Online Library on [22/10/2025]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License

18.5 Training the Neural Network Model
 379
% 3. Calculate WL
% WL = number of bits of the integer part of max value + 1 bit for sign +
% FL
WL = 1 + ceil(log2(max(abs([max_val, min_val])))) + FL;
 
% 4. Convert XTest to fixed-point using WL and FL
XTest_FixedPoint = fi(XTest, 1, WL, FL);
 
% Optional: Display the results (You can comment this out if not needed)
fprintf('Max value: %f\n', max_val);
fprintf('Min value: %f\n', min_val);
fprintf('Max fractional (non-zero): %f\n', max_fractional);
fprintf('Min fractional (non-zero): %f\n', min_fractional);
fprintf('\n------------- Data (Testing) Conversion');
fprintf('\nWord Length (WL): %d bits\n', WL);
fprintf('\nFraction Length (WL): %d bits\n\n\n', FL);
end
Also, the codes for the function “Convert_Parameters_to_Fixed_Point” in MATLAB m-file 
(Train_NN_Model.m) are as follows.
function [W1, b1, W2, b2, W3, b3] = Convert_Parameters_to_Fixed_Point()
 
data = load('Trained_NN_Model.mat');
W1d = data.W1d;
b1d = data.b1d;
W2d = data.W2d;
b2d = data.b2d;
W3d = data.W3d;
b3d = data.b3d;
 
maxW1d_val = max(W1d(:));
minW1d_val = min(W1d(:));
 
fractional_W1dvalues = abs(W1d - floor(W1d)); 
fractional_W1dvalues_non_zero = fractional_W1dvalues(fractional_W1dvalues > 0); 
if isempty(fractional_W1dvalues_non_zero)
    FLW1d = 0;
else
    minW1d_fractional = min(fractional_W1dvalues_non_zero);
    FLW1d = 1+ceil(-log2(minW1d_fractional)); 
end
    
WLW1d = 1 + ceil(log2(max(abs([maxW1d_val, minW1d_val])))) + FLW1d;
W1 = fi(W1d, 1, WLW1d, FLW1d);
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
maxb1d_val = max(b1d(:));
minb1d_val = min(b1d(:));
 
fractional_b1dvalues = abs(b1d - floor(b1d)); 
fractional_b1dvalues_non_zero = fractional_b1dvalues(fractional_b1dvalues > 0);

    FLb1d = 1+ceil(-log2(minb1d_fractional));

WLb1d = 1 + ceil(log2(max(abs([maxb1d_val, minb1d_val])))) + FLb1d;

    FLb2d = 0;
else
    minb2d_fractional = min(fractional_b2dvalues_non_zero);
    FLb2d = 1+ceil(-log2(minb2d_fractional)); 
end
    
WLb2d = 1 + ceil(log2(max(abs([maxb2d_val, minb2d_val])))) + FLb2d;
b2 = fi(b2d, 1, WLb2d, FLb2d);
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
maxW3d_val = max(W3d(:));
minW3d_val = min(W3d(:));
 
fractional_W3dvalues = abs(W3d - floor(W3d)); 
fractional_W3dvalues_non_zero = fractional_W3dvalues(fractional_W3dvalues > 0); 
if isempty(fractional_W3dvalues_non_zero)
    FLW3d = 0;
else
    minW3d_fractional = min(fractional_W3dvalues_non_zero);
    FLW3d = 1+ceil(-log2(minW3d_fractional)); 
end
    
WLW3d = 1 + ceil(log2(max(abs([maxW3d_val, minW3d_val])))) + FLW3d;

% from all neurons of given layer
% Note: All inputs of this function should be in fixed point format
 
Nodes = size(Weight,1);
WL = 16;
FL = 10;
PM = fi(zeros(Nodes,1),1,WL,FL);
WS = fi(zeros(Nodes,1),1,WL,FL);
ysig = fi(zeros(Nodes,1),1,WL,14);
 
for k = 1:Nodes
    PM(k) = Weight(k,:)*X(:,1);
    WS(k) = PM(k) + Bias(k);  %u
    y = sig_pw_HDL(WS(k));    %y = sigmoid(u)
    ysig(k) = y;
end
The MATLAB function “BinaryPrediction” in Figure 18.24 is defined by the following codes.
function yprd = Binary_Prediction(u,Thresh)
% Call Function
yprd = Binary_Prediction(u,Thresh);
The codes for the function “Binary_Prediction” are as follows.
function yprd = Binary_Prediction(u,Thresh)
if u > Thresh
    yprd = fi(1,0,1,0);
else
    yprd = fi(0,0,1,0);
end
